CADEMIAE CHRISTO ANONYMOUS GIFT

SPORTING LADY By GENE GAUNTIER Author of 

"Cabbages and Harlequins" THIS

HIS novel constitutes at once a fascinating story of a woman's rise to wealth and dominance in a community from which she had been driven as a girl, and an amazing chapter in mid-Western American life.

Ambitious and regally beautiful, Cora Simmons becomes infatuated with a dashing member of Jesse 

James' infamous gang and runs away from her unsympathetic family in 

Clay County, Kansas. Her lover's precarious existence cut short, and Cora herself sick and wretched, she is befriended by the Madam of a sporting house. From this point her unique rise in her chosen profession as well as to power in political lifeamid the gaudy glamour of gambling, horse-racing, sporting days-unfolds as an enthralling chronicle of personal triumph.

This is perhaps the first authentic study in American fiction of a subject heretofore considered taboo. Without sentimentalism or lushness, Miss

Gauntier brilliantly recreates an era, and a personality easily recognizable as an eternal type.

CHAPTER ONE 

SLE 1 LEET, which had been threatening all day, broke suddenly in a fierce tattoo upon the roof of the kitchen. The elder of the two girls listened for a moment, then crossed to the outer door and throwing it open stepped onto the porch. Wind, sweeping in, fanned the light of the kerosene lamp. It flickered and went out. "Shut the door, Cora," complained her sister. "Now you've blown out the lamp. Come on in and light it. 

The chimney burns my fingers. What do you want to go out for, anyways? Your lookin' for 'em ain't goin' to bring 'em any sooner. For the life of me I can't see why you don't sit down and stay quiet like me.

Rarin' up and down like a wild animal don't do any good. They'll be here when they get here and not before. Hurry up! It's gettin' cold." "They make me mad,” muttered the other, slamming the door. "You'd think I didn't have a thing to do but wait for 'em and keep their supper hot." "Well, have you?" "None of your sauce now. A lot it worries you whether they have anything to eat or not, s'long as <7»

you can stitch on those darned splashers. Here," she approached the table with a blazing splinter of wood, "shove over the lamp. I suppose it doesn't matter whether it burns my fingers or not." "Don't be so cross, Cora. You know when Paw goes hunting we never know when to expect him back. 

There, ain't that pretty?" holding up the oblong of unbleached cotton on which figures of embracing lovers were outlined in turkey red. "It's pretty enough. But what are you going to do with it? That's the fourth one you've done and I don't see any of 'em decoratin' any around here."

Flora glanced slyly at her tall frowning sister before bending her bright head over the needle she was threading. "No, and you won't. I'm keepin' 'em for my own home. When I get married." Cora laughed shortly as she bent over and opened the stove door, thrusting in sticks of wood. The flames lighted up her rather handsome face with its tawny eyes and low forehead, from which the thick dark hair sprang backward as though alive. Pettishly she banged the door and stood at her full height. Six feet of it. A splendid figure of a woman, deep bosomed, full throated, long legged. With arms akimbo she surveyed her fragile blonde sister.

Flora stared at her a moment, meditatively. "Don't you ever think of gettin' married, Cora? 

Havin' a home of your own and children, and bein' your own boss? You're nineteen now-three years older'n me. When I'm as old as you- And you know, you are goodlookin', even if you are so big." "I'm never goin' to get married. I remember the life Maw led too well to ever pattern one like it for myself. Cookin' and washin' and scrubbin' and milkin'

SPORTING LADY 

<9» and hoein' and plantin' and havin' children—and buryin' 'em. And never a decent dress to her back or a kind word. Nothin' but work from sunup to sundown until she just wore out. She was glad to die. She told me so. 'No, I ain't afraid,' she says, 'all I want's rest.' Well,

I hope she's got it, for I haven't had any since she went. 

But I'm not goin' on like this much longer, I can tell."

The deep voice became shrill and the yellow eyes blazed. "I'm goin' to have some excitement in my life. 

I'm goin' to travel and wear fine clothes and live among crowds and music and see plays and dance. Other women do, why shouldn't I? I've got health and looks and ambition. That's enough to start me. And I know what I'm goin' after. It's money. And I'm goin' to get it, somehow. And when I've got that the other things'll come. I've made up my mind and there's no man goin' to sidetrack me. It's all in knowin' what you want and goin' after it. Everybody's good for something. Everybody's got a place waitin' for 'em if they only find out what it is. Well, there's something waitin' for me and 

I'm goin' after it. And before long, too." 

Flora pursed her lips primly. "Well, you're right-in a way. But you've got to know just exactly what you do want. I think that way, too, but I know how I'm going to get it, and you don't seem to. First, I'm goin' to marry. And it's not goin' to be one of these Clay County farmers, neither.

It's goin' to be someone from over in town. And I'm goin' to have a nice home with a yard and a hired girl. 

And I'm goin' to go to receptions and belong to a nice church and have my clothes made, and a diamond sunburst and a horse and carriage. I'm goin' to have two children, a boy and a girl, and give 'em music lessons, and the boy is goin' to be a professional man like his

father, and the girl is goin' to marry rich; and maybe some day after the children are out of the way my husband and I will go to Paris and London." "Then our lives are all mapped out and all we got to do is to start. But I won't be waitin' for a husband to come and help me do it. And I reckon you'll be waitin' a long time if you expect a city fellow to come huntin' you out here in Clay County." "Oh, I don't know. There's been quite a number passed here since the Denver Express holdup last month. 

Goin' out to look at the Samuels' farm. D'ya know-I believe Jesse's home again."

Cora replaced the lid on the iron pot and turned excitedly. "Why? Have you seen him?" "No-but when I took the butter to Brother Weed's this mornin' I rode round thataway and saw a lot of extra shirts and things on the line. And I know Doc 

Samuels don't wear all of 'em. I should think he'd be scared to come back so soon. Of course, no one in Clay would inform on him, but you never can tell when a 

Pinkerton will turn up.' 99 "He's certainly brave. But they'll never catch him. 

He's too lucky." "Anyways, I'd be scared to run such risks. Such a life! Never knowin' when he'll have to sneak out the back way-always havin' to keep a horse saddled and ready. It must be an awful strain." "Oh, I don't know," said Cora, dreamily, "it must be kind of fun. Exciting. Never knowin' when you've got to kill an enemy. And just goin' out and holdin' up a train or robbin' a bank and gettin' all the money you need. And then, ridin' away for months at a time, seein' the world and not havin' to worry about money.

« II > And laughin' in his sleeve at all the police and detectives -even talkin' to 'em and them not knowin' he was 

Jesse James at all. Must be lots of fun in it." "How can you talk that way, Cora? And what about his wife? She gets all the worry and none of the fun. 

I can't see how such a nice girl as Zerelda Mimms ever come to marry him. No sir-ree! Give me a little less excitement and a sound place in society. I wouldn't marry an outlaw-even one of the James Boys, even if they are right-in a way—for anything.” "Oh, I don't know. At least there's always somethin' going on. Listen to that sleet! Paw and Ed'll be frozen when they get here. It's nine o'clock." "So it is. Well, I think I'll go to bed. My eyes hurt.

Wrap up an iron for me and take it up to the bed, won't you, Co? While I undress. It's goin' to be cold tonight. I'll bet it's down to 15." 2 The hands of the kitchen clock crept round the hour. 

Outside the January sleet beat ceaselessly against the window panes; inside no human sound disturbed the quiet. A striped cat purred before the glowing cook stove, the clock ticked loudly on its shelf, and Cora sat hunched forward in a splint-bottomed rocking chair, chin buried in red chapped hands, smouldering eyes fixed vacantly on the wide walnut boards of the floor.

She was thinking over the conversation with Flora, and of her own desires for the future, voiced for the first time. Those desires had really sprung into being

« 2 » 

SPORTING LADY 

with her utterance of them. They must have been lying dormant, inarticulate, for a long time. They might have been struggling for recognition during the last two or three years of restlessness, sullen brooding, flashing rebellion; moods that caused the disconcerting hot waves of blood that sometimes flooded her brain, leaving her enervated and listless. Not that Cora reasoned thus. Cora never reasoned much-she only felt. And the reaction to her feelings brought on the moods. "Cranky," her father called her at such times; "Snippy," 

Flora complained; "Moody," her brother Ed thought, and watched her curiously.

Putting her hitherto vague longings into articulate form had eased and comforted Cora. For the first time in her nineteen years of life-long years they had been, measured by the monotonous round of seasons on the small farm-her future assumed definite shape. To get away-to be free-to be forced to obey nothing but her own desires, no one but her individual self. How?

There must be a way. She felt the abundant vitality coursing through her tall, long-limbed body, the reckless feeling of boundless health and rich, demanding blood. It belonged to her, this strong and firmly rounded six feet of bone and flesh and muscle. No one else in all the world knew its secrets, the hidden thoughts of its brain, the whole intricate machinery of its parts. It was all she possessed-but it was enough. With its attraction and vigor and energy she need only summon up sufficient resolution, to go out and win a prize in life, no matter what the struggle. Ruthless she must be, and brave. She would welcome hardships, even danger, if they brought with them the rewards she visualized. 

Cora's mind, wandering into a relating trend, moved on to the little farm some miles north, to the bravest

SPORTING LADY 

« 13 » person she knew, Jesse James. She had known him all her life. Before, as a mere boy, he had joined Bill Anderson's bushwhackers, and, later, Quantrill. Her earliest recollection was of Jesse making her a willow whistle.

She remembered just how he had cut the bark in half, had gently pounded it until it slipped off, had cut out a small wedge-shaped bit, working dexterously, explaining; and how astonished she had been when it was finished and he had blown for her a long, low, liquid note.

The Jesse she knew had been a good boy, if mischievous; and, as boys go, a religious one. He never missed Sunday School, and seldom church, until after the war came. Then, when he was only fifteen, the 

Yanks had hung up his step-father, old Doctor Samuels, and raging for revenge Jesse had gone out to join Bill Anderson's bushwhackers and she hadn't seen him again for years. But she heard about him and his older brother 

Frank, and the younger boys from over near Lee's Summit; gossip passed around among those farmer folks who were friendly to the Confederate cause. 

Through all the years while he had been out with Quantrill's men bushwhacking, burning, and killing, and she had been growing from childhood to girlhood, she had remembered him and his large blinking pale blue eyes and tender womanly mouth; and his gentleness and kindness to her as a child. And, after the war, when the Government had refused immunity to the Boys and the bank robberies began, robberies including wilful murder and which rumor laid to Frank and Jesse, he was still her hero; and she thrilled at each new exploit, each fresh disregard for the laws. For had not their country itself outlawed them? Were not these crimes but protests and reprisals against an unfairness and discrimination which everyone at least all here-

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

abouts-acknowledged? Their country and their laws had turned against brave men, and it was a just revenge that the James Gang were taking. So thought the Clay 

County people, Jesse's and Frank's friends and neighbors; and so thought Cora Simmons along with the others.

Perhaps her hero worship of Jesse might have turned into something warmer had she not known, as had everyone, of his devotion to his cousin Zerelda who, in those years following the end of the war, had nursed back to health his broken and shot-riddled body. Only last spring the lovers had finally married. She herself had seen Jesse that April day when, spick and span in natty clothes, riding a prancing horse across whose saddle bow was slung a Winchester, he had passed through the main street of Kearney on the way to his wedding. And the thronging townfolk, well knowing him for a bank robber and perhaps a murderer, had waved friendly hands and called out congratulations for a long life and prosperity. And had meant it. Jesse knew they had and had laughed and waved back and invited them to "Come and see us when we are settled down." As if he were destined ever to be really settled down!

So Jesse had married, and for months there had been quiet and peace. Perhaps he had got enough as his share of the loot in the recent stage and railroad holdups to fulfill his needs; perhaps he really did have an idea of becoming domesticated. For eight months he was not seen or heard of. Then came the holdup at Muncie, Kansas, only ten miles out of Kansas City. Just six weeks ago. So tongues wagged again. Of course it was the James Boys. What other gang would make so bold

SPORTING LADY 

<15> in their territory? And curious but friendly eyes were watching for some sign at the old homestead, knowing that they generally came home to hide after a big raid. 

But they had not been seen; there was no evidence that they were about, unless the shirts on the line were evidence. Which they well might be.

If Jesse were at home-if only she could talk with him-perhaps he could advise her. He had been out in the big world; he knew about conditions in Kansas 

City, St Joe, even St Louis. He might be able to tell her how to get away. She must manage to see him. Secretive though he was when in hiding, she must find a way to meet him. She drew a deep breath, rose and stretched her arms above her head; opened the door and stepped out, closing it behind her. 3 

Beyond the yellow patch cast by the unshuttered window the night lay black as a badger hole. In that visible spot the ground was covered with ice and the twigs of the lilac bushes heavy under it. Cora strained her ears for sign of the returning hunters, shivered, and was about to re-enter when she caught an alien sound through the slash of the sleet. Two horses, their hoofbeats growing louder as they neared the gate a hundred feet away. They were stopping. She could hear their voices now. Curiosity gave way to a tremor of fear.

There were other bad men ranging the country, remnants of the blood-thirsty gangs who had created terror

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

during the years of the Civil War. Men steeped in blood and still unsated; worse still, men who took their women where they found them. She thought of Flora, frail and helpless, asleep upstairs. Two women alone in this vast darkness of sleet and wind, several miles from the nearest neighbor. Strong and husky though she was, she stood no chance against such invaders. 

She heard them dismounting, heard them fumbling with the bars of the gate; and slipping around the house she entered by the front door and jerked from the nail above it an old musket which hung there, always loaded and ready. There was no time to reach the kitchen door and bar it; already the strangers had knocked and as she stood, musket at shoulder, it opened. "Stay where you are," she commanded. 

For a moment they stared, startled. Then with a smile and sweeping bow the taller one spoke. "There now, sister, don't be frightened. We ain't meanin' any harm. We're sort of lost in the storm and just stopped by to ask directions. Put down that blunderbuss. It might go off."

Slowly Cora obeyed, her eyes fixed on the man's face. 

The large black hat which he held in his hand uncovered a mass of dark curly hair which grew over his ears and low on his neck. The knotted bandana at his throat made the dusky skin appear even darker. A drooping mustache partly concealed full red lips. But it was the brilliant black eyes, half mocking, half smiling, as he gazed down on her, that held her gaze. Mesmeric eyes that made her heart beat fast. For a moment she closed her own, confused by the tumult that swept her. Then he was beside her, had gently taken the gun and laid it aside, had pressed her down into a chair.

SPORTING LADY 

< 17 » "Pardon us, Miss. We hadn't no aim to scare you.

Shorty and I are strangers in these parts. Have ridden all the way from Kaintuck. We're lookin' for the Samuels place. Is it anywheres hereabout?"

For a moment she did not answer, staring at his long lean figure towering above her as she sat. Even under the thickness of his furlined jacket she detected the bulges that could be made only by six shooters. Her eyes travelled to Shorty's rotund body. His waist line, too, was knobby with hidden weapons. Pals of Jesse's.

Two of his gang, come from Kentucky to join him. 

She smiled up into his eyes. "Jesse's an old friend of mine," she said slowly, and watched him. His eyes narrowed, then he smiled slightly. "Yes? Then let us consider ourselves introduced.

She didn't want him to go. And she knew he didn't want to go; there was no need to say it in words. She just wanted him to keep on standing there looking at

her, filling the room with turmoil. She started from her chair, and his hand was beneath her arm assisting her. 

Shorty, shuffling impatiently, grunted. She turned to him "But you are in no special hurry? Won't you have a cup of coffee? And a plate of rabbit stew? I've kept it hot for the men. It's all ready." Hendrix bowed. ee "Anything that will prolong the pleasant hour. Shorty is hungry, I'm sure. As for me--my hunger is of another sort that the sight of gorgeous beauty is appeasing." The kitchen clock had marked off another hour. The men had eaten and now sat tilted back in their chairs smoking their pipes. Cora had cleared away the dishes and, leaning her arms on the red table cloth, chin on cupped hands, stared unblinkingly at Hendrix as he talked. She had asked him about his travels, about the cities he had seen, and the Kentucky country he had just left. He had answered, expansively, colorfully, stimulated by the warmth of the hot meal and the very evident admiration in the eyes of the girl who faced him.

It had been months since he had talked to a woman; longer than that since he had felt such an attraction as this sombre, tawny-eyed creature played over him. His eyes sought hers, clung hungrily, dropped. If they were alone-if Shorty would only go. Hell, what did a few hours more or less matter? Jesse and Frank would be

« 19 » there until tomorrow morning, and nights, opportunities, like this, came seldom. As if in response to his desire Shorty slammed down his chair, yawned, spoke: "Come on, let's be getting along." 

Hendrix rose, turned his back, winked deliberately at his impatient companion. "Run along, Shorty, and tell 'em I'm comin' soon. 

I'm sure Miss Cora would feel safer, after the scare we gave her, if I should keep her company until her folks get back. Wouldn't you, miss?" 

Cora nodded. "All right, Boss. Reckon I know when I'm third wheel to a cart. See you in the mornin'. Thanks for the vittles, Miss. Goodbye.' وو Hendrix followed him out onto the porch, repeating the directions she had given, closed the door. She stood still in the center of the room, hands gripped tightly over her breasts, lips parted, breath coming quickly.

Suddenly she ran to the narrow stairway leading overhead, listened intently, heard no sound. Flora was asleep in the cold upstairs room, head probably hidden under the comforts. She-they-were alone. No time for thoughts, no time for fears. Live for the moment—this wonderful moment that might never come again. The door opened, closed behind him. He leaned hard against it and held out his arms. "Come here, honey."

She sprang into them and slowly their lips met and clung together. He caressed her neck, her shoulders. 

Time stood still. Then, suddenly, she jerked back her head, threw off his exploring hands, listened, taut and tense. "It's Paw and Ed," she whispered. "You must go!"

The bark of dogs came clear and close. "Not now, honey-not yet!" he pleaded, and would again have clasped her. But she retreated fearfully, swiftly rearranging her rumpled hair, her loosened bodice. He struggled into his coat. Steps sounded on the porch. "But I'll see you again-soon?" Her voice was husky, anxious. "We'll all be off tomorrow early. But in the spring 

I'll come back. You'll never escape me now, Cora." "Well, thanks for the information," he was saying, as the door opened and the dogs rushed in barking and sniffing. 5 "What'd those men want?" 

Her father and brother were peeling off their wet coats, hanging them over chairs before the fire to dry. 

Her back was turned as she dished up the stew and poured the black coffee. They could not see her reddened cheeks and brilliant eyes; the listless voice with which she answered belied her singing heart. "They were lookin' for the Samuels' place. First time here, I reckon. Stopped in out of the storm. I fed 'em." "Belong to the gang?" "I dunno. I s'pose so. What kept you so late?" "We were just starting for home when the dogs treed a coon. After we'd cut down the tree and got t'other side of Dan Askew's place they ran up a possum. Fattest

< 21 » one I ever saw; must weigh close onto sixteen pound.

By the time we got it out it was sleeting so hard we stopped at Dan's to rest. Gosh, Co, what's the matter?" as the cup she was handing danced in its saucer. Then belligerently, "Did those men behave themselves? Didn't try any monkey business, did they?" "Of course not," grumblingly. "I'm just tired waitin' up till nearly midnight for you all. It's a wonder you didn't stay there drinkin' all night. Lots of consideration you give me. I ought just have gone on to bed like 

Flora did, and left you get your own supper. And I will too, next time. A lot you think about us girls, leavin' us here all night alone. A lot you'd care if anything did happen to us." "Oh, shut up. As if a great big strapping girl like you couldn't take care of herself anytime."

Her father, a small, gray man of few words, had not yet spoken, but, hunched over his plate, had shovelled in the food with the greed of a ravenous man. Now with laden knife suspended, he looked up, batted his eyes, and said: "They's sompin' funny 'bout that new hired hand at Askew's. That Jack Ladd he calls hisself." He shook his head several times, blinked, conveyed the knife to his mouth. "Funny how, Paw?" "I dunno." Another shake of the head. "He's too f'miliar. Asks too many questions. Makes friends too easy. Talks too much. I don't trust him. Give me some more stew. And 'nother cup coffee." "What was the man sayin'?" Cora questioned her brother. "Oh, I guess he'd just had too much likker. They were

both at it when we got there. Takin' up for the Jameses. 

Paw thought he kinda overdid it. Saying they were persecuted, and he hoped they'd keep on with the shows they were putting on and make those Pinkertons look like fools, like they had been doing.' "Well, what's wrong about that, Paw? I've heard lots of people talk like that and you didn't find fault." "Ye-ah. Clay County folks. But this man's a stranger.

We ain't even sure where he come from. 'Tain't natural, strangers takin' up for the Boys." "Ye-ah," admitted Ed, slowly, "it did seem to me that he was a little forward about it. And the way he up and joined the church and goes to socials and neighbors with everybody, as if he was tryin' to horn in.

Some one told me he even tried to keep company with Sallie Samuels, too. Of course she wouldn't have anything to do with him. As if any of the Samuels were goin' to take up with strangers. Even if he is what you might call right good looking, and has a smooth tongue. 

Too smooth, I'd say. I never yet heard him disagree with anyone. No, that ain't quite natural.” "Well, just because folks round here are tight lipped and clannish 'tain't no sign anyone different is to be suspicioned."

Her thoughts flew to the stranger, if indeed they had ever been far away. And in spite of her words, a little feeling of anxiety stirred her. "Well," yawned Ed, "it's none of our lookout. We've got nothin' to hide. Come on, let's turn in. I'm dog tired." "You go on. I'll feed the dogs and clean up first."

She didn't want to go to bed yet. She didn't feel as if she could ever fall asleep this night. She wanted to sit

CHAPTER TWO 

1 DUSK was down u. The deep and reflent 

USK was settling down upon the little frame farm by the melted sleet had frozen over again. Snow was in the air, and even as Cora stood looking out, her forehead pressed against the cold window pane, big feathery flakes began to fall. From the shed nearby she heard the ring of an ax as Ed chopped at the hard hickory logs with rhythmic strokes. Paw was in the barn next to it, feeding the stock. Behind her the water in the iron kettle boiled, ready for the mush to be cooked. The table was set, and a huge loaf of home-made bread, a crock of butter, a jug of sorghum molasses, and a pitcher of buttermilk, were in place. 

Hunched over the glowing stove, Flora with her throat bound in red flannel, nursed a fresh cold. But 

Cora, impervious to the peaceful scene, unheedful of the querulous complaining and sneezing of Flora, was far away, following in fancy the journey of the four men who had ridden off three days before. Where? 

South? West? What fresh news of outlawry would the next day or two bring? A little shiver of apprehension ran over her. Heretofore the report of another raid by

<< » 

SPORTING LADY 

the James Gang had meant only a welcome excitement and topic for conversation; something to mull over and talk about during those long hours before bedtime. as all sat round the kitchen fire. Even Paw would become loquacious, although neither he nor Ed were entirely in sympathy with their notorious neighbors. Theirs were religious and law-abiding natures. Confederates, naturally; but when their cause was lost they had resigned themselves to events and had lived under the new Union, much the same as before the war. With little to lose, they had never been affluent enough to own slaves.

But now, since that chance meeting with Hendrix, Cora was filled with apprehension. She had never feared for Jesse; he bore a charmed life, and was, after all, only a neighbor. But this man who had so strangely become a strong factor in her life-what guard had he against danger? What experience to help him to safety? She did not know. She knew nothing whatever about him except that the thought of him filled every waking and sleeping moment since he had closed the door behind him and gone out into the storm. 

When was spring? He had said he would come again, in the spring. And this was the twentieth of January. 

Hundreds of hours, crawling slowly by. Days upon days of watching the sun rise, mount and sink. If only he would send some word. But perhaps he would, and if he did not she might seek it. She could go to Mrs 

Samuels or the Doctor and ask for information. But even as the thought occurred she knew that would be useless. 

The Samuels either never knew or never told. What a woman that Zerelda Samuels was! Close-lipped and unafraid as her own daring sons. A woman who should have been born a man. No question as to where the

SPORTING LADY 

« 25 » boys got their recklessness. That soft-spoken man, their preacher father, had bequeathed none of his characteristics to the sons he had begotten.

Q2 Suddenly through the thick flying snow several men on horseback loomed darkly at the gate. She heard shouted hallos as they waited, still mounted; heard Ed's answering call and saw them both emerge and go towards the visitors. She opened the door and stood listening, recognizing the voice of the constable at Kearney. They were excited, talking fast, gesticulating. She could not hear what they said; caught only the name Samuels, the words "killed" and "fire." Her heart beat sickeningly. 

What she had feared had come. Pray God they were all safe; but, oh God, if any had got away, let it be him. 

She saw them wheel their horses and ride furiously away, saw her father and Ed start up towards the house, and only then heeded Flora's peevish cries, closed the door and walked unsteadily to the big rocker, slumping down with frightened eyes. "What's the matter?" queried the sick girl. "Somethin's wrong. Somethin's happened to the 

James Gang."

Their father and Ed entered. Their faces were excited, shocked. They dusted off the snow which covered caps and shoulders while Cora waited tensely. She had a thought that she was beating her breast with her fists, but they were clenched at her sides. She tried to speak

Then another came, heavier than the first. Mrs Samuels had run in, and she got a tobacco stick and pushed it into the fire, and then it exploded and killed Archie-tore a big hole in his side --and mangled Mrs Samuels' right arm below the elbow so it's got to be cut off. It's a damned outrage! They didn't care if they killed the whole family. It's murder, that's what it is, and those detectives ought to hang for it." The boys is goin' to make somebody pay for this and they won't be too particular who it is.” 3 Winter, late in arriving, stormed in fury through the short February month. Every day it sleeted or snowed and white banks piled high on each side of the roadway, kept open with difficulty by teams of the scattered farmers. The Missouri froze solid and ice cutters began their harvest in spite of snow and wind. Around the 

Simmons weather beaten farmhouse Ed and his father were forced to dig anew each day the paths to outhouses and gate. Save for the farmers clearing the road few passed by the house, and church going by the family was abandoned. Icicles a foot long hung from the eaves and drifts piled high as the window sills. 

Though isolated from neighbors and shut in upon themselves, they still rose hours before daylight and went about their appointed tasks. There was always plenty of work to do even on a small farm. Milking

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

and feeding, harness mending and wood chopping and splitting rails for new fences in the spring; cooking and cleaning, washing, darning, sewing, and making carpet rags. Within the kitchen, the only room in which a fire was kept, Cora went about her duties energetically, working off her restlessness. Trying not to think. Trying not to count the days. Hurrying them along by activities to meet the springtime. When she paused and looked back and remembered, numb aching seized her; and when she looked forward fear and doubt assailed her. For, when all was added together, there was so little upon which to base her hopes. An hour's acquaintance, a savage kiss, a hurriedly whispered promise. And since then, no word, no sign. Silence, isolation, sometimes almost despair. 

March came in like a roaring lion, with thunder, wind and flooding rain. The snow melted fast under the warm rains and suns until only bare patches were left on the fields and in fence corners. The ice on the river rotted and broke up, floating away in big chunks. Green ran around the ground, the tree buds swelled, and cherry and peach blossoms bravely burst and powdered the branches with sweet white and pink blossoms. All day long in the adjacent fields Ed and his father followed their plows, turning up the rich black earth, planting the wheat. Bedding and winter clothing hung airing on clothes lines in the door yard; the acrid smell of smoke, and soap, boiling in the great iron pot before the barn, filled the air. Half heartedly assisted by Flora, whose deep chest cough still hung on, Cora spent every spare moment digging and planting and weeding in the small vegetable garden between the barn and the orchard. 

Pausing, resting on heels, trowel dragging listlessly from her hand, gazing out over the countryside springing into

SPORTING LADY 

« 29 » new green life; sniffing the currents of air that came softly bearing odors of burning weeds and warm manure to mingle with the sweetness of fruit blossoms. Had not spring come yet?

April was moody, alternately smiling and weeping. 

The roads bogged in mud, and farmers on their way across the river to the city had hard going at times. 

Then May smiled on the land, with cloudless skies and a sun mounting higher with each breathless perfect day. 

Red bud and dogwood flowered. Corn planting began, and weeds in the garden required constant vigilance. 

Truly, Spring was here. And still there was no sign of the man upon whose promise she had fed her hungry heart through all the dragging months.

With compressed lips and cloudy eyes she went about her dreary tasks. Her speech was short and gruff. The family ate the food she set before them in silence, the men tired from their labor in the fields, Flora listless, half sick, racked by her cough and climbing early to bed, the men soon following her, leaving Cora alone to face the waking hours of night.

It was then, in the soft warm dusk, that she would steal to the gate and, leaning on its top, watch the roadway for a figure to emerge. The scent of lilacs rising from the big old bushes beside the gate stirred her senses until the crouching forces within her were almost uncontrollable. All her after life the perfume of lilacs was to bring back to her those nights of starlight and darkness and soft, vagrant zephyrs; and her desperate, fearful waiting. One thing only sustained and kept her hope alive. The James Boys, too, were still away. It could mean that he was with them; that important affairs hindered their return. They had not visited their home since the bombing, and the countryside waited, ex-

«30≫ 

SPORTING LADY 

pectantly. Well they knew the tragedy would not go unpunished. But not until it was safe to strike. Not until the Pinkertons, who had continued to turn up at intervals, relaxed their watchfulness and retired to other activities. On the day that news of old Dan Askew's murder again roused the County to tense excitement and whispered insinuations, Cora went about her work with flushing cheeks and brightened eyes. Dan's murder could mean only one thing: the James Boys had returned and taken their revenge. She joined eagerly in the talk concerning this latest crime, and after supper as she washed the dishes with clattering energy, she was humming under her breath. Ed, bringing in the foaming pails of milk, gazed at her unconscious back meditatively, speculating on her changed humor.

It was later than usual when she took her place at the gate. A moon, full and golden, flooded the roadway, bringing every object into clear relief. An hour went by but tonight it did not drag. She had much to think about, something to hope for. The James Boys were in the neighborhood; it was possible that some of their men had accompanied them. And Spring had definitely come.

When he was fully able he would come. He had remembered, even as she had. Had thought enough about her to send word by 

Jesse; thought enough to confide in another man. Twice upon her return to the house shortly before midnight she found Ed on the porch smoking his pipe; had answered his gruff, suspicious questions indifferently. "I couldn't sleep. Anything wrong in that?" and "You seem mighty concerned about me here lately. I'll just thank you to quit watching me. Don't it ever occur to you I might want to be alone and think, sometimes?"

She felt a secret contempt for Ed. He was so smug and narrow. Churchy. Had never even looked at a girl for all he was five years older than she was. Always

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

grumbling about wanting an education; wanting to read law. And poring over some old books a lawyer in 

Liberty had given him. No hot red blood in him; only icewater. His mind was musclebound, and heavy with old prejudices, handed down as morals. And anyway, what had she done that could look suspicious to him?

Walking alone in the starlight wasn't any sin. He made her nervous and apprehensive, having to be so careful and always on her guard. What if he found her with 

Hendrix when finally he did come? Well, what if he did? She would face him down. He was a coward; he would be afraid of one of the James Gang. And if he drove her too far!

She was glad the dogs were away, loaned to a friend in Liberty last February. With Ed's suspicious nature and with the dogs around there'd be little chance of her romance being undiscovered. She'd tell them all about it soon enough, if everything turned out all right; when she found out if she was more than a passing fancy to the Kentuckian. She would know soon, now. 

CHAPTER THREE HE 

1 

E CAME the first week in June, on a starless, cloudy night. He was afoot and appeared out of the darkness only a hundred yards away. At first she wasn't sure and drew back into some hazel bushes, her

SPORTING LADY 

«35» heart thumping, until he was almost opposite. He was carrying his hat in his hand, and even though she saw that he had shaved off his mustache, there was no mistaking the long black curls and the towering figuretaller even than her own six feet. She called softly, sprang forward, and their arms clasped each other hungrily. Afterwards they climbed the bank and sat in the lush grass, holding each other closely, greedily savoring each other's lips, murmuring together of their loneliness, now appeased.

When the first drops of rain began to fall, she led him stealthily to the barn. It was a little distance from the house, and her sharp eyes detected no sign of life from the slumbering household. In the sweet smelling hay in the loft they lay side by side while the hours passed by unheeded. And after their first ecstasy had spent itself, with her head on his arm and cheeks pressing close, she questioned, and he told her something of the events of his absence. 

The morning after he had joined the Boys they had ridden away, up through Kansas and over into Nebraska. There had been a badly timed and awkwardly pulled off raid, and only by separating had they escaped the posse close behind them. He had gone down into 

Arkansas to let things blow over, and in early May had worked his way back to Jackson County, intending to keep his promise to return. But he was broke, and had no intention of coming empty handed. So he had staged a little affair, singlehanded, had got away and hid with friends of the Boys. That night about midnight a half dozen men had surrounded the house, threatening to fire it. With a double-barrelled shotgun in his hands, two Colts at waist, he had suddenly darted from the door, shooting his way to the barn where his horse stood

«36» 

SPORTING LADY 

bridled. He had tossed away the gun when emptied, and with both revolvers blazing while his horse dashed ahead without guidance, had made good his escape. But not without injury, for two bullets had gone through his leg. For two days he had hidden in the thick brush, riding only at night, and coming at last to one of the Gang's hideouts, where his wounds were cared for. 

Luckily, two weeks ago Jesse himself had arrived. He brought news that Hendrix was still being looked for, and had taken him to an island some miles up the river, stocked him with supplies, and advised him to lay under cover for a while. Jesse himself was on his way to 

Kearney to meet Frank. That was how he had been able to send her word.

Cora was impressed as she listened to the recital. In spite of the matter-of-fact way in which he told it, her imagination was stimulated by the dangerous events through which he had passed. He was brave-as brave and reckless as Jesse himself. And he had confided in her. That could only mean that he was surely hers now -her man. He had said so and she no longer doubted. 

Out of all those he had met who might have succumbed to his good looks and his daring, he had selected her for his woman and had returned to her. "Take me back with you," she begged.

He hesitated. She threw herself upon his breast, covered his face with kisses, pleaded ardently. "It can only be such a short time," he countered. "Only a few weeks. And then what?" "I don't care," she cried. "You've got to. We belong together now. And when you go away, I'll wait for you to come back. I'll be there always, waiting for you. 

Don't you want me, Wayne? Don't it mean anything that I want to come?"

SPORTING LADY 

« 37 » "God, yes! I'm only thinking of you, honey. It ain't fair to you." دو "It ain't fair to leave me here now. And if you don't take me, I'll come anyhow. I know the river-and that island. I can get there. You've got to, Wayne. I can't go on here any longer-now."

So it was settled. He would be waiting at a certain point on the river bank late the next afternoon. It was a risk, coming by daylight, but the river was rising; they couldn't chance the currents after dark. It had been bad enough tonight; tomorrow would be worse.

And after tomorrow he would be unable to come even to see her until the rise subsided. That was what decided him.

She remained behind in the loft while he made his way quietly down the steep ladder. Listening, she heard his receding steps passing stealthily behind the barn. 

Then something else other hurried feet running as if to intercept, two shots, silence. She whitened and her hands gripped together. Ed! He had seen them—had known they were there all the time. He had been too cowardly to come out and confront the man; had waited until he left and then shot at his back. Rage shook her. If he had hit Wayne she'd kill him. The dirty little sneak.

She heard him ascending the ladder and rose to meet him. The gray light of dawn outlined the square opening through which his head and shoulders emerged, but within the loft it was still dark. She moved forward, her fingers flexed into claws, red anger still clouding her brain. The guttural sounds which came from her throat were not her own voice. "What have you done to him? If you've shot him I'll kill you!"

< 38» 

SPORTING LADY 

"You dirty slut! I'll teach you to befoul our home.”

She sprang for his throat, but his fist had already shot out and caught her on the point of the chin. She staggered back and went down into the hay. 2 Cora opened her aching eyes to the cobwebby rafters of the loft. Slowly she turned her heavy head from side to side, struggling back to consciousness, wondering for a moment where she was. Her neck and shoulders felt bruised; when she lifted them she was nauseated and sank heavily back again. It was broad daylight. Through the aperture in the floor she could see beams of sunshine dancing in the dust. She closed her eyes wearily and sank back into a semi-comatose state. When she again opened them the sun's rays had shifted. She sat up and looked around, remembering slowly. Her eyes sought the flattened straw beside her. Her nuptial bed. 

God, how her head ached. Stiff and shaking she rose to her feet, made her way slowly down the ladder and out into the sunlight. Blinded for a moment by the bright glare, she covered her eyes with her arm and leaned sickly against the barn. When she opened them she saw 

Flora, holding up her long black calico riding skirt, emerge from the house. With averted eyes and lips compressed into a thin line she went quickly down the path to where Ed waited on his horse, holding hers by the bridle. He was facing straight ahead. She watched dully as Flora mounted to her side saddle from the

SPORTING LADY 

« 39≫ horse block and they rode away through the spattering mud.

Mechanically she made her way to the pump, unfastened her bodice and, taking the pins from her heavy hair, let the cold water run over head and shoulders.

The shock cleared her brain and entering the empty kitchen she poured a cup of black coffee from the pot on the stove and, carrying it out to the porch, slowly drank it. With its stimulation her mind began to function. Her anger had passed. It was all so inevitable. Already she felt a stranger to the scene spread before her as her eyes wandered to the barn and outbuildings, the green of the growing wheat, the black plowed field beyond. This had been her world for nineteen years. 

And now she was leaving it, never to return. That too was inevitable. And she was glad. How glad she was!

Far down the plowed field she saw her father's figure rise upright. He was planting corn. It must be in this week. Then why had Ed left him to finish alone? 

Why had he and Flora ridden away together?

Wet hair spread out over towel-covered shoulders, she made her way in the morning sunshine to where her father worked. If he was aware of her approach, he gave no sign. For the first time she noticed how bent and weary he seemed. Yes, Paw was getting old. Perhaps she would never see him again after this evening. 

Well, people got old. Fast. She herself would be old some day; but before she got like Paw she was going to really live. She stood beside him. "Where did Ed and Flora go to?"

He shot her a glance that took in the red and blue discoloration of her chin, but his voice was flat and expressionless as he replied: "Gone to get the dogs."

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

"Well-but why did Flora go along?" Not that she cared. But she must say something, if he wasn't going to talk. "She went to see the doctor for her cough." "Oh." Silence. Cora shifted her feet. Then, involuntarily, "Paw, I'm goin' to get married."

The sad old eyes turned to her, turned away as if ashamed. "We'll-we'll talk about that afterwards."

Down the row his roughshod feet moved, the withered crooked hands dropping corn. A little mist came over Cora's eyes. "Goodbye, Paw." Though her lips formed the words, they were silent. Turning she went back to the house. 3 There was no time to lose. She mustn't be here when 

Ed got back. She must go at once to the place on the river bank and wait there until Wayne came for her. 

If he came. If Ed had not shot him and he would be able to. In that case she would have to manage somehow to get there alone.

She could see Paw through the window as she gathered together a few necessaries. Laying a shawl on the bed she placed in it her best seersucker dress, bonnet, a change of underwear, soap, stockings, towels. Then she hesitated. It might be rough living on the island. She entered the room occupied by Ed and Paw and returned with jeans, a calico shirt, and a pair of boots. Tying

SPORTING LADY 

< 41 » together the ends of the shawl, she carried it down to the kitchen and added some cold corn pone, part of a boiled ham, coffee and sugar. Perhaps Wayne's supplies would not reach for two mouths. Her sunbonnet hung on a nail. Donning it she caught up the bundle and furtively went forth. Putting the house and barn between herself and the old man in the field, she followed the way that Hendrix had taken the night before, eyes searching the ground for signs of blood; but she saw none, either there or on the roadway, and her anxiety lessened as she sped along the road, muddy from last night's rain.

The sun beat down hotly, the air was wet with humidity, her head ached fiercely, but she did not slacken her pace. It was a long way to the river. Several times she hid among bushes on the roadside to escape other wayfarers, but when they had passed she quickened her pace, sometimes half running. Caution told her Ed would not tarry long in Liberty. He would hurry back to watch her. With the aid of the dogs. Well she knew why he had gone for them. What luck that she and 

Wayne had made their plans last night. Suppose they had waited and he had come again tonight! No chance for him to creep to the loft with the dogs on guard. 

There would have been bloodshed this time.

When she reached the river and saw the swift muddy water she experienced some misgivings. It was highdangerously high. Already driftwood was plunging down its current and large suck holes swirled threateningly. The water was eating into the high banks—not high now, but almost on a level with the brown surface. 

It would not be easy to cross in a skiff. And there were still hours to pass before he came; hours in which the

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

gnawing river might eat out the entire banks, growing more greedy, and more dangerous, with each hour.

She turned along the riverside and made her way as hurriedly as possible over the slippery clay, but had gone only a short distance when it grew unsafe. The waters were undermining it. When the ground began to tremble under her feet she turned a little inland, crashing through thick underbrush. Vines and tough tendrils of wild grape clutched at her with detaining fingers, retarding her progress. But it was cooler here, shaded by cottonwoods from the fierce noonday sun; and she was hidden from view. There was now no hurry. 

Hours yet before Hendrix would come.

When she reached the clump of willows which was her destination the water was halfway to their tops. 

She went on beyond them, slushing through seepage, to a small high clearing, and scanned the river, straining her eyes in the direction of the solitary island out of sight. Not a sign of life on the angry brown surface. 

Far across, in the direction from which she had come, were the bluffs crowned with squat buildings above the Levee at Kansas City. She could make out the white blot of a river steamer tied up at the foot of Main Street. But in the west, where tonight she would be heading, there was nothing to be seen but the swollen breast of the mighty stream now on its yearly rampage.

She crept under a tangle of hazel bushes where the ground was dry, and from where she had an unobstructed view upstream. She had eaten no breakfast and hunger pangs assailed her. After devouring one of the huge corn pones sprinkled through with cracklings she felt better; but her head was heavy and still ached. She rested it on the bundle and stretched out in relaxation. 

A pleasant feeling of freedom possessed her, the first

SPORTING LADY 

< 43 » she had ever known; a quiet happiness flowed through her veins and around her heart. The heavy scent of blossoms on a nearby clump of elderberry bushes soothed her, her eyes grew heavier and she slept-a deep sleep of hours.

She awoke suddenly, thinking she had heard the baying of dogs; listened intently. Suppose Ed had returned and with the dogs had taken up her trail! All was silent, but the thought awakened nervous fears. Ed had had time to get back. A squint at the low sun told her that. 

And though the dogs were not man hunters they might be able to pick up her scent and follow. Her heart hammered at the thought. In the west the sun was watery, dimmed by a mackerel sky. To the northwest, through a little space between treetops, she could see a black and ominous cloud forming and moving towards her. Flashes of heat lightning played across it. It was going to storm. She ran to the little rise of clearing and strained her eyes up the river. He must come soon; after the storm broke they would not be able to cross. It still lacked an hour of the appointed time. But perhaps he had seen the cloud and had set out earlier, trusting to her common sense to do likewise. 

As if in answer to her straining wish she thought she detected a black spot on the river's surface, a long way off. Of course it might be a floating log, but she watched it with tightened eyes as it came slowly forward seemingly maneuvering its way cautiously. It was many minutes, during which time she stood tense and motionless, before she made out that it was really a skiff, and oared by one man. She caught her breath on a dry sob of relief. Wayne had started early.

With her eyes alternately upon the dark encompassing cloud and the solitary man skillfully approaching in the

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

down stream, she waited on the spot she had selected for him to land; waited, waving her arms in signals to his unconscious back. Then he turned and saw her, answered, pulled with mighty strokes which sent the skiff flying through the swift current; cautiously turned in to shore and grounded. 

There was no time for sentimental greetings. She tossed her bundle and clambered in, while he fitted extra oars into the oar locks. "You'll have to give a hand," he said. "We've got to beat the wind." She nodded, seized the oars, and with strong strokes they pulled out, crossed through the current into quieter waters, and headed upstream. 4 In the perilous hour that followed no word was spoken save an occasional order from the man seated behind her. The sun, washed out by the mackerel sky, was lost to view. An ominous still dusk settled over their yellow path. From the northwest the black menacing cloud showed banked spheres of wind, in the flashes across it. Occasionally the lightning was jagged forks of death-dealing electricity, not yet turned earthward. Doggedly they bent their backs, teeth clenched, mouths dry, hearts pounding with the physical strain.

Smaller currents from the strong central one reached out and clutched at them, sometimes sending their frail craft spinning round, threatening to carry it back the weary way through which they had come. If the wind

SPORTING LADY 

< 45 » caught them before they reached the island, the situation would be desperate.

Then, all of a sudden, out of the dusk it loomed ahead of them. Not one of the vagrant sandbars, here today and gone tomorrow at the will of the capricious river; covered only with willows, cottonwoods and coarse grass.

Duck Island was a natural one, narrow and perhaps a mile long. The ground at the near end was low, but it began rising halfway of its length, and ended in bluffs at the farther point. Cora, twisted round in her seat, could dimly make out forest trees towering against the dull sky; a mere blot on the fast darkening landscape. "We're making it; we're almost there." 

He rested his oars and turned round to look. "We must land nearer the other end of the island. 

This part will all be under water." 

Then the wind came. It caught them up with a dull roar that whipped the tops of the trees and swayed them low. But they were hugging the shore and by calling upon all their remaining strength managed to weather this first shock. Now darkness enveloped them like a pall, rain fell in huge warm drops beating a tattoo on the bottom of the skiff, kicking up splashes in the water beside them, growing more violent in swifter and swifter gusts until it slapped at their faces, ran deep on the bottom of the boat, and they were compelled to row through the glancing sheets blindly, save for the recurrent flashes of lightning which flared through them.

It was a long half mile, but danger was practically past; for, if disaster overtook them, the shore was only twenty feet away. But the pull in the teeth of the storm slowed them up until they seemed to be barely making headway. Then suddenly above their heads the black

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

sky opened in a long, slow, blinding flash, the skiff shook and stood still, and Cora found herself in the water at the bottom of it. For a moment she was blinded, and numb and terrified. Then came his voice: "Steady there! Are you all right?" 

She fumbled to her seat. "What was it? Are we struck?" "No. It was a tree there on the bank. Sure you're all right? Just sit still. I can make it alone now. We're nearly there."

She gripped the wet oars again, gritted her teeth, and bent to the steady rhythm he set. By a flash of lightning they grounded on a mucky shore. 

Through the deep mud she slipped and crawled up the natural runway, grasping at roots to aid herself. Looking back, she made out Hendrix struggling with the skiff, attempting to pull it up to safety. She ran along the higher bank she had gained and called through the wind: "Point the bow up here. I think I can reach it."

Struggling and cursing, he managed to heave the heavy craft onto his shoulders, twined his leg around a sapling to steady his slipping feet, and edged the bow upwards toward her. She grasped it and tugged. Her dripping hands slipped along the wet sides, unable to secure a hold. Two thoughts rode her mind—they must not lose the boat, their only means of communication with the world; and she must not fail him. She must

SPORTING LADY 

< 47 » prove here, now, that she was his helpmeet as well as his woman. He must see that he could depend upon her in emergencies; must be proud of this big strong body that was to be at his disposal always—and in all ways. 

To her desperate mind this was a test of their future together. Her desperation won. Just when it seemed she would fail she felt him, with what must have been superhuman strength, shift the skiff forward once again. Her hands caught the bottom of the small bow seat and she slipped and fell backward. Pulling it up inch by inch across her flattened body, she tugged and twisted until a safe margin lay upon the ground. It took her last ounce of strength and she lay still, breathing heavily, her heart pounding sickeningly, half under the boat and with its weight resting on her. There Wayne found her and muttering curses and praises, words sweet to her ears, lifted it off and would have helped her to rise, but she shook her head, saying, "Wait a minute. 

I'm winded," and lay still, drawing deep gurgling breaths, grateful for the slash of rain on her upturned face. Over sodden grass they made their way through the blackness of the trees. His arm was around her, aiding her stumbling feet. Neither spoke, for breath still came in deep irregular gulps from their overstrained lungs. 

Her right arm smarted painfully and her legs were shaking, but she tried not to lean upon him-tried to walk steadily. They came to the wet folds of a collapsed tent

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

and for a moment her mind wavered in disappointment.

She so needed to sink down in a warm dry spot and rest -God, rest forever. But his words, short and crisp, dissipated her momentary gloom. "I moved the things to the cave. Thought there'd be a bad storm.”

A moment later they entered its dim mouth. By the flare of a lantern he lighted she looked around. It was not large, but the ground was covered with white sand that still retained some warmth from the sun. It must have been laid only this afternoon. On the ground in the far end was a bed of branches over which were thrown blankets. Opposite, in a wooden box and overflowing to the sand floor, were the stores. With a deep sigh she sank to an upturned log seat and closed her eyes. 

She felt a tin cup held to her lips, heard him say: "Drink it, you need it," and felt the fiery liquid scorching her throat. She saw him pour himself a cupful from the huge jug, and felt a tingling exhilaration run through her veins, and happiness and warmth slowly taking possession of her. Then he was on the ground beside her removing her soaking shoes and stockings, while his eyes sought hers hungrily. "You're not to stir. I'm going to undress you and put you to bed. Christ, Cora, what a damned good sport you are!" 

His hands fumbled with her buttoned bodice, ripped it open, slipped it off. Obediently she rose and stood motionless, watching him with shining eyes as one by one he removed her drenched garments until she stood, naked and white, in the dusky flickering shadows of the cave. Then he was rubbing her with a rough towel -swift, strong strokes that brought a rosy glow to her skin and sent the blood pumping to her heart. The in-

SPORTING LADY 

< 49 » sidious exhilaration of the whiskey played through her brain. Suddenly she threw her arms about his neck pressed her soft body against his wet clothing, sought his lips. But with an amused, excited chuckle he firmly removed them, caught up a blanket and wrapped it tightly around her, pinning her arms to her sides, boldly took the kiss he had denied; then, lifting her bodily, laid her upon the bed of boughs, and blew out the lantern.

She could smell the sweet tang of the tender crushed leaves beneath her, heard the heavy downpour of rain beyond the low entrance, caught glimpses of him against the solid silver sheet of it when lightning flashed, leaving the darkness denser and more mysterious than before. Then he lay beside her, her head pillowed against the roughness of his blanket, her body strained to his. "And now it can rain all it damned pleases!" he laughed as his lips closed down on hers.

CHAPTER FOUR 

C% 1 

ORA awoke the next morning from a restful dreamless sleep and lay for some moments luxuriously stretching, and watching the brilliant sunshine through the opening. Wayne was already dressed and out. She had roused as he was stirring about, getting into his clothes, then dropped off again into slumber. Now

« 50» 

SPORTING LADY 

she lay watching the shadows of the leaves, stirring gently in a light breeze; sniffing sensuously the hot moisture being drawn from the wet ground; dreamily thinking back over those precious hours which had succeeded the darkness and drama into which her hitherto dull life had plunged. Utter happiness and contentment filled her. She would have been satisfied to lie thus for hours hugging to her heart those recent memories which surely never again in the future could find their counterpart. 

But Wayne's figure blocked the opening, and he was calling: "Wake up, Lazibones! Going to sleep all day? I'm hungry as a wolf." Wrapping the blanket around her she sat up and looked about for her clothes. "I hung them out to dry. They're still wet. Got anything else to wear?" She had, and scrambled into jeans and shirt, tugged on the heavy boots, swaggered before him. The garments fitted snugly on her slim hips, too tightly over the voluptuous bosom. He laughed at her, slapped at her; gathered up dry wood salvaged before the rain and set about building a fire. She fried thick slices of bacon, made coffee, warmed the corn pone, and they ate hungrily. Laughing, careless of the future; exhilarated by the return of the sun, by their unalterable nearness, by prospective long days of close companionship.

He had been to see the boat, had pulled it to higher ground and made it firmly fast to a tree. The river had risen much higher during the night. They were in for a real flood, it looked like. They had brightened at that. 

A flood would render them even safer from chance hunters and fishermen.

After the fire was extinguished and their tin plates and cups rinsed, they made their way to the highest

SPORTING LADY 

«SI» point of the bluff overlooking the river. This was not so high now; not more than ten feet above the swirling waters. Cora looked at them speculatively. "Is there any chance it will go this high?" she asked. "I don't think so. It never has covered the island. 

Even if it does, we've got the boat." 

Silently, a little awed, they gazed out over the vast expanse. Already the river was some miles wide at this point. Logs were being sucked along in the current that ran a hundred yards off shore; uprooted trees, chicken coops. Golden sunshine spangled the air about them; the sky, cobalt blue above, dipped to lighter tints on the horizon. In the green-leafed boughs birds twittered, occasionally trilling forth little arias. Save for them, and the dull angry surge of the water, no sound broke the summer stillness. They were alone together and the golden hours of idleness and love stretched before them with promises so glad that they sat silent upon a fallen trunk, arms intertwined, bathing in the joy of the moment. 2 The sun was low in the west when they went again to look after the boat. It was still high and dry, but the river had risen two feet since Wayne had tied it up in the morning. The water was within a yard of the ground on which they had struggled so desperately to haul it—was it only yesterday? The day had sped so swiftly, yet yesterday seemed very long ago. Back home -HOME! Had it ever been home? Had she not always

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

been a carefree vagabond in trousers living in a cave which already seemed more a home than the farm in 

Clay? What was that saying? "Home is where the heart is." Well, that made this island, their cave, truly her home. But, back there where she came from, what were they thinking? They had missed her now since twentyfour, perhaps thirty, hours. She smiled a little maliciously as she conjured up Ed's face-his anger. He would be her jailer, would he?

But, even if she was in disgrace, they must have felt some anxiety. Perhaps the dogs had followed her and lost her scent at the river's edge; perhaps they thought she had drowned herself. She wished she had left her bonnet caught in brush at the river's edge. Then they would have been persecuted forever with the thought that they had driven her to death. And she was dead so far as they were concerned. Never again did she want to see them. Her life, her beckoning, joyful life was before her, and in it they would never again have a part.

It was then that her eyes, gazing out unseeing, suddenly focused on a small roofed object upriver near the shore. Something white stood against its darkness. She clutched at Wayne's arm. "Look, do you see that? Up there, headed this way. 

It looks like a chicken." "Yes, it does. I could use a good fat hen right now."

Rapidly it came closer, eddied into shore, swirled out again. "It's a dog!" he exclaimed. "Oh, Wayne, let's save it. Poor little thing. I'd love to have it." 

"You want it? Then we must try to get it."

He snatched up a long branch as he ran along the

SPORTING LADY 

«53» bank and threw himself down, leaning half over, extending the pole full length. If he could brush it off it could swim in to shore. Cora watched intently. She could see it plainly now, crouched down, hanging on precariously by its paws, its eyes terror-stricken and piteous. 

The threatening branch frightened it, and it yelped. 

But when almost within reach the little house lurched in an eddy and was driven farther out. Cora uttered a disappointed cry and turned away her eyes. She didn't want to see it drown out there in the swifter flowing water. Then she felt Wayne rushing past her, heard him cry: "There it is, against those logs down there." 

Sure enough the kennel had lodged insecurely against some driftwood tangled about a group of half submerged cottonwoods. Holding to one of the slender trunks, Wayne plunged chest deep into the water and caught the little thing by the scruff of the neck as the kennel dislodged and went sailing on tenantless. "Here's your doggie," he grinned, holding it before him as he stalked dripping toward her.

It was shaking and whining with its tail curved in a bow and its soft dark eyes gazing pleadingly into her own. She took its wet little body into her arms and cuddled it against her breast, talking baby talk, gently reassuring it. She could feel its little heart beating violently against its bony ribs, and for a moment forgot the man looking down at her in amusement while she sought to reassure it. When its trembling grew quiet and the little pink tongue darted out and licked her face, she rose laughing happily. He stood, wet hands in wet pockets, his lips under the stubble of beard, a trifle grim, his eyes curious.

More frequent now the signs of destruction from up river. Larger uprooted trees, sheds, and a dilapidated house. Swollen bodies of horses and cattle. A box car. During the night the skies opened again and the rain came down in torrents. The little dog whined, as if the sound of the downpour brought back tragic memories, and leaving his corner, crept into her arms. Wayne, too, was restless, stirring continually.

Once she roused to see him peering out; but the sound of the rain was soothing to her, and she cuddled the warm little body of Moses closer and dropped off to sleep again.

That was why Jesse had brought him here to hide away until the time set. And now she was thankful for the flood. They would have a week more, at least. Of course he would come back to her-after the job was finished. 

Naturally. And they would go away somewhere together, flush with money, and she would begin to see that world about which she and Flora had talked that night-the night Wayne came. Yes, it would be a grand life after they left here, but-no days could ever again be as wonderful as these of their honeymoon. What one has, one has; make the most of it. "Well," she said brightly. She munched the cold ham, throwing bits of the less choice parts to the quivering, eager Moses; watching 

Wayne as he lay breathing heavily. The stubble on his face had grown longer, unkempt. It marred his attractiveness. He hadn't told her, but she surmised he was not shaving for a reason, as she had noticed he had razors. He was growing a disguise. Somehow, she wished he hadn't at this particular time. Nor did he look any better for sleeping with his mouth open. It was the first time she had seen him asleep; it had always been dark. 

She watched him curiously for awhile, thinking unpleasant little wisps of thoughts. He began to snore and, shaking herself a little angrily, she took Moses in her arms and sat on the sand at the cave's entrance, gazing out steadfastly and dejectedly at the slackening

SPORTING LADY 

<57» rain-watching for the first patch of blue, the first gleam of light through broken clouds, that would enable her to get out of here out into the fresh clean air. For something intangible and disheartening had entered into this place; for some reason her ecstasy had departed and she felt bored and restless.

The next day the sun shone and the waters began to recede. In relief at this discovery and at the first whiffs of the cloying warm air, Cora's depression dropped from her. Wayne, too, was cheerful and as they boiled their coffee over the fire built on flat stones, and ate breakfast hungrily, they were once more gay and happy.

She forgot, resolutely, the long lonely day and night just passed; forgot the disillusionment that had so suddenly and sharply assailed her. For the first time they talked of the future and his assurance was given that he, too, was contemplating a reunion after-what? He did not say. Outside that first confidence, which excused his tardy return to her, he had not mentioned his unusual way of making a living. Possibly he never would. Probably he expected her to take him just as he was, without explanations of any sort. Well, that was all right. 

What she didn't know wouldn't fret her. She'd always be waiting for him when he came back. And he'd always come back. These things they managed to convey to each other, without going deeply into the matter. 

That day Wayne retrieved a pig, marooned upon a rooftop. He got wet doing it—and, needlessly, thought

Cora, for the sun was very hot and his clothes drying quickly, resorted again to the jug. She caught her lips curving a little contemptuously and resolutely straightened them. Anyway, he did not take so much, and it only made him lively and amorous. Then later he butchered the pig and cut up the choice pieces, wrapping them in leaves and placing them in a hole in the wall of the cave. "We'd better go a little easy on the stores," he remarked. "There won't be any game left, and we'll get tired of fish. We might be here a month yet." 

Day before yesterday that remark would have made her happy. Now she received it silently, thinking how long a month might seem. 

Moses gorged himself for the first time and lay, pot bellied and drowsy, in the sun. She looked at him and smiled affectionately. He was disgracefully muddy and dirty. She must wash him after he had digested his food, and see what his long tangled hair looked like when it was clean. 

Wayne appeared from the cave. He was flushed. 

Drinking again. The bristles on his face were arrogant, the thick black curls tangled on his forehead. She turned her eyes away. "I'm going down and try to salvage some planks and logs for a raft. Want to come along and watch me?" "No. I'm too lazy. I'm going to wash the dog after awhile." 

He looked at her sharply. "Not getting tired of my company already, are you, sweetheart?" "Not yet." She watched him moodily as he strode away.
