«59» "I hope he finds the planks," she thought. 

But her wish was unfulfilled. 5 The waters, now receding, left thick black mud and slime over the lower part of the island. For days it was almost impossible to make one's way through it, and they were consequently confined to the relatively small plot of high ground which had remained above water, until it finally dried out under the sun which now blazed down daily. There was little to occupy them. 

They slept late in the shadowed cave. Even so the June days were long and tedious with idleness. It took but little time to prepare their simple food. After breakfast 

Wayne, carrying fishing lines and shotgun, on the chance of coming across small game that might have survived the high water, wandered away out of sight. 

She dawdled over the few household duties taking as much time as possible. Sometimes she would throw herself down on a carpet of thick coarse grass, and with Moses dozing beside her, lie for hours gazing up into the leaves of the trees, listening idly to the liquid notes of wild birds, her mind for the most part blank. She was enervated and listless and lazy, and wondered sometimes if perhaps she had not got a touch of malaria from the flood waters. It wasn't like her to feel so no account. 

But she did not mention it to Wayne. They were rather silent when together. It almost seemed as if, in those first rapturous days, they had said all they had to say to each other. She would have liked to hear about

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

his adventures, those exciting times through which, she knew, he had passed; but after one or two futile efforts to draw him out, had desisted. Such men as he did not tell of those things. She doubted if Jesse and Frank had even confided in Zerelda Samuels, their mother. She quite understood it was part of the game and did not feel hurt at his tight-lipped evasion of her questions. 

Often she and Moses went to the highest point of the bluff above their camping ground, and she sat hugging her knees, gazing out at the river, now back in its banks. 

Sometimes a steamboat would pass and she watched it out of sight. On its decks she could distinctly make out some of the passengers and crew. And after it had passed she would wait expectantly until, quite long afterward, she heard its far away whistles as it rounded the bend and approached the Levee in Kansas City.

She imagined herself on it. Talking to the travellers; sitting down to sumptuous meals at the long table in the dining saloon; the increased activities as they approached a river town; evenings under a full moon with the voices of the darkies singing quartettes. Floating day after day with happy fellow passengers down to St

Louis-on to New Orleans. It was fun to imagine it all and to know that some day she would actually be doing it. She never questioned that. Maybe before long; maybe as soon as Wayne came back to her after his next venture.

Wayne was fretting; she could see that. He had expected word from Jesse long before this; was worried at the delay. That of course was why he was so silent and taciturn these days. He was provoked about the boat getting away, too. He would have been over to the mainland before now, reconnoitering, learning the cause of the delay, had he the means to go. It made him

SPORTING LADY 

« 61 » restless and sullen. It was getting on her nerves, too. 

Anything might have happened out there in the world, while they waited here. If only there was something definite, some time set for them to leave, they might perhaps recapture those first thrilling emotions-with separation facing them, pack into the short time left enough memories to last until their reunion.

But this deadly waiting, watching, listening-while June passed into July, and the sun blazed down with tropic intensity, and there was nothing to do but pass the time as they could and wait for nightfall. The nights were still happy, still satisfying. Lying in the darkness, close together; whispering silly sweet words that somehow could not find utterance in daylight. If it could always be night! But then, that, too, would grow monotonous. It was, partly, the tedious hot days that made the nights so welcome. 

Presently another problem faced them. Skimp as she would, by mid-July the flour had given out, and there remained but a small bit of bacon. They were reduced to living on Wayne's daily catch of fish. And when the bacon was gone, they would have to boil it. Her stomach retched at the thought. Never, in after life, was Cora to eat catfish with relish. There were no frogs either.

Wayne had managed to get together one mess. Over and over during the catfish days she thought with longing of the tender white meat of frog legs; over and over she remembered the scant cup of wild strawberries she had gathered in June. Weeks ago. Her craving for green things became an obsession. Especially did she long for cabbage. Raw, white, firm. Sometimes this appetite reduced her to chewing green leaves of trees or bushes. The tender young ones were not so bad; at least they relieved her craving for a time. Sometimes

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

Wayne shot a squirrel; but they were poor and strong, and only the broth was palatable. 

Many times during these days she thanked God for Moses. He was always at her heels, always ready to seemingly smile and wag his tail at a kind word. As she lay idly around in the shade his eager devoted eyes were always watching her, waiting for a caress. And he was a beautiful dog. His bath revealed him as white and silky. Long hair covered his ears and eyes and hung in ridiculous whiskers from his chin. She didn't know his breed, but he was a good dog. Had race. She brushed him for a long time every day, with her own hairbrush.

And washed him often. It gave her pleasure to see how his silky coat responded to her care. After that first time, Wayne had not again displayed jealousy; hardly seemed to notice him. And Moses paid scant attention to the master of the place. His was a one track mind, his little dog heart incapable of devotion to two persons. 

And it had been given unreservedly to her. That was one reason she loved him so. 1 

One day, more listless than usual, she whistled to Moses, asleep with one eye open, and walked slowly along the bank towards the far end of the island. It had suddenly become imperative for her to have companionship and she sought out Wayne. She knew that he generally fished at the far point of land. The fishing was also an excuse for watching, hour after hour, for the skiff that must come to liberate them. Even to each other they no

SPORTING LADY 

«63 » longer pretended that they were content to be alone together.

He did not hear her as she came into view and stood looking at him. The fishing poles lay on the bank, their corks bobbing in the water. His chin, covered now with a thick growth of beard, was on his hunchedup knees, his eyes were gazing down the stretch of river and across to where, out of sight, lay Clay County. 

How completely he had forgotten her. How little she really meant to him for all his protestations, for all she had given up for him. She smiled at the dishonesty. For him? For herself. And no matter what happened to them if they should eventually be separated for good, she would always be grateful to him for releasing her from the farm and Flora and Paw and Ed. 

She sat on the bank beside him, put her arm about him with a little hug, laid her cheek against his shoulder. 

He glanced at her in surprise, mechanically returned her caress. "I was lonesome, honey. I wonder how much longer it'll be." "God knows." His tone was dark, petulant. "I'll have something to say to Jesse James about this. He'll hear a thing or two when we meet." "Mebbe something has happened. Then, too, he thinks you have a boat, and could come yourself." "That's just it. He might think I have run away on him. I was to meet him before the first of July. But 

Hell! He knew how high the water was. He should have come to see that I was all right." "Maybe he couldn't come-maybe he was-called away."

of water and look for a skiff to head for the island. 

Suddenly Cora spoke— "Why don't you chop down some trees and make a raft? It wouldn't be dangerous now to get across. We could float down even faster than rowing up. And it would give you something to occupy you, even if he comes before you get it done." "I've thought about doing that," he said glumly, "but

I've chopped down trees. Come on, let's start right now. I'll run and get the ax and you be selecting the trees." It wasn't so bad after that. With a definite object in mind time did not hang so heavily. Their taut nerves relaxed so that they no longer rasped each other. 

But when the logs were all felled and trimmed there still remained the task of binding them together with thews sufficiently strong to withstand the current. Pawpaw bark was the logical choice, and pawpaw trees were comparatively plentiful throughout Missouri woods; but 

Duck Island seemed to have escaped them. They hunted its whole length and breadth in a vain search. Cottonwood, hickory, walnut, pignut, red haw and maple were to be found in abundance, but no pawpaw and, even stranger, no willow. Sullenly Wayne gave over the search, sulkily he ate the supper she prepared, ignoring

SPORTING LADY 

«65» her attempts to relieve the depression. At last she too fell silent and gloomy. She felt the injustice of his palpable antagonism towards her; as if she were to blame for all his troubles. Even if she had not been here with him, the boat would in all probability have been lost, just the same. She had certainly borne her part cheerfully and willingly; and she was just as anxious to get away as he was. One certainly got to know a person, shut off together like this. Moodily she watched him as, rising abruptly, he kicked viciously at the stump serving as a stool, and entered the cave. Angrily she clattered the tin dishes together, and stalked to the bluff where in sullen rebellion she sat until the moon waned and set. 

When at last she reentered the cave, she found him sprawled crossways over the makeshift bed, and caught the fumes of liquor on his heavy breath. Repugnance swept over her. Gathering up a blanket she went out again into the fresh night air. There, spreading it over the grass, she took Moses beside her and spent the night. 

She was roused by his footsteps soon after sunrise. 

He did not notice her. With staggering steps, and carrying the jug, he disappeared in a zigzag trail through the underbrush. Much later she came across him, stretched out in a drunken stupor on the ground. The corkless jug had rolled some distance away, and picking up she saw that it was empty. it "At least there'll be no more of that," she muttered, tossing it aside. For a moment she deliberately surveyed him—the bewhiskered face, unkempt hair, the flushed bloated features. Was it possible this was the handsome fascinating man who had appeared out of the January storm eight months before? Was it this sodden wreck whose lips had so thrilled her, whose caresses had given

«» 

SPORTING LADY 

her such sensual enjoyment? Could love die—or be killed -so soon? Revulsion assailed her-and with it a slow wave of nausea. Quickly she turned and ran from the sight. It was two days before Hendrix recovered from his debauch. Two days in which he moved about like a pale glum ghost, or lay on the grass sick and still, waiting for his jagged nerves to quiet. Few words were spoken and they avoided each other. For Cora the days were endless burning monotony. Each night she slept out under the trees; each day she watched the river or tramped with Moses through the woods, her mind a blank except for the ever present wish for release. Fortunately the weather continued fine. Had it stormed or rained heavily, keeping them cooped together in the cave, the hours would have been unbearable. 

Nor did things improve perceptibly after Wayne had fully recovered. It was as if a door had closed between them. Deliberately they kept apart, meeting only for the meals, grown more frugal and distasteful. Silently each evening he came and laid down the fish he had caught, now their only means of sustenance. No longer did he help her clean and prepare them, but wandered away again smoking his corncob pipe, to sit on the river bank silhouetted against the sunset sky, until she called him to supper. Nor did they talk during the hasty meal. 

To have spoken would have meant to give way to their

SPORTING LADY 

«67 » jangled nerves. Slowly their hatred for the island, for their forced restraint, spread into an enveloping flame of hatred for each other. It was a hot still night, the middle of August. Wayne had departed towards the lower island as soon as he had gulped his supper. Cora had thrown herself face downward on the ground and for the first time had given way to tears-angry, futile, tears that came from racked nerves and lowered morale. Moses, watching in sympathy, had crept along on his stomach close to her, his little pink tongue licking at her wet face. Suddenly he sprang up, ears pointed, sympathy forgotten, as he danced about, running a little way off, then scampering back, yelping excitedly. She sat up, drying her tears with her sleeve. Then she heard voices, and ran forward. 

It was Jesse at last; and Wayne with him, laughing, his eyes sparkling; recapturing in a few moments of joy and relief some of his old charm. Her own smile was flashing, her hands outstretched to welcome their visitor. What matter that his greeting was a trifle forced, even condemnatory? What mattered anything but that they were now mercifully set free. She slipped her arm through Wayne's and felt his close about her with something of its old warmth. "So this is where you are? You sure kicked up a lot of gossip when you run away, Cora. However, I had my suspicions as to your whereabouts all the time."

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

"But you didn't tell them?" "Sure didn't. It's your own business, after all. But what are you going to do now? I've come to get your man. Are you thinking of going back home? Because if you are "I'm never goin' back." 

Jesse ignored the interruption, continued: "Because if you are, you'll find some changes. I've got bad news for you Cora."

For a moment he hesitated while their eyes questioned him. "You remember that storm the day you left?" 

She nodded silently. "Well-it struck your home. Your paw was there alone, standing on the porch. Ed and Flora had been held up in Liberty. Well-it was the lightning killed him. 'Twasn't a bad death; leastways, it was quick. And it set fire to the house. When Ed and Flora got home there wasn't nothin' left. They just left everything and come over to our house for the night, after they had hunted round for you a little. But Ed seemed to know you were gone. I seemed to, too. But the rest weren't satisfied until they had searched the ruins for your body. Most folks still think you were burned up. 

And now don't feel too bad, Cora, but you ought to know-Ed kinda led 'em on to thinkin' that. He told me you was as good as dead to him and Flora."

Cora stood very still and there was silence for a moment, while Wayne's arm pressed her closer in sympathy. 

When she spoke at last it was calmly. "Poor Paw! But as you say, it was a good way to go. 

And he hadn't long anyways." Then after a moment, "I don't mind Ed's feelin' thataway. I only wish he

SPORTING LADY 

<69 » really believed I was dead. Because as far as they are concerned I am."

Later they sat before the cave and talked and laughed. 

Jesse had a keen, if rough sense of humor, and any gossip of the outside world was of intense interest. Jesse had been away-unavoidably. His previous explanation must have satisfied Wayne who nodded affirmatively. 

He had ridden sixty miles that day, and when he learned Wayne had not showed up had got a skiff and come for him immediately. He was needed, and at once. They must leave before sunup next morning. The question was, what to do with her? He looked at Hendrix, who nodded. But to Cora there was no question about it. "I'm going over to Kansas City and wait for him to come back.' وو Already the imminence of their separation had recaptured a breath of their romance. It was almost as if the last ugly weeks had not been. "Yes," assented Wayne, "I guess that's the best thing to do. But keep away from any accidental meetings with 

Clay County folks. And afterwards we'll go away somewhere together." "By the way," said Jesse, “Ed and Flora are living in 

Kansas City." "They are? What are they doin' there?" "Well, after the place burned up they hadn't no place in Clay to live, so they sold the stock and land, and Flora is keeping house for him. Mr Gibbs the lawyer in Liberty got him a job and he's reading law with Willock and Creighton. Gettin' along fine, I hear. Well," with a big yawn, "I'm tired, folks. Got an extra blanket? 

I'll roll up out here. You'd better get to bed, too, Hendrix. We've got a long day ahead of us."

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

10 Side by side they lay wide eyed through the night, whispering, caressing, planning; yes, somehow, now, regretting their departure. Some. Even through their voiced regrets each in his heart hugged a pleasant excitement over the prospect of fresh scenes and adventures. Daring adventures for Wayne, in which keen judgment and steady nerves and, yes, a sure hand, would be balanced against danger and death. And for Cora, the pleasant adventure of life in a city, rubbing elbows with thousands of people. Streets, stores, crowds. To the country girl whose few trips across the river had ended at the market square where all day long she sold eggs and butter and green vegetables, the prospect of living every day and sleeping every night, up on those bluffs, of really being one of its fifty thousand inhabitants, was particularly alluring. And afterwards, when Wayne returned, they would take the river steamer and float down to St. Louis, and maybe even farther. On their real honeymoon. She would have beautiful clothes 

Wayne had promised her that; and they would have plenty of money, he promised her that also; and with so much to see and so many things to do in Kansas City the time would pass quickly-she promised herself that. "I'll leave you fifty dollars," he said. "It's all I can spare just now but that should see you through until I come back, or send word. And I'll give you the address of a quiet boarding house where you are to wait for me, or a message from me. You'd better stay pretty close

SPORTING LADY 

< 71 » to your room and not go gadding about; you don't want to run into Flora and Ed, or anyone from your neighborhood. If I'm not able to come myself I'll have you meet me somewhere; it all depends on how things go. 

But anyway, we'll be somewhere together the first week in September. And if things turn out like we look for we'll have plenty to see us through the winter in style." "Will it be dangerous?” "Not particularly. Not more so than usual. Now, you're not to worry about anything. Just stay quiet and lay low until you hear from me.'

So they talked and planned through the short hours, and as daylight streaked the sky, arose and made coffee. 

No time for breakfast if they were to be on their way before people were up and about. 11 Jesse was silent as they rowed in the gray dawn towards the town below the mouth of the Kaw. Cora and Moses sat facing him, in the bow of the boat. She looked unusually handsome in her best blue seersucker whose tight bodice and voluminous draped skirt accentuated the bold curves of her figure. Under the narrow brim of her bonnet, tied with black velvet ribbons, the braids of heavy hair were wound around her head, leaving two curls to fall over the shoulders, giving her a girlish look she did not always have. Youthful, too, was the flush on her tanned cheeks, the sparkle in her tawny eyes.

As his own blinking ones glanced occasionally at her, Jesse acknowledged her good looks, her vital attraction;

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

and his lips set in a stern line. He didn't like it-he didn't like it at all. Of course, it was none of his business; men didn't interfere in this sort of thing. But 

Hell! He had known Cora since she was knee high to a grasshopper; had watched her grow up. They had always felt a genuine affection for each other. And here this new man-this Kentuckian, Hendrix, had done something none of the others would have dared. If he had guessed such a thing would come about he wouldn't have brought Hendrix up, valuable though he was in other ways. No, he didn't like it a little bit. There was a streak of chivalry in Jesse James' makeup; women were in a class alone; quite apart and above the grossness and lasciviousness of men. He had always looked up to women, perhaps because he had known only decent ones -his mother, his sisters, his own dearly beloved wife,

Zee. The other kind had not interested him. 

Cora facing him, acutely conscious of his silence, sensed its cause. She caught his eye, leaned forward. "Jesse, this-between Wayne and me-it ain't what you think. We-we're goin' to be married-just as soon as he comes back. We would now if there was time. 

And the only reason we haven't before is because there wasn't an opportunity. But he means right by me, Jesse, and we really love each other. You'll see." 

Jesse's blue eyes lightened and he smiled at her. "God, Cora, I'm glad. I'd have hated it if you had turned out that kind of a woman.' دو

1 HEY landed her on a clay bank a quarter of a mile 

THE 

west of the Levee, by the Hannibal Bridge. There was no farewell demonstration; she and Wayne had said their real goodbye on the island last night. Only the firm pressure of his hands as he helped her up the steep bank, and a final wave when they pushed off. 

Cora watched them as they bent over their oars and pulled swiftly across the Missouri toward the opposite bank of Clay County, until their boat became a tiny black speck. Then picking up her bundle, and with 

Moses trailing along at the end of a rope, she started along the river bank towards the town. 

A short distance from the Levee she hesitated and paused. From the size of the crowd gathered it was evident that a steamboat was about to depart; in it there might be someone to recognize her; someone from Clay 

County, or Liberty-even, perhaps, Ed or Flora. Everyone with an excuse would be on hand to share the bustle of departure. She was not tired from her sleepless night.

On the contrary, a sharp excitement and satisfying feeling of freedom and irresponsibility stimulated her. 

She would have liked to mingle with the people, hear « 73 »

<74» 

SPORTING LADY 

the loud voices of the negroes as they trotted on board under their heavy loads; watch the finely dressed passengers leaning over the rails of the decks, calling down last messages to friends gathered to see them off; feel herself a part and parcel of the lively throng. But caution prevailed. Some other time-some fine day when she herself would be one of the chief actors in another such scene.

She had paused at a cut between high bluffs through which a dusty road ran past several houses built against them, only their top stories level with the ground above. 

This grading had been done since she was last over the river. Through the thick yellow dust she trudged ankle deep, her skirts held high to guard her only decent dress, her leg muscles straining at the unaccustomed climb. After several blocks she turned east towards 

Main and, assailed by increasing pangs of hunger, stepped boldly into a coffee house and expended fifteen cents of her newly acquired wealth for a breakfast more generous than she had ever imagined-mush, fried steak, fried potatoes, hot biscuits, batter cakes, sorghum molasses, coffee. She gorged herself, with a backward contemptuous flip of memory towards boiled catfish and hoecake; what she could not eat she fed to Moses. 

It was eight o'clock when she stepped forth, replenished and heartened. The sidewalks were filling with people going about their day's business. The street was alive with vehicles-farmers' wagons, buggies, surreys, riding horses; even a covered wagon drawn by six span of mules, its rear end bursting with bedding and children; two cows and a horse trailing by halters. As she stood staring at it the driver larruped to his teams, raising high his hands filled with reins. With creakings and lurchings they got under way, heading south to the

SPORTING LADY 

«75 » Santa Fe trail and the long, still dangerous road to California or Mexico.

Revealed by the wagon's departure a street car, mule drawn, was preparing to draw out. Cora hurried across the street and interrogated the driver, showing him the slip of paper upon which Hendrix had written an address. "Can I ride near this place?" "Sure. Hop on. I'll let you know when you get there.”

She entered the tiny car with its straw strewn floor and settled herself on a long side seat. With a jar and a rumble they started east on Fourth to Walnut, south on Walnut. Cora sat upright, interested eyes roving over the districts through which they passed. This was certainly better than walking. And how Kansas City had spread out. Everywhere something being done to it; a water main being laid; the framework of new cottages going up; hills being demolished, streets graded. Some of the homes they rolled by were quite grand. One had a fountain in the center of the large green yard behind white pickets, and from the upraised beak of a crane spouted a small stream of water, sparkling in a sun already grown hot. She knew Kansas City had had a water system for a year now, but this was her first view of its workings. What wouldn't they think of next! 

Once they stopped for passengers—a little girl hugging a wax doll, and her mother, elegant in pink sprigged dimity freshly laundered to pasteboard stiffness. 

The ruffled polonaise was elevated at the back by a huge bustle, and her trim basque was unbelievably small at the waist. Suspended from a black velvet ribbon around her neck hung a black enamel cross set in rose stones.

Mitts covered the hands which clasped a bright parasol,

<» 

SPORTING LADY 

and on one side of her head was perched a small tiptilted sailor with loops of black and white plaid ribbon which hung to the left shoulder. Cora gathered that they were going to "spend the day" with friends. She gazed at her enviously. Her own old-fashioned chin-tied bonnet and hot seersucker dress seemed unutterably shabby.

The street car turned aside onto a switch and the driver descended and lighted his corncob pipe as he lolled on the grass beside the track and waited for the town bound car to pass. Cora was glad for the additional time. She was taking mental notes of everything about the costume of the lady opposite the cut of the polonaise, the glass buttons of the basque, the smart twist of the bows on the sailor. For she had made up her mind that her first investment out of the fifty dollars would be for material; and her time of waiting should be partly spent in fashioning for herself another such dress as the one she was admiring. It was to be a surprise for Wayne. He would think her gorgeous in such clothes; and she would wear them first when they went aboard the Joseph Kinney for their honeymoon trip to St Louis. 2 At Tenth Street the driver drew in his mules and called back to her through the open door. "Here you are, Madam. Walk one block east. Your house is one of them up on the bluff.”

SPORTING LADY 

«77» walk until at the next street she stood facing several boxlike frame houses perched high upon rain-scarred clay banks. Narrow steps with spindly railings led up to the porches and Cora walked slowly along looking for her number. Apparently there was none, but at the further house was a white sign on which was crudely painted ROOMS AND BOARD. She climbed some half a hundred steps and pausing to rest at the top looked out over a panorama of the city. What a big place it was! Towards the west, block after block stretched on toward the bluffs above the "Bottoms." 

That bluff she knew from hearsay was called Quality

Hill and held the homes of the wealthy. Magnificent mansions of ten or twelve rooms with spreading grounds that overlooked the whole western skies, with carriage houses and croquet grounds; lighted by gas and heated by furnaces. Homes built by pioneers who had come west when the Town of Kansas was Westport Landing, outfitting place for prairie schooners during the Gold 

Rush of '49; men active in the trade with Mexico before the railroads came; men who had migrated into the wilderness from St Louis, Kentucky, Ohio, trekking in wagons, steaming up the Mississippi and Missouri to this outpost of the wilderness at the junction of the Kaw. 

They had looked upon this fertile land with vision, and had found it good. They had planted them a city and had thrown stout hearts into the task of making it grow. And now, less than thirty years after its organization, it spread around her for many blocks in every direction; with fifty thousand people living together harmoniously, all concerned heart and soul in making the sprawling ugly town the Metropolis of the plains. 

She counted the church spires rising into the still hot 

August air-six, eight, ten; more probably behind the

houses to the east. Her eyes trailed over the blocks of squat buildings cut through by narrow streets and came to rest on the river. The steamer she had seen earlier was pulling out into midstream. A toy boat. Across the river, strangely narrow from this distance, spanned by the new Hannibal Bridge, she could see the wooded banks of Clay County. And somewhere back in the country beyond her vision, Wayne and Jesse, and perhaps Frank and others, were trotting along the country roads southward and eastward toward the scene of their next activities. West Virginia, she had gathered from guarded talk. They had not confided in her, but she was not dumb; she had deduced more than Wayne and

They were going to need a lot of money to carry out her aspirations; and she hoped there would be enough to allow Wayne a long vacation from danger. She wanted his companionship intensely at this moment, here at the top of the world gazing over the city full of strangers. Never had she felt so lonely. And suddenly she felt very tired. Her eyelids drooped wearily. There had been no sleep all last night. But now she had nothing to do but sleep. No housework. No dishes. Nothing but rest. She flung herself across the hot crazy quilt on the bed and let her lids close. A fly buzzed on the window pane and she listened to it drowsily for a few minutes. She ought to undress—at least take off her shoes. While she was considering the effort she slept. 3 со During the days that followed the sultry August heat was unbroken by showers and Cora found her worst fears for the west room verified. After the sun had crossed the meridian and began to find its way into the window, it was like a bake oven. She rose and breakfasted at a long, red-clothed table with half a dozen men, uncouth as to table manners, and generally distasteful. After a first introduction by Mrs Watson she met them with a curt "good morning," and applied herself exclusively to her food, leaving the table as soon as she had finished. After the first day's glimmer of

SPORTING LADY 

« 81 » interest the men too ignored the big handsome woman and felt a distinct relief when she quitted the dining room.

In the several weeks she lived there she exchanged words with no one except the ever inquisitive and bustling landlady who popped in and out of her room upon the slightest excuse. Cora was not averse to this. 

Knowing practically nothing of the big town near which she had always lived, she questioned idly and learned from Mrs Watson's voluble replies much about it—its politics and petty gossip, the history and social status of its prominent citizens. Mrs Watson had lived in Kansas 

City for some thirty years-when it was known as Westport Landing and extended only along the water front. Her mother had been a cook, and she herself had done "chamber work" in the old Gillis House on the Levee. It was there she had met and married Lem Watson, an engineer on a side wheeler. He had made good money-$125 a month-and they saved a lot of it, for, with him being away so much, she kept on with her own work. And it was well that she did, for on one of his runs the boilers burst and they never did find Lem's remains. In the depression after the War she had bought this place dirt cheap and had managed to make a good living, and put enough by to see her through her old age. During the comparative coolness of the mornings, Cora worked on the new dress, which progressed rapidly. She

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

had found just the material she wanted at John Doggett's, and Mrs Watson helped her with the fittings. In a week it was finished, the sailor hat trimmed, and both pinned away in newspapers, and she began counting the days until Wayne should return. With her dressmaking over, there was little to occupy her; for she followed his advice and spent but little time down town. Evenings after supper, when the railings of the porch were cluttered with her fellow lodgers and her room was too stifling to enter until late, she and Moses walked through the residence sections, and sometimes even ventured as far as Quality Hill, strolling admiringly past the mansions set in spacious grounds that crowned the western bluffs.

But her idleness was pleasing. After a lifetime of farm labor these long hours in which to do as she pleased, the meals which she was served, instead of serving, the time to devote to her personal appearance, were enjoyable. Already she thought of herself as a lady. Sitting on the high perched porch, empty of a morning, her dreamy eyes wandered over the western panorama unseeing, as she drew pictures of her future life beginning, now, so shortly. A life of comfort and travel and real hotels. As Mrs. Wayne Hendrix. She erased from her mind the grosser memories of the drunken bewhiskered man her lover had become during those last weeks; resolutely she remembered him as he was when first they met-dark, handsome, ardent.

Sometimes she longed for those caresses to which she had become accustomed. During the long hot nights tossing on her lonely bed, she conjured him beside her, laxed herself into his phantom arms. She did not question if she loved him; it was enough that his mere image could evoke such manifestations of desire. To her he

SPORTING LADY 

«83 » personified luxury, as pictured by her ambitious imaginings; and pleasure, provoked by his presence. She felt herself growing, expanding, in this new environment; becoming a mate of whom he would be proud. For long periods she gazed at the reflection in her mirror, noting and approving the fine textured skin, the well modelled features, the tawny, heavy-lidded eyes, the vigorous coarse black hair. When she undressed at night she stretched her voluptuous body to its full stature, standing naked before the bureau, striving to catch its entire magnificent height. Her hands, so coarsened by toil, became to her a symbol of her future. Upon them she spent hours daily, trimming nails and broken cuticle, bathing them in rain water and scented soap, massaging them with glycerine and tallow, sleeping in gloves, until the nails and palms grew smooth and rosy, the texture soft and white. As she beheld this evidence of ladyhood emerge the pride in her hands increased. She posed them before the mirror, waved them about in airy gestures, practiced at crooking the little fingers; and resolved that never again would she subject them to the indignity of manual labor. August crawled in sullen heat towards September, the time Wayne had promised to come or to send word.

Each day now she purchased the Journal and read eagerly through its news. For a fresh raid by the James 

Gang would be news here in Missouri even though it was occurring in West Virginia. The days that here-

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

tofore had moved along tranquilly now became surcharged with restlessness and suppressed excitement; days to be lived through somehow from one issue of the paper to the next. She paced her room, walked 

Moses for hours in unfrequented neighborhoods, redoubled her care of hair and hands and skin; tried and re-tried the new clothes-and sought out Mrs Watson with feverish conversation and questions. One day she ventured to ask her if she knew a Mr Hendrix. "Hendrix? Here, in Kansas City? I don't think so. 

What's his first name?" "Wayne Wayne Hendrix. Very tall-dark-goodlooking." "No-can't say as I've ever heard the name." 

It must be as she had guessed. If Wayne ever had lodged here it was under an assumed name. Perhaps he had only heard about it as a quiet place for a person to hide. 

On the second day of September her avid thirst for news was satisfied. A black headline on the front page focussed her eyes. She hurried past Mrs Watson rocking on the porch sewing balls of carpet rags; with Moses scampering at her heels fled to her room and, snapping forward the catch, read the account for which she had been waiting. 

On September first four men on horse-back, clad in long linen dusters, had ridden up to the bank of Huntington, West Virginia. While two had entered, their

SPORTING LADY 

« 85» companions remained in their saddles holding the riderless horses, firing pistols in the air to clear the streets.

Inside, one of the robbers had covered the cashier and a citizen while the other compelled the cashier to open the bank safe and cash drawer, collecting several thousands of dollars. Stuffing the money into a wheat sack they rushed out whistling to their comrades who brought up the led horses and, all firing pistols, dashed out into the country. A posse was being gathered to pursue them.

Cora's hands were cold and wet but her heart was beating with elation as she read. Only four men! So much the better. The James robberies were usually undertaken by six or seven; and four meant a bigger share for Wayne. He'd probably get at least a thousand, and what a grand time they could have on that. For half a year, perhaps. The forming of the posse was nothing to worry about. There was always a posse; and the Gang always got away. They didn't go into a thing like this without first familiarizing themselves with every road and trail in the vicinity; without having perfect plans for a getaway. Everyone knew that.

So now the waiting time was almost past. A few days to let things blow over-a little time to enable Wayne to ride back to her or, if inadvisable, to send word where to meet him. Then the end of the old life and the beginning of a new one. She spoke to the recumbent 

Moses, bewhiskered chin on paws, beady black eyes watching her every move: "We're going away, Moses. On a big boat. We're going to travel, and have a grand time. Your master's coming, darling. You haven't forgotten him, have you? 

And him will buy Cora's baby great big beef steaks and

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

ribbons for him's curls, and we'ums'll be such a happy little family. Won't you like that, Toodle'ums?"

Moses, sensing her excitement, pawed ecstatically with his front feet, sprang to her lap and licked her face, his bright eyes snapping, as if he too understood their release from this humdrum, confining existence. 

CHAPTER SIX 

1 

OMBRE days, with cloud banks spitting rain.

Through muddy roads and wooded trails, over the mountains of Virginia into those of Kentucky, rode four horsemen, pushing their weary nags to the limit of their endurance. Long ago, back in the hills of Virginia, they had left their own blooded horses. Two had been shot under them in pistol fights with the closely pursuing posses; the others, carrying double, had sunk with fatigue. Twice they had raided isolated farms securing new mounts which had likewise foundered under the brutal strain to which they had been subjected. 

Now the ones bearing them were lame and heaving, and in the mountain fastnesses to which they had escaped after definitely throwing off all pursuers, there was small chance of securing fresh mounts.

It was ten days since the Huntington bank robbery. 

Days of stealthy slipping through tangled forests and nights of swifter progress over rough mountain roads.

SPORTING LADY 

«87» For the first week posses had been close on their trail; not one, but half a dozen. Twenty men, well mounted and armed, had ridden forth close behind them when they shot their way out of Huntington. At various points these had been joined by others. When one bunch of man hunters had lost the trail, others had picked it up. Sometimes they had come so close that pistol duels had taken place. But by rare good luck, the raiders had always managed to elude the enemy. Now for three days there had been no evidence of pursuit and for the first time they relaxed their vigilance. In another week they would be in sanctuary. Friends in eastern Kentucky were expecting them; plans for their final escape to safety were formulated and awaited only their arrival to be put into execution. Now, at last, they could snatch a few hours' sleep, and rest their weary horses.

They were silent as they pushed forward through the misty morning light. Their faces were drawn and haggard, their eyes, shifting from side to side of the roadway on the lookout for a spot that promised shelter from the rain and concealment from chance travellers, were glazed and heavy from loss of sleep. Their stomachs rumbled and gnawed with hunger. 

The tall man in advance raised his hand, pointed forestward, and plunged his horse down the mountain side. The flea-bitten nag of the shortest one rolled its eyes, sprawled its trembling legs, sighed deeply and sank to the ground. Its rider slid from his seat, unbuckled saddle and bridle and, exerting his strength, rolled the fallen animal down the steep hillside out of the sight of passersby. A small bay mare, catching a foot in tangled vines, crashed down, its leg snapping under. It lay screaming with pain until with a muttered curse the rider drew his revolver and ended its agony.

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

"Well, boys, it looks like we'll be travelling shanks mare for awhile. These plough nags won't even carry single another day, much less double. But what the 

Hell! Me for eight hours of sleep." 29

In a log cabin standing in a small clearing, two young mountaineers bent over a copy of a Louisville paper, reading by aid of stubby forefinger and flickering candle. The wife of one, spinning in a corner, listened intently as word by word her husband spelled out the story of the man hunt. Finished, he pounded the paper with his fist and spoke: "Ah tell yuh, Bill, those bank robbahs is headed thisaway, and we'd jes' bettah be ready foh 'em." "Ye-ah, I reckon you'se right. We'd bettah load foh big game. This ain't goin' to be no Sunday School picnic if they finds themselves in this veecinity." "Now, boys, don' you all go and bite off more'n you kin chaw. Them is desprit men and they ain't goin' to take no nonsense off'n a par of mountain cats like you all.” "You hold your tongue and tend to yo' knittin', Het.

This is a man size job an' no advice asked." 

From above the fireplace they took down two old army muskets which they proceeded to clean and load with slugs, working with a paucity of words and a kindling inner tension. Satisfied at last as to the reliability of their firearms they stepped out into the cold

SPORTING LADY 

« 89 » mountain drizzle, picked their way down the zigzag path to the rough road and, secreting themselves in underbrush through which they commanded a long view of the upward trail, began their watch. 3 For four days the brothers kept their vigil; in the daytime prowling through the adjacent forest or lying hidden in the thickets; at night keeping a close lookout along the road. In vain Het sought to dissuade them by jibes and entreaties. With the dogged stubbornness of their kind, fostered by the possibility of glory and a large reward, the Dillons kept at their appointed task to the neglect of woodchopping and farm work. But even they were beginning to tire and to wonder sheepishly if they were making fools of themselves. Weary and short tempered from loss of sleep, they had about decided to give over their quest and restore the guns to the pegs on which they had rested these many years. 

This night, they agreed, would be their last.

It was. Shortly after midnight, from their place of concealment they heard the breaking of twigs in the woods that lay behind them. Stealthily they crept forward as four figures came quietly into the clearing, making their way towards the barn. The rain clouds. had passed and in the bright moonlight could be plainly seen the bulge of pistols at waistlines. From their covert the mountaineers saw them hesitate, then separate, two of them making for the road. The others advanced

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

towards the watchers and when within ten yards Bill, covered by a tree trunk, called out: "Halt! Throw down yoah guns!"

Two pistol shots rang out, two muskets blazed; the intruders, dashing to cover, disappeared within the black shadows of the woods. 

A frightened Het was waiting as they came in sight of the cabin door. With satisfaction they related their prowess. "And," said Bill, "Ah's sahtin I hit one of 'em." "Me too," said his brother, "Ah'm as sure as shootin'." "Wall, mebbe one of you did then," sniffed Het. "An' now Ah hope you'll come on to bed and git some sleep. Ah haven't had a decent night's sleep since you two tu'ned vigilants.”

But they were too wary to be caught napping at this stage of the game. Those others might return; it was more dangerous than before. So alternately they dozed and watched and at break of day took themselves to the scene of battle. Blood spattered the ground and following its tiny trail, growing more clearly visible with each step, they came across their victim lying between rows of a cornfield a hundred yards distant. In his side was a great gaping wound where the slugs had done their deadly work. His face, under its growth of black beard, was waxen white, the lids closed. He still breathed in faint convulsive gasps; and the brothers, silent now and a little grim, lifted his shoulders and feet and lugged him to the cabin. Het met them, her hands twisting nervously under her apron. "Well, now I hope yo' ah satisfied. Po' man. And so purty, too. Bring him in and then go for a doctah, one of you man-huntahs."

SPORTING LADY 

« 91 » The dark eyes opened, pain racked and dull, and stared up at her as if seeking a familiar face. The tight white lips moved— "I'm-I'm dying. Where are my friends?” 

Bill bent over him: "What's you' friends' names?" he asked. "It-none of your-God damned-business."

The effort took the last shreds of his strength. Wearily the dark eyes closed, there was one moment of twisted anguish on his face, then the head dropped forward and breath ceased.

Tears came into Het's eyes and dropped on her ample bosom. She folded the long slim hands over his breast. "An' now Ah hopes you all's satisfied," she said grimly. 4 Cora, reading the account of the capture, refused to believe; denied vehemently, and with low curses flowing strangely from set lips, that the dark bearded man might be her lover-even that the James Boys had had a hand in the robbery. Over and over she read and strove to believe the skepticism voiced by the newspaper -that the James Boys did not work so far from home; that the robbery in all probability was committed by a Kentucky mountain gang which had recently terrorized Eastern Kentucky and West Virginia. Even though she knew they had headed for that region she forced herself to believe that they had been forestalled. The dark

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

hours dragged by with her brain hammering down doubt and the small voice of conviction, which persisted despite her reiterated assertions to herself. 

And then, two days later, the highly sentimentalized account of the return of three strangers to the log cabin where, resting in a rude casket supported on the backs of two chairs, the slain bandit lay. Mrs Dillon alone watched with the remains as the men entered and demanded that the lid of the coffin be unnailed. They had gazed silently at the still face and one of them had wept openly. Then they had courteously bidden the frightened woman goodbye, and she had watched them walking away through the corn field. 

Several days later Cora received an envelope, addressed to the name she and Wayne had agreed she should assume Mrs Kate Black. Within, a piece of black crêpe. 5 Now that it was all over, now that all doubt was dispelled, it was not so bad as she had anticipated during those hours of uncertainty. The cataclysmic crashing of her future stunned her into a stoicism that dulled the pain she might have felt under a less drastic blow. The immediate need to devise ways and means of living through the coming months pushed into the background the grief she might have felt under softer circumstances.

The three weeks' board she had paid and the money

SPORTING LADY 

«93 » she had expended for the finery which would never now delight Wayne's eyes had made serious inroads into the sum he had given her. She had left less than twenty dollars. It was imperative that she get a job at once. 

For some time now, even before Wayne had left, she had suspected that she was pregnant. She could not be sure. She had never talked of such things with other women, nor even with Wayne, and was curiously ignorant of such matters. But recurring symptoms had vaguely worried her. At first she ascribed them to the drenching she had received the night of the storm; but when certain functions continued to disappoint, instinct told her that all was not as it should be. Adroitly contrived conversation with Mrs Watson convinced her. 

She was to bear Wayne's posthumous and illegitimate child unless-unless there was some way to prevent. 

Even at this late day.

Help from Mrs Watson was out of the question. 

Their intimate talk had plainly revealed Mrs Watson's prim and narrow convictions. There was only one thing to do; she and Moses must find themselves a cheaper room; she must get work; and she must make inquiries from someone capable of giving the information she needed. Later-when Jesse came back, she must manage somehow to get word to him and demand Wayne's share in the haul.

But she was destined never to meet Jesse again; Missouri did not see the Boys until ten months later when details of a particularly bold robbery in Cooper County headlined the papers. Then silence. Then the debâcle at 

Northfield, Minnesota, and the breakup of the gang.

After that, wanderings far from Missouri; three years' peace while "Mr Howard" lived a quiet family life in Tennessee, while Clay County wondered about its fa-

mous bandits; wondered until 1882 when the newspapers shrieked out their farewells to Jesse.

CHAPTER SEVEN 

1 WEEK later Cora was dishwasher in the Bon- Ton Café. Untrained in the finer points of housework she had barely considered such employment. Then, too, there was Moses. No one would accept a hired girl with a dog. And she was set on keeping him with her; he was the only living thing she had left now. Here at the Bon-Ton she got her meals and plenty of good scraps for him. They lived in a small odorous room above the kitchen, for which she paid four dollars a month out of her wages of eighteen. They could manage nicely on that; even save money. There was always a newspaper left behind sometime during the day. Reading that through, and taking Moses for his constitutional after her work was over at nine o'clock, comprised her relaxation. The work was hard and disagreeable, and weather continued warm for September. Some days, when business was unusually brisk, particularly Saturdays, her legs would ache with fatigue and her feet swell from standing, so that she could scarcely drag herself out for Moses' evening walk. But she never neglected him, however tired she might be.

There was lots of time to think, as she slushed the

SPORTING LADY 

«95 » stacks of china boats in the strong soapy water. But, because her thoughts only induced worry, she shirked them, made her mind a blank, moved as an automaton. 

Something would turn up-something had to. Perhaps she had been wrong in her surmises. Anyway, she was too tired at night to make any effort to remedy matters. 

It was not until that morning when she noticed for the first time the change in her figure that sparks of life returned to her dulled brain. The old basque in which she worked would scarcely fasten. It was true, then. And if anything was to be done, she must see about it quickly.

She had learned things from the uncensored papers over which she poured. Every issue contained one or more salacious items, colorful and detailed as if the writer had secretly savored before giving utterance to them. Stories of "girls gone wrong; inmates of brothels." "Same old story of Love, Seduction, and 

Ruin!" "Killed Offspring! Army of 40 in Pursuit! Will

Lynch!" "The Wages of Sin:-A Terrible Picture of 

Life on the East Levee." Then there were the victims of abortions-all of them described as refined, and ladies born. Only last night there had been a black headline at the top of the front page: 

SARAH'S SIN. 

Discovery of the Den of a Female Abortionist on Main Street.

All day long, slushing the suds about with an aroused nervous energy, she wondered where it was; what the cost; how dangerous. But that didn't matter. She wasn't afraid.

The tired stupor that heretofore had claimed her after her day's work was replaced this night by restlessness

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

and desire for action. She felt stimulated and energetic. 

From the closet shelf she took down the carefully pinned parcel containing the new dress she had never worn. 

Soon, perhaps, she would not be able to. She brushed and rearranged her hair, patted her face with a starch bag, and donned the finery. Two buttons would not fasten. No matter. She placed the little sailor hat over her heavy braids at a perky angle and surveyed herself with satisfaction. The effect was really stunning. Even the shadows under her curious yellow eyes were becoming. She'd wear the outfit every evening now; get some good out of it while she could. If only her hands—ruefully she looked at them, such a short while ago rosy and smooth, now red and bitten by lye and grease. But that, too, would pass. Funny how confident and encouraged fine clothes made one feel.

She buckled his collar on the eager, waiting Moses and they went down the rickety stairs into the gas-lit streets. 2 It must have been ten o'clock. They had walked out into the suburbs and Moses had had his run. Now coming down Main Street towards their lodging, the dog subdued and quiet at her heels, she sauntered slowly, loathe to go inside; pausing to look in shop windows, catching reflections of her transformed self in the glass. Cora liked her; liked the twinkling blue eyes, the sweet voice, the generous maternal bulk of her. No woman had ever talked so kindly to her before. her lover's name and occupation. But she related quite simply that she had gone away with him, that he had departed on business before she knew she was in a family way, and that she had just received word that he was dead. She told it dispassionately, feeling relaxed and at ease; confident that she was at the end of her troubles and, in relief at the thought, ready to pay the price for mental and financial security that her practical brain told her would be expected of her. Nor was there horror in the thought of what she felt, rather than knew, that price would be. This color and softness and comfort, which hitherto she had only guessed existed, intrigued her; the gaieties and pleasures hidden behind the closed doors below piqued her curiosity. Her veins pulsed at the thought that she, Cora Simmons of Clay County, might some day be a part of that curious, enticing world; gorgeously clad in perfumed velvets and satins; released from the kitchen and dishpan forever. Her amber eyes glowed. 

Madam was frowning, her lips pursed in reflection. "But, child, you have waited too long. Over four months, you say. It's too dangerous. I can't be mixed up in anything like that."

The light faded from Cora's face, leaving her eyes tragic. She wet her dry lips, twisted her hands nervously in Moses' white fur, stared through the silence that fell. 

As her hopes went crashing, the bright pictures which a moment before had raced across her mind became the only thing in life worth while. Madam pursed her lips, frowned, considered deeply.

« 105 » that the details of it must be learned from experience, the general aspect was not unfamiliar. She could not remember just when she had first learned that there existed a class of women who lived by purveying to the grosser natures of men. But she had known of it long before she had entered womanhood, and had always felt curious about them.

There had been the time when she was about twelve -a lowering autumn day. Some of Ed's acquaintances from Liberty had ridden over. They had liquor with them and, though Ed did not drink, not that she ever knew, he had led them to the barn loft. She was a tall girl even then, as tall as a woman; and she had been disappointed when he had taken the fellows off to the barn. She wanted to be around them, have them pull at her pigtails, tease her; wanted to feel the stimulating warmth that always came over her in the presence of strange young men. She had sulked a little while, then had crept quietly to the foot of the ladder and listened. 

Their voices were low, but not too low for her sharp ears. One of them was reading from a book or pamphlet; revealing, bawdy things that made her blush, alone with herself. They chuckled together, slapped at each other, swigged from the bottle; interrupted the reading to relate experiences of their own. Cora's heart had thumped and she had felt strange and excited. After that she had never been quite as she was before she had listened to the things she had been ashamed of hearing, and yet, somehow glad that she had heard. It was as if certain portions of her brain had awakened and, thereafter, dwelt secretly, sometimes ecstatically, sometimes morbidly, upon the strange things revealed that day. Or half revealed. Disclosed just sufficiently to quicken her

SPORTING LADY 

imagination and pique her curiosity. And though she sometimes felt soiled at the thoughts thus engendered, the elation never died. 

It was then that she had lost her respect for Ed; questioned his Sunday School and church activities; resented his opinionated remarks and his watchfulness and criticism of herself. It was after that also, that she began to read with some degree of understanding, certain news items in the papers and find subtle interpretations of them. The conversation of the young men had given her a key to their meaning. The reading matter gave her additional food for thought. 

But it was not until those nights on the island, when she had lain in Wayne's arms and they had talked in the dark, that she had received a composite picture of that half world toward which her interest had long been directed. Wayne had explained things to her, laughing at her ignorance, enlightening her as a husband might. He had told her stories of women, from Denver to Louisville; some sordid, some magnificent. He had been casual about them. Matter of fact. Understanding them and their viewpoints, he explained them as human beings, without contempt.

One woman, of all of those Wayne knew, had most aroused Cora's interest. Her name was Kate Clark, and she conducted an especially fine house in St Louis. She had been the mistress of the famous Quantrill who upon his death had willed her the quite considerable fortune accumulated through his outlawry. It was with this legacy that she had set up her establishment in St Louis, had grown notorious, had waxed prosperous. Wayne had been often to see her; every time he was in St Louis. "Kate was a wonderful woman, with a fine business

SPORTING LADY 

< 107 » head. But hard-hard as nails." Those had been his words. She had remembered them exactly.

It was on this basic knowledge of the life now offered her that Cora was making her decision. If, in the first few days after her visit to Annie Carter, she had moments of doubt and hesitation, the weeks that followed erased them all. Supporting a weary body that grew increasingly heavy and awkward as the days dragged on; standing for hours on aching puffy feet, over the pans of soapsuds; viewing with sagging spirits the hundreds of small dishes awaiting her drubbing; inspecting with disgust the ever coarsening and reddening hands with their broken, bitten nails-hands that seemed to grow larger and thicker the more they were immersed in the labor that meant food and shelter for herself and Moses, Cora yearned with ever increasing desire for the promised release from this drudgery. Annie Carter's house, viewed from the stuffy, airless room wherein she worked, took on an added brilliance and glamour. Both her mind and her body-her coarsened, clumsy body which in time might again achieve symmetry and beauty -clamored for the comfort which, if the Madam kept her word, would be hers. 

If Annie Carter kept her word! As time crawled by, and impatience and longing increased, there grew a haunting fear. Now that she wished it so fiercely, the very strength of her desire shook her confidence. She was no longer handsome. Her face was sallow, its rich color faded out, its bony surface marked by splotches. 

Black circles lay under eyes no longer strange and arresting, merely dull. She was now unable to wear the modish dress fashioned in August, even had the season permitted; instead, a mother hubbard's ugly folds

SPORTING LADY 

wrapped round her; and when she walked out with

Common sense warned her not to show herself to Annie Carter under such distressing conditions, but one despondent night Cora had not heeded it. Miss Annie had been cold and disinterested and had hurried her away with an admonition not to come again until she presented a better appearance. Cora remembered her every word, with pangs that bit into her very soul. "My God!" she had exclaimed. "I'd never have known you. Even if you are in a family way you shouldn't let yourself go like that.' دو "But Madam," Cora had protested, "I have to work so hard. I just can't do anything to help." "That's a poor way for any woman with ambition to feel. Now listen, Kate, you mustn't come here again. 

Suppose some of my clientèle should see you? Hell, I'm not running a baby farm. Now you sneak out the back way, and you stay out. Mebbe, after you're out of this mess we can come to terms. It all depends on how presentable you are then. Now, I don't mean to be unkind, but I've got my business interests to look after. And my girls have always been the swellest in the city. This is no charity organization. I've got to look after the welfare of my house. Now you run along, and come back in March and we'll talk it over. No, I haven't no time to discuss it, I should be downstairs right now. 

Take the back stairway and be very careful you're not seen." 

That was all. No offer of help; no further mention of advancing money to tide over the last weeks. That was why she was so fearful; that was why Annie Carter's house, in retrospect, took on such an enticing glamour;

SPORTING LADY 

< 109 » and why entrance there, now, spelled the only future she could face.

CHAPTER EIGHT 

1 FLORA locked the front door of the small frame cottage and stepped out into the snow storm. It had begun in the night and the ground was already thickly covered. She sniffed the freshness of the cold air and raised her face to feel the soft white flakes upon it, tangling in her long lashes. Soon her little round fur cap and tippet were covered. 

Flora had improved greatly in the seven months she and Ed had lived in the city. Her figure had rounded into soft young curves and her cheeks were pink and plump. Even Ed, who apparently had eyes for nothing but the law books he brought home to pore over until long after midnight, had noticed of late the brightness of her eyes, the rosy clearness of her skin, and the sparkle that had touched up her personality.

She turned into Main Street-a white, crisp Main Street, with icicles hanging from the wooden roofs that covered its sidewalks-and sauntered slowly, pausing to look into show windows bright with their Christmas decorations. The crowd that hurried along the narrow sidewalks, laden with bundles, was gay with the holiday spirit. Evergreen festooned the entrances of Bullene's

« IIO »

and Doggett's and their windows were filled with diamond powdered cotton providing a background for the miscellaneous assortment of toys. In Bullene's a jovial red-coated Santa Claus, half emerged from a painted brick chimney, waved greetings to the eager children flattening their noses against the glass. She had not yet bought Ed's present, although it lacked only two days until Christmas. It was such fun looking things over, trying to decide; she strung out her shopping trying to make the purchasing last as long as possible. She was also knitting him wristlets and a scarf, and turned in to match the wool. There was still an hour to kill before court would be over.

Every day now for two weeks she had stopped in at the law offices where Ed acted as clerk while reading law. At first he had been surprised at her regular appearances, but had accepted her explanation of loneliness, and had allowed her to sit on the extra chair in his tiny cubicle looking over the afternoon paper; had gone about his business leaving her to her own devices. 

Over the top of the paper in which she was seemingly absorbed she could steal quick glances through the glass which separated his office from the adjoining one of the junior partner, Mark Creighton. Sometimes their eyes met and held for a moment before she demurely dropped hers. Occasionally he came to the door, ostensibly to speak to Ed, and greeted her cordially, his eyes lingering admiringly. Sometimes, the desk was closed, his swivel chair empty, and although she prolonged her visit until Ed hinted broadly at her departure, he did not reappear. 

Such days were lost days.

But yesterday had been most satisfying. Creighton had been sitting with his face turned towards the glass partition, elbows on chair arms, chin resting on inter-

SPORTING LADY 

« III » laced fingers. His gaze was absorbed "thinking," she surmised, "about some law suit." She stood outside the door unperceived, admiring the coal-black shiny waves of hair above a high, square forehead, the strong straight nose and handsome thin-lipped mouth. Ed was absent; she could look to her heart's content. But too soon his eyes came back from far off and caught hers before she could pull them away; caught, too, the blush that ran fiery red to neck and hair line. His chair scraped back and he stood beside her, drew out the chair, talked of the weather. Then, "I missed you yesterday," and "Ed's lucky to have such a ray of sunshine around him all the time”; and something about brightening up these dull old law offices; then-"I'll be in court tomorrow until four."

That was why she was lingering on Main Street. Ed didn't like her walking the streets, but this was 

Christmas. And it wasn't yet four.

The office was on Third. Four blocks away. She'd just be in time. She buried her hands in her little round muff and walked slowly along sniffing the snow storm, feeling the flakes melt on her cheeks, pleasantly excited at the impending meeting. For he had meant to see her today; his eyes had said so though his lips had only said, "I'll be in court tomorrow until four." For no reason at all, except for her to so time her visit. 

She had passed through and past the holiday shoppers. 

This part of the street was comparatively deserted. Only a tall black garbed woman leading a small woolly white dog-white even against the snow. He was a cute little thing with a bright ribbon bow on his collar. The woman stood gazing into the festooned window of a butchers display-sirloins and strung sausages, halves of beef. unskinned deer, wild birds and game and ruffled

SPORTING LADY 

bronze-necked turkeys. The little dog stood with his front feet pressed against the glass barking at the dead animals within. Flora stooped to pat his fluffy head and started to speak to his mistress. But the words died on her lips as she stared for one awful moment into the haggard face of her sister. For one brief moment, drawn back from her brooding thoughts, Cora failed to recognize the girl beside her-the pretty, pretty girl with the small pointed face and fair hair curling in tendrils against the black fur cap. And when she did the girl had sprung away. She could see her, almost running down the street, around the corner. 

Breathless and pale, Flora appeared at Ed's door. He looked up, startled. "What's the matter, Flo? What's happened?”

She sank into the chair and raised tear-filled eyes, deep with tragedy, to his. Her voice was a tense whisper: "Ed! I've seen Cora!" 

He frowned darkly. “Where?” "On the street. Just a couple of blocks away. Oh God, Oh God, what are we going to do?" The wail in her voice became audible, shrill. "Ssh! Do you want the whole office to hear you? Be quiet. We'll talk about it tonight." For a moment his chin sank, silence weighed between them; then with set teeth, "Damn her!”

Flora was the one to hear the brisk step down the hall, and dabbed her eyes hastily with her handkerchief. 

Creighton paused in the doorway, filling it with his tall bulk, bringing in a breath of cold air. "Good afternoon, Miss Flora. You're just the young lady I want to see. My sister has commissioned me to ask if you and Ed can come to a Christmas Eve party she is having. It's rather a sudden affair so she isn't

SPORTING LADY 

« 113 » sending out invitations. I hope you haven't a previous engagement." 

Ed's set face relaxed and he looked pleased. "Why, no. We can go, can't we, Flora?"

Flora hesitated, raised her eyes to Creighton's, dropped them. His own were eager. "Come, now, Miss Flora, don't disappoint us. Won't you please give us the pleasure of your company?" 

Flora smiled graciously. "That is kind of your sister. We shall be very pleased to attend.” "That's fine. At seven o'clock then? Now I must get back to court. They haven't adjourned yet, but I stole a little time thinking you might be here. And I wanted to invite you personally." 

Brother and sister stared at each other. "That's the first time," said Ed, "that anyone has recognized us socially. We're getting on, Sis. Just you wait. You'll be mingling with Quality Hill if you stick to me."

Flora smiled slyly to herself; well she knew the reason for the invitation. But her elation was short-lived. She turned a stormy face to her brother. وو "Ed! She has to bob up just now, when-when- "Now, don't you worry about that. We'll talk that over later."
