"There, don't cry, Flora. What'll people think if they see you? You go on home and quit worrying. I'll figure out if there's something can be done.” "Just now! Just when we are getting into society." "For God's sake shut up! Go on before someone sees you making a spectacle of yourself." 

But after she had gone he sat with brooding eyes, tasting of the disgrace that was touching them. "Damn her-damn her! She'll get out of this town or I'll know the reason why!" he groaned. 2 Cora supposed her child would be born around the first of March. She had not seen a doctor; it seemed an unnecessary waste of money. Briefly, with no waste of words, she had answered the unspoken suspicions of her employer, giving the old excuse-accidental death of her husband. He did not believe her and she did not care. But she did her work and did not come in contact with his customers and he did not discharge her as she had been fearing he would. The waiters who fed the dirty stacks of dishes to her pans were too rushed during their hours of service to indulge in conversation; besides, they were negroes who respected her glum silence and detachment from her surroundings. 

Deprived of his long walks, Moses grew fat and lazy.

Now Cora only took him down into the back yard. Her evenings were spent in listlessly fashioning a few garments for the child whose coming so complicated her life. Working on the tiny clothes by the dull coal oil

SPORTING LADY 

<< IIS » lamp, she wondered at her lack of maternal feeling. 

According to all rules she should have felt a love for the unborn infant, considering her infatuation for its father, even though its advent meant increased hardships. But she did not. Rather, her feelings were of complete indifference towards it and a wish to be through with the whole troublesome business and, disposing of it in some way, get herself in shape to once more meet Annie Carter's approval.

By dint of savage economy she had saved nearly thirty-five dollars; with what she had, it might see her through. Nevertheless, she had made up her mind to make one last effort to visit Madam and endeavor to enlist the help she had once promised. She waited as long as she dared, then one night late in February, after a careful though futile effort to render herself less unattractive, she locked Moses in the room and set forth. 

But now Fate seemed destined to turn sympathetic. On the dark deserted stretch of street she saw approaching an elegant lady clothed in furs whose face seemed vaguely familiar. As they met she recognized her and spoke, almost involuntarily: "Irene!"

The lady stared blankly until Cora recalled herself. 

The pity and sympathy in Irene's face unloosed her tongue. She poured out her troubles, begging advice, asking timorously if she would be doing wrong to continue on her visit to Miss Annie. Irene was skeptical but friendly-decidedly friendly. They paced back and forth along the lonely block. "Madam is a little queer in some ways, Kate. She simply can't abide ugliness—and you know you're not at your best. She simply lost her interest when she saw you that last time. You really shouldn't have gone. But

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

if you come through all right and look like you did the night I introduced you, she'll be just the same as she was then." "But where am I to go to have it? She said she would take care of all that. And I don't know a thing about the city. And it's such a short time now-next week maybe. Then there's Moses. What am I to do with him. while I'm sick?”

The tragedy in the hollow eyes touched Irene. This poor great big goop of a woman seemed as helpless as a child. Perhaps some earlier trouble of her own recurred to her. She turned impulsively. "I'll take Moses. I can get away with anything at 

Miss Annie's. And if he is already there, she can't refuse to keep him after you come. And I'm going to talk to her about you. I'll get her in a good mood-buy her a bottle of wine and drink it with her. She's always weepy after she has been drinking fizz water. And I know of a place out towards Independence where they take fallen girls, and then care for the babies until they are adopted, or taken into the orphanage. Of course you'll have to pay in advance for your sickness. How are you fixed for money?" 

Cora told her. Irene pursed her lips, considered. "That's not enough. I'll try to work Madam for a loan; and if I can't, I'll pay it myself and you can give it back when you can." 99 Cora never forgot Irene. Standing beneath the flickering gas lamp, in a strong wind that swirled her voluminous clothes around her misshapen body, she watched the richly clad girl trip on her way, and wondered if anything she might ever do could repay even in part for the burden that was lifted from her mind.

SPORTING LADY 

< 117 » 3 Two days later Irene came for her. Cora was packed and ready; had been since advised by note that Irene had succeeded with Madam. There was a closed carriage waiting at the door of Cora's rooming-house. Carefully 

Irene assisted her to her seat, Moses bounded in, and after giving directions Irene entered and they rolled away. Out past the fringes of the city, through new suburbs far out on Fifteenth Street where Cora had never been, and onto the Independence road with its deep ruts carved by the frosts and snows of winter. A premature wild March wind bent the barren trees in the woods through which they passed, and whistled over the top of the carriage. 

Moses, excited by his first experience in driving, glued his nose to the closed windows and yapped joyously, while Irene coached her protégée in the ways of the new career so miraculously opening for her. Stressed the gaiety and fullness of the life; outlined the pitfalls; painted an informative picture of the days and nights; the rules that must be obeyed, the privileges that might be taken. Irene was herself excited by the breaking of this adventure into the routine of her life. Her own generosity and the curious call to sympathy this big ignorant woman made on her gave Irene a peculiar happiness. She felt responsible for her-wanted to make her over; to create something unusual out of this personality which she felt had hidden possibilities. She wanted to dress her up, like a big elegant doll, and

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

present her to Annie Carter as proof of her own shrewd judgment.

It hadn't been easy to inveigle that fifty dollars out of Madam. It had taken two bottles of wine, and all the ingenuity she possessed to make the new prospect sufficiently attractive to loosen her purse strings. And 

Irene herself had had to guarantee repayment of the loan, in case the girl slipped them.

They laughed together over Irene's account of her morning session with Madam (for it was before breakfast that she had presented herself at Miss Annie's bedside, with her iced cooler and her championship of Cora). Irene outlined Madam's idiosyncrasies and pet aversions, warning as to this and advising as to that set idea of the mistress who held their destinies. Every bit of knowledge that might benefit the new recruit was unfolded during the long ride out through the windblown country to the rambling old house which was to shelter her during the next weeks. Cora, diligently assimilating the information, felt that she could pass an examination upon the duties awaiting her, without benefit of experience.

They were received kindly by the matron. While 

Cora waited with Moses, Irene completed the details necessary for her admittance. She was shown to a large pleasant room and shoved into a big easy chair, while Irene unpacked her meager belongings and moved about placing them, chatting gaily. She opened a bundle she had brought and, delighting in Cora's surprise, displayed the articles, demanding exclamations which Cora readily gave. Two nightgowns; Cashmere Bouquet soap; powder; a small container of carmine; a bottle of Llang- 

Llang; a florid box of chocolates; half a dozen diapers, starch bags, woolen bands, baby dresses. At sight of the

SPORTING LADY 

« 119 » small garments held up by diminutive sleeves, Cora was touched as she had not been during her own nightly sewing. Irene laughed away her sudden tears, grabbed 

Moses and lifted him for a farewell kiss. "Come on now, young man. You are going to prepare the way for her; and you and me are going to have one hell of a row with Miss Annie. But we know who'll win, don't we, Mose, old boy? Now, Kate, do your duty! We've done all we can, and now it's up to you!" 

Three days later, with little effort on Cora's part, Wayne Hendrix's son came yelling into a world that held out scant welcome to him; one more little derelict to kick and fight his way through a life he had not asked for and could not refuse.

« 123 » joking with the girls. Run along now and have a good time. I'm sorry I can't be down tonight, but Irene'll look after you. Anything you want to know, just ask her about. Come over here and I'll give you a kiss for luck."

She heaved her large body into a sitting position. Her eyes were bleary with a fresh cold, and from the red flannel cloth that encircled her neck came an offensive smell of turpentine. Cora submitted her cheek for the caress and escaped with relief. Sickness always irritated her.

As she entered her comfortable room to apply additional paint she heard the roar of the storm without, the lusty gusts of rain that beat against the windows. 

March was certainly going out like a lion. Not likely to be many visitors tonight. She was neither glad nor sorry.

It had to begin some time. Tonight was as good a time as any. The sooner she began, the sooner she would get to making money. Money to pay back Irene, and Annie 

Carter. That was going to take some time unless she was an immediate success. Then, after they were paid, she would go out and buy things; add to her scant wardrobe, consisting now of the "parlour dress" she wore and one street costume. Then she'd fix up her room. 

She looked around it. Heavy walnut bureau with a long glass down the middle, dividing deep drawers. Its brown marble top was devoid of toilet articles, the big drawers empty. She'd line them with sachets of pechula—she loved pechula; and she'd get a fine toilet set and cut glass bottles to hold bay-rum and things; a spangled scarf to throw over the high Gothic top; a huge red earthenware vase for gilded cat-tails on that corner table, and a bright screen to hide the wash-stand and

SPORTING LADY 

its conspicuous flowered bowl, pitcher, and fat-bellied slop jar on the floor beside it; a fancy spread for the wide walnut bed, and ball fringed tidies and puffed cushions, and a plump footstool. It would be elegant. 

Her mind's eye saw it in its transformation. A place where she'd be proud to invite company. Of course, it was grand now-far better than she had ever imagined she would possess. And so warm. The house was heated by those new-fangled furnaces, still on, for the spring was backward. At first the heat had stifled her, but now that she was used to it, it was fine. Moses liked it too. 

He lay now on his cushion before the black grill, relaxed in doggy slumber. She knelt beside him and patted his head. "Does hims like it here? Just you wait, Mose darling -this is only the beginning of our prosperity. My, what a yawn! Well, go on back to sleep. You got to learn to sleep a lot-nights, and days too. But Muvver'll take him down in the back yard before she turns in. Now, be a good doggie and don't bark at any strange mens. 

You got to learn things, too, Moses. You and me both."

Without the ceremony of a knock Irene opened the door. "Kate, for Heaven's sake, what's keeping you? Come on down. It's way late." "Anybody come yet?" "A few. Friends of some of the girls. No strangers.

They're what you are going to have to depend on until you work up your own clientèle. There's certain ethics in this business, and one of them is you can't monkey around the other girls' gentlemen friends. « 125 » ing for partners soon as you get known a little. Why, you're beautiful-well, not that exactly, but you've got something about you. You're damned attractive, that's what you are, Kate. If I didn't like you so much, and feel like I had a part in making you over, I'd be jealous of you. I sure would. And lots of the girls are going to be, too. But don't you let them worry you or get the best of you. You just take your own part, only keep out of trouble all you can. "In the six years I've been here I've seen Miss Annie turn out some good girls," Irene went on, "just because they was trouble makers and fighters. You just be quiet with the girls. Treat 'em nice, but save your arts and graces for the men. Make the girls some little presents sometimes, and praise 'em and admire their gentlemen friends in a disinterested sort of way, and they'll soon leave you alone and accept you. Most of 'em, anyway.

They're a good bunch as a whole; but you'll find some of them different when they're on the floor than what they are in the dining room. 

They were going down the long flight of stairs with its curving walnut balustrade and thick red carpet. 

From behind the closed doors on their left came sounds of a piano being played boisterously. Although she had been in the house a week (days of preparation for her début), Cora had never seen the parlours during receiving hours. Now as Irene opened the folding doors she beheld the rooms in all their glory. The flickering gas flames in the cut glass chandeliers, bringing out bright lights from the gilded be-plushed furniture, dazzled her. Huge plate glass mirrors threw out reflections, made for greater space, gave back the brilliantly clad figures of bare-armed, bare-bosomed girls and women.

standing in groups, sitting alone; two or three triumphantly coquetting with the several men drinking at tables in the middle, the drinking, room-joining their high-pitched laughter to the deeper voices of their companions. It looked so sumptuous, so elegant. And she was now a part of it. She followed Irene across the threshold and stepped eagerly into her new life. 2 The hours dragged slowly by. After the few assorted couples had disappeared, one by one, through the doors to the upper chambers, the remaining girls became more and more bored; sitting listlessly together, arguing and gossiping in desultory fashion, or just sitting, like Cora, listening to the increasing fury of the storm as it came to them from outside the heavily draped windows. 

Juanita, Mexican, and at twenty-one already showing that premature disintegration of her youthful charms peculiar to her race, bent her near-sighted eyes over a centerpiece of intricate drawn work, probably learned during childhood in that far west from which she had rolled by stage coach several years before, in the full bloom of early luscious maturity; with, perhaps, dreams of another sort in her sparkling black eyes. 

Those eyes were now criss-crossed with fine wrinkles and squinting and winking as she held the fine white fabric so close to them; the voluptuous figure, grown unconcealedly fat, was overemphasized by the purple and yellow parlour dress, cut empire, which was the

SPORTING LADY 

< 127 > pride of her heart and the butt of the other girls' jokes. 

Juanita spoke broken English and bathed too seldom and, according to Irene, would soon be on her way out. 

Even her fellow countrymen who, homesick for the sound of their own soft speech, for a time carried her along on a false wave of popularity, had tired and almost ceased their calls. Madam was about to give her the gate, according to Irene, who seemed to share the madam's confidence to a greater extent than any of the others. "Then what will she do?" asked Cora. Not that she cared. But she wished very much to keep a companion beside her on the love seat which she had held down in rather conspicuous isolation for two hours. The others were watching her warily; she had not yet been accepted. "Oh, go to a cheaper and less particular place. Or become a street walker. Or maybe go to Negro Nan's. 

Mexicans don't draw the color line so sharp as us Americans. Lots of mixtures among them."

Irene had told her about most of the girls. She felt she almost knew them, by knowing their stories. When they finally decided to be really friendly to her, the evenings wouldn't be so long. She stifled a yawn. Of course, when there were more customers, when the rooms were well filled with men and the tables with decanters and beer bottles and long glasses, and lively couples danced in the brilliantly lighted, mirror-lined ballroom beyond those tables in the drinking room, it would be different; more like she had imagined. She was thirsty, too. If only someone would come in and pick her out and buy her a long amber glass of cold beer! 

Cora had never craved beer before. But she wouldn't buy it for herself, at a dollar a bottle; and Annie Carter

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

was close with her girls. If she ever had a place of her own she would treat the girls once in a while, especially on a dull rainy night. If she ever had a place of her own-! Suddenly she laughed. "Whoa! Pull up, old girl!" But-was it such a crazy notion after all? Miss 

Annie had said a girl must be ambitious. Well, wasn't that being ambitious? Her hard sense told her it certainly was for one who had sat, isolated and unselected, for more than two hours on a love seat (the irony of that). Even the women were unattracted towards her. 

She was just an awkward country gump; never would be anything else; never would make a success at this business.

Then across these gloomy thoughts flashed the remembrance of Wayne. She had been a success with him! 

He had told her so, more than once. She closed her eyes, shutting out the glare of sparkling cut glass and flickering gas, and was again on the rain-slashed island, snug in the cave; her handsome lover was again stripping her of wet clinging clothes, wrapping her in a rough blanket; lying beside her in the lightning-lighted darkness. Her breath came quickly, color painted her rouge even deeper. So absorbed was she in delightful memories that she did not see the other girls move to attention at the opening of the outer door, at the muffled sound of masculine voices; did not see them rearrange themselves, with glinting eyes and animated smiles. As the doors were thrown apart by the old negro she tumbled back into the present, her eyes feverish and sparkling, her cheeks brightly flushed with her provocative memories. 

Half a dozen men in evening dress-young men, good-looking, personable ("Bon-tons," whispered Irene, brushing swiftly past her), filled the door space of the

SPORTING LADY 

« 129 » room. Gone was the dullness of the past two hours; merry and chattering the girls, like frisky magpies; cooing and laughing, laying white arms on black coats, possessively; smiling suggestively up into male eyes. 

Rumble of deep voices, greetings, calls for the waiters; a shuffling of chairs and re-groupings; the parlours coming to life.

There had been a stag party uptown, and a formal ball on Quality Hill. Those from the latter had escaped for gayer dances, less formal companions. So she gathered from the tossing talk, as she sat alone, self-conscious, shy, wanting desperately to make an invitational move, but not daring nor knowing how. Yet to one slightly inebriated young man her quietness seemed repose, her shyness an indifferent poise. She had seen him immediately; he had seemed to stand out starkly from the crowd. Perhaps because he insolently shook off the detaining hands, frowned at them, detached himself and with folded arms looked through and past them all; past them and into her eyes and then walked, a trifle unsteadily, to where she quietly watched, and seated himself beside her. "You're a new one, aren't you?"

In spite of his somewhat thick tongue she liked his voice. Deep and resonant. Refined. And the strong chin and olive skin, and the way his thick black curls were brushed in small waves from the high square forehead.

It embarrassed her, as though she were looking at Irene naked. There were circles under her slightly reddened eyes, and a few blotches and fine wrinkles around her chin and mouth. Her claw-like hand trembled as she poured the strong black coffee for both of them. "Irene's looking old," thought Cora. "She's been here six years. I wonder if I'll look like that in six years?”

The thought was disturbing, and she frowned it away.

Evidently he was living up to his resolution to be a "good boy"; at least Annie Carter's house saw him no more. However, so far as admirers were concerned, Cora did not miss him. As spring glided into summer and summer into the golden fall days with which Missouri is so bountifully blessed, Cora's popularity grew with each passing month. Before long she was accepted by the girls; at first grudgingly, then as her popularity with men grew and her status became fixed, unresistingly; and then, because of her own determined friendliness and generosity, with affection and even enthusiasm. She acquired an individual parlour manner, learned to talk and dance and beguile; fixed upon a new,

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

blooming makeup and distinctive hairdressing. Her room became luxurious, at least in her eyes; with downy bedcovers and gay curtains, and scarfs and throws and cushions and a chaise longue. On the bureau were silver brushes and mirrors and cut glass bottles of strong perfumes and boxes of scented powders. Moses slept in a satin-lined white wicker basket to which he took as though he had never been a river derelict, but had always scampered through life perfumed and beribboned, and snuggled to sleep in a feathered nest. And her wardrobe grew apace.

Always handy with the needle and possessing a natural flair for fashion, Cora herself had made some of her clothes. Not all-not the finest ones. Bullene's and 

Doggett's head dressmakers fashioned those, from rich and costly materials-more costly still because she was "one of Annie Carter's girls." Overcharged, wherever they went to buy. The girls all knew it, but what could they do?

Her dainty underwear and several of her parlour dresses and all of her bedroom robes, of silk and velvet and maribou and swansdown, she made herself, in her off hours; while the other girls were reading or embroidering, or sketching crude water colors or painting in oils on vases and bottles and tambourines and chopping bowls; or sleeping. Sometimes she sewed for the other girls. That was one of her generosities. She liked to fashion things-express herself so. And she could afford her luxuries. Occasionally she made as high as $200 a week. Of course Annie Carter kept half of that; still, she could afford her extravagances. 

For times were prosperous. After the drouth and the insect scourges of the several preceding years, Kansas

SPORTING LADY 

« 139 » City had, in 1875, entered upon an unprecedented period of prosperity. Kansas had recovered, too, and crops brought producers sometimes four times the value of the land on which they were raised. Kansas City became celebrated over the nation as a central grain market. Buildings were going up everywhere-new, comfortable homes with furnaces and water and barns and inside blinds and sodded yards. New homes going up and prices coming down and speculation ceasing and Kansas City passing from a pioneer town squatting on the plains to a city of national renown, fostered and advertised by its founders and public-spirited men. And its social life, empty during the depressing aftermath of the War, was forming.

Not that the social life affected Cora, or any of Annie Carter's girls. They touched only the ragged hem of it. 

But Cora never failed to scan the society columns for a glimpse of her former surname, and sometimes she saw it. "Miss Flora Simmons" had been to a party or a tea or a musical entertainment. Not often. Flora had not achieved success as quickly as her more florid sister. 

Then Cora would smile sneeringly, perhaps with a little unconscious envy; would talk it over maliciously with 

Moses and bide her time. 

When, in October, she looked back at the novice of

March, the change seemed to have come with startling rapidity. But living through the months, striving painstakingly towards the goal which she had set for herself, the woman she was to become, it had seemed slowand not so easy. There had been many gloomy days and ugly prickings and plain nasty things gone into the crucible. She had toughened, and her soul had grown muscle-bound and unresilient, and ugly things did

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

not matter so much now; never would matter so much again. She was growing into her profession. 

CHAPTER TWO 

1 

´N NOVEMBER she again saw Creighton. 

IN After a good deal of cajolery Cora and Irene had finally won Miss Annie's consent for a visit to the theater. It wasn't often that Miss Annie allowed the girls to absent themselves from the parlours during business hours; but Cora was becoming rather well known about town now, and the two girls persuaded her that to be seen at a public entertainment might attract new customers to the house on Third Street. It might be good advertisement for her wares.

It was Cora's first time at a theater and the excitement of anticipation lent a natural glow under the carefully applied makeup. Their gowns were rich, but quiet; décolleté, but discreetly so. Irene, through the generosity of Charley Frost, flashed diamonds on her fingers; but Cora had not yet arrived at such prosperity and contented herself with several strands of amber beads and a large topaz ring worn on her index finger. The yellow of the ornaments accentuated the lights in her tawny eyes. They had visited Vincent's hairdressing establishment where they were massaged and manicured and becurled; and, after inspection and commendation

SPORTING LADY 

< 141 » by Miss Annie, had entered their hired hack and driven grandly away to the Coates House, blocks to the south. 

They were purposely late. Of what use the experiment if they were not to be seen? argued Miss Annie. The orchestra was playing when they swept down the carpeted aisle behind the usher and were shown into seats well down front. The house was crowded with fashionably gowned women and their escorts. Everyone with escorts. The arrival of two unattended women in itself would have caused a speculative interest, aside from the youth and outstanding good looks and obviously painted cheeks. But it was obvious, also, that the women were recognized by many for what they were. "Fast women" went the word through the waiting audience. Turning of heads, whispers behind hands, averted faces or curious peering ones. Cora and Irene felt the wave that swept about at their entrance. A subtle indication of recognition that flattered and pleased them. Had they been actors they might have bowed their appreciation; as it was they occupied themselves with a rather ostentatious divestment of evening wraps, ruffling of program leaves, discreet smiles and whispers. 

Three rows behind them Mark Creighton, with startled recognition, frowned and concentrated upon his program; beside him, Flora, after the first horrible moment when the blood surged crimson over face and throat, turned her head aside, fumbled nervously in her evening bag, strove to master the heart thumping that seized her. Mumbled words from the two florid men on her left reached her "Sporting women-Annie Carter's girls"-reached also the ears of Mark Creighton whose lowered, sidelong glance caught the furious blush of his companion. "Poor child," he thought, "what an innocent she is.

In the foyer were acquaintances, drummers, strangers in Kansas City, who, unabashed by the amused looks of conservative citizens, flattered them, hung on their sallies and laughter, and escorted them to their aisle at the ringing of the warning bell. But long after Flora had whimpered herself to sleep Ed sat with head bowed on cudgeled fists surveying and rejecting different plans, worrying and even praying. 

Until suddenly the idea came to him. Came after a trail back through memory to the little farm in Clay County. 

It was so simple. Why hadn't he thought of it at first?

Cora had always been fair, and usually responsive to kindness.

He'd go to her, tell her just how things were, and ask her to leave town for the sake of their future. It wasn't as if she couldn't follow her trade in any other city. If necessary he would offer her money. That would be too bad, but he'd make arrangements and be prepared for it. Of course women in that life were mercenary, and Cora had probably grown as grasping and hard as the rest of them; but if there was a spark of the old kind-hearted Cora left, in spite of her debauchery, he

As Ed accepted Cora's gestures to be seated, he forced the surprising and distasteful incident from his mind and struggled mightily to be calm and conciliatoryeven achieving a touch of friendliness that caused Cora to consider him at first amazedly, then suspiciously. Of course there was something up; his visit and his surprising attitude were not the result of any interest in a sporting house (she knew him too well to believe that) or of a desire to renew relations with the sister he had disowned-of that she was sure.

Indeed, Ed had gone against every tradition and instinct in thus boldly entering, for the first time, Annie Carter's or any other sporting house. He had debated ways and means of confronting her; had torn up several letters, deciding with legal caution, that trouble might arise from written pleas or threats; at last had determined, as he put it to himself, to take the bull by the horns and surprise her. That he should have encountered

Creighton, not only in a sporting house but apparently deeply interested in the woman he was himself seeking was a bit of bad luck for them both. Not for a moment did he believe the glib and ready explanations. "I want to talk to you alone, Cora. It's important. Is there any place where we can be private?"

She studied his face deliberately-his white pinched face with its narrow eyes and smug look. "More like a weasel than ever," she thought. Then her own eyes narrowed as she struggled with her insane desire to laugh,

You have improved, too, Cora. No one would know you for the hard-working farm girl of two or three years ago. Other cities will offer you bigger opportunities for making money. Kansas City is only a hick town compared with St Louis or New Orleans. The world is open to you; and it will be only a matter of a few years anyway until you will move on to larger fields. Why not make the move now, before harm comes to us-to Flora and me? We can't go. Our future is here in Kansas City and our opportunities are just coming to us. If a breath of our relationship gets out, Flora and I are utterly ruined. We simply couldn't live it down; while you could make a success wherever you go. you have chosen it, I hope you'll make a big success at it. Maybe in some other town you can get a house of your own and be able to live a life easier than what you must put up with now. Cora, I would get down on my knees to you-I would pray nightly for you-I would forgive everything and let bygones be bygones and remember you only as you were over there across the river when you were taking care of us all. Cora broke it, her voice was hard and there was a metallic glitter in her cat-like eyes. "Well, you've spoken frankly enough and I will do the same. Of course I know all about you and Flora-

« 153 » that as long as you leave her alone she won't raise her head-or her voice. Just that long and no longer.'

As if he had not spoken she continued: "As for Flora, she's a little cat and Creighton is a hundred times too good for her."

Of course, I don't believe that cock and bull story he told. Is there anything between you?" "Oh ho! So I must keep my mouth shut about you and you only, huh? Flora's clutches? However, it isn't true. He is, just as he said, handling some investments for me. Yep, I've got to the place where I have money to invest. Not so bad, huh? CHAPTER THREE CORA 

1 ORA'S anger died out almost as quickly as it had flamed. The week passed by and still there had been no move from Ed. She had been anxious at first, wondering what he was going to do if he really would take action against Annie Carter; and if so, would she be kicked out; or would Miss Annie, confident that her own influence was greater, defy him? She didn't really believe Ed would do anything drastic. He'd be afraid she'd tell—and maybe she would. One word to Mark

Creighton and the marriage would be off. She was certain of that. Yet-maybe Ed did have something up his sleeve. If so, she wished he would come out with it. This suspense was, to say the least, unsettling.

Waking early one morning-hours before her usual time, depressed and uncertain-Cora had closed her window and taken Moses into bed, where he snuggled close against her and returned to slumber. But Cora could not sleep again. Behind closed eyelids her sharpened mind evoked pictures she could not shut out. But, back it went, to her childhood, when her mother and father

« 156» 

SPORTING LADY 

still lived, and she and Ed and Flora were children together. She caught the very feel of their frugal life. 

She had loved her mother, and had been fond of her gentle silent father; but looking back, she realized that she had never had any real affection for Flora and Ed. It was almost as if they had been children of different parents. Ed had spoiled his baby sister. They all had. There were two children in between who had died in infancy, and this last little sister had been delicate, anaemic, taking cold easily, eating not enough to keep a bird alive, Ed always said. A few years later, when the overworked mother had died in giving birth to her sixth baby, stillborn, Cora, though only a child herself, had taken up the drudgery where her mother had laid it down. It had been slipshod management, but she had done the best she could, and had even managed to attend the country school during its brief sessions. But she had always rebelled; had always, she saw now, been jealous and envious of Flora, of whom nothing was required or expected. She was too frail to help with the bigger tasks; had invariably made her weak constitution an excuse to get out of anything distasteful. And Cora was always so big and strong. At fourteen she had already attained her unusual height. She was as strong as a horse, and her family had taken full advantage of her robust health. Yes, Cora had always been jealous of Flora.

Down through the years, as she contemplated them, she now saw it clearly. Little incidents, almost forgotten, arose to affirm it. 

Cora's mind had never been fully developed. Certain parts of it scarcely functioned, seemed numb; but, once started, the part that was thoroughly alive scarcely deviated from its course. And it was an honest mind.

Things either were or were not right, or wrong. It never

SPORTING LADY 

« I57 » analyzed or dissected, but swept on unreasoning to its conclusions, clinched them, and retained them. A narrow mind, but surprisingly just.

That night she had spoken to Ed in the heat of anger. 

She had been inflamed by a sense of injustice, and of envy. She had said that she hated Flora for not coming to her aid. Now, lying here, her mind so sharp and clear, she knew that it was not that alone. She had always disliked Flora. Flora had always rasped her. She had disliked her indolence and primness and little practiced wiles to get her own way with Paw and Ed. Jealousythat was it. As for Ed, they had never had anything in common. Her own honest mind had early seen him for what he was unprincipled, vain, self-satisfied; devoured by ambition; bent on achieving it, no matter what ruthlessness and dishonesty he must practice to do so. A bully. He had always snooped and watched her. A sneak and a coward. He had shot at Hendrix's back. He would do anything behind people's backs. 

Then why, if she so disliked her brother and sister, was she lying here depressed by the scene which had taken place last week? Was it because, after all, she was nearer to Flora and Ed than to anyone else in the world? Did having the same blood bind people together, even though they hated each other? Or was it the close early associations which they had shared? Sleeping under the same roof, eating together the same food, experiencing mutual worries, joys and griefs, year after year; sharing the same parents. That must be it; they were bound too closely together by memories ever to entirely cut the bonds, however hateful they might become. 

Of course she had been wronged. Of course she had got the worst of it. But could that have been helped?

Wouldn't she have acted the same, had conditions been

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

reversed? If Flora had been in her place-if she were the one who was headed for success and social position and was going to marry Mark Creighton, and it was Flora who threatened disaster to her whole future, wouldn't she herself want Flora to get out and leave them in peace? Go away and live the life she had chosen —no, not deliberately chosen, been pitched into by her own actions? She would, most certainly.

After all, Kansas City meant nothing to her. Ed was right. Her stock in trade she could carry in the relatively small bundle of her body anywhere in the world she cared to roam. Cora didn't reason thus in so many words. The ideas flashed in waves through her mind without being formed into sentences; yet that was the gist of them. And Kansas City was a hick town, just emerging from the mud and shanties of pioneer days; filled with overzealous citizens, emotionally mad boosters, who imagined they could rear a metropolis on its ugly clay hills, pull themselves out of the mire by their boot straps. A raw crude town, small enough for everyone to know everyone else's business, small enough to ruin by malicious gossip; so small that men must creep to their pleasures in secrecy, or be scandalized by pointing fingers and venomous tongues. Too small for her business. 

Yes, Ed was right. If she were to make a career, a big career, it would be better to head for a metropolis where her particular field offered greater opportunities. 

It was all right here for the Juanitas and Irenes and 

Annie Carters; but she had bigger fish to fry.

St Louis, now. There was a city; cultured, wise, and big enough to turn around in. She remembered Wayne's description of Kate Clark's house. That would be more like it. And Wayne had been a friend of Kate's. Surely

SPORTING LADY 

« 159 » there wouldn't be any trouble getting in there; not now, when she had developed into a drawing card. 

There was money in St Louis. Rich old families; millionaire brewers and manufacturers and shipping men. And the trip down on a river boat, the trip which was the climax to her dreams that she had never looked beyond; the journey she had been going to take with 

Wayne. Ten days of mingling with crowds, not as a sporting woman, but as their equal; accepted at her face value. It would be nice to experience that, just once. 

Music and dancing and gambling and eating at the same table with people of that other world, the way she had planned it before Wayne got killed. 

Excitement kindled within her. Why shouldn't she go? What was to prevent her? In K. C. she couldn't rise any higher than Annie Carter's. Should she settle down into this narrow sphere just in order to make it uncomfortable for Flora and Ed? Would revenge on them compensate for spending the rest of her life within the limited confines of Annie Carter's house? And if she left she'd have the satisfaction of knowing that Ed and Flora would think she was sacrificing herself for them; when it wouldn't be that at all. It would be because she felt the urge of ambition. And she would never have to see Ed and Flora again. They needn't think they were the only ones who dreaded a meeting. She didn't like to run into them any more than they wanted to see her. 

Maybe Irene would go with her! But no-she didn't think so. Irene was after something else. She was too tangled up with Charley Frost; had some insane idea that she might induce him to marry her. No, she'd go by herself. What was that saying Creighton had quoted one night when he was debating with himself (and her) the idea of getting married to Flora. "He travels fast-

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

est who travels alone." She didn't need a companion now. She had grown; was self-sufficient. Had learned all the points of her profession-at least all she could learn at Annie Carter's. Maybe at Kate Clark's she would progress even further. 2 She sprang out of bed, suddenly determined upon her course. Trailed around the room dressing, making up, chattering to Moses; pleasantly stimulated. It was all so clear now-all so for the best for everyone concerned. She even felt kindlier to Ed and Flora for his having put the idea in her head; even felt reconciled to Flora's marrying 

Mark Creighton. Almost. Of course, he was the very nicest man she had ever met-nicer even than Wayne Hendrix, more refined. And she still believed that, perhaps, she might have cut Flora out; if not before, at least after they were married. Shared his bed if not his hearth. 

Hadn't he been to see her every single night since that evening at the theater, over a week ago? Didn't he love to talk to her, laugh with her, dance with her? Of course he didn't realize his own danger. But she did. She had grown wise these past months; experience had taught her just where Mark Creighton was heading. 

But he didn't know-yet. Wouldn't ever, now. She hoped he'd miss her a little. But as for herself, there were lots of men in the world-lots of them in St Louis. 

Why should she be a fool, like Irene? As if there was only one that really mattered.

< 161 » Suddenly she stopped, frowned, eyed the yawning 

Moses gravely. "What a fool I was about to be, Mose. This sacrifice stuff is all very well. But Ed's got to pay for it. A cool thousand, I should say, shouldn't you? A thousand simoleons. Gosh! And now I'll take you down, and then for an interview with Miss Annie. Come on, babykins, the sooner we get going the quicker we'll arrive." 

Miss Annie glanced up from the letter she had been reading, a black look on her face. Her huge body was propped on pillows, a ravaged breakfast tray rested on her protuberant knees. With her wrinkled, unpainted skin, steel-rimmed spectacles under overhanging frown, frowsy gray hair escaping from the night cap that only partially concealed its disorder, she looked more like some unattractive, grumpy grandmother than the notorious Annie Carter who, stiffly corseted and expensively gowned, reigned as hostess in the most elaborate house of the river town. As the knock was repeated she called out angrily: "Who is it?"

Cora hesitated, inclined to turn and run. She still stood in awe of her protectress, still shrank from her sudden fits of temper or, almost worse, insolent indifference. And Miss Annie's voice proclaimed her temper at this moment was not of the nicest. What if she should refuse to allow her to leave, now that her mind was made up? Then she shook herself. She was no novice-

PART II CHAPTER FOUR 

C% 1 ORA leaned over the deck rail and watched the preparations for departure. The Levee was alive with people, the boat already well filled with passengers. 

The last of the month would see an end to the steamship season, unless this untimely warmth continued. For it was a golden day, an Indian summer day with the blue haze that beautified Missouri's autumns resting lightly upon the high yellow bluffs, and along the wooded banks of Clay County across the river. But weather was treacherous in this part of the middle west. 

In twenty-four hours the thermometer could drop fifty degrees, and a blizzard, sweeping across the plains from the Rockies, could bind a light scum of ice across the river and mantle the countryside in white. The last of 

November was the latest one could rely upon to travel by water. After that the ice would cake and pile around the hulls, the river sag lower in its banks, and yellow gleaming sandbars rise from its bed to baffle pilots and menace passengers.

Household goods were stacked on the cabin deck, and a steady stream of roustabouts, running the gang planks, added sacks of wheat and corn and hemp; hogs- « 165 »

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

heads of tobacco; sides of sheep and steers from Plankington and Armour's packing house over in the West Bottoms.

Color and confusion and babble of voices, and shrilled out last messages as piles of freight on the Levee were transferred to the decks of the gleaming white boat. Ebony faces, sweating with the burden on straining shoulders; negro voices rising in melodious chanteys as bare black feet twinkled up and down the runways. Travellers, like herself, lining the rails. Black suited men in broad brimmed hats; beflounced and bustled and mitted ladies waving white handkerchiefs;

Irene, there, standing alone; even in that throng a woman apart, waving to her and Moses; dabbing her handkerchief to her eyes. Irene, the only one of the girls to whom she had confided her immediate departure; 

Irene, who had been so kind, to whom she was responsible for her life; or at any rate, for her present affluence. She felt slobbery herself. If only Irene had come with her. She had broached the subject last night and 

Irene had not even hesitated. Hectic, gay, taut-dancing with Charley Frost; smiling into his face, ruffling his hair playfully; crimson flags on her haggard cheeks, nervous light in her blue, blue eyes; whispering like an excited schoolgirl-"I can't leave him, Kate, I'm crazy mad about him. And I think he's going to ask me to marry him. Think of it, Kate-a home, a husband, children! Leading a respectable life after all these years. 

And him knowing it and still wanting me. I couldn't go, Kate. A few more months and if luck is with me 

I'll be through with this business forever." Poor Irene, dwelling in a fool's paradise-or was she? But her only friend whom she would never desert, whom she would

SPORTING LADY 

« 167 » pay back if Charley Frost fell down. Irene, pretty and fashionable and standing there apart dabbing the tears from her eyes-tears she was shedding over her, Cora.

Even as she watched, Charley Frost was there beside Irene, lifting his broad brimmed black hat from his yellow hair, talking earnestly for a moment as he pulled at his drooping blonde mustache. Then they turned to her, waved a last adieu, walked away from the Levee onto the board sidewalk, up the steep Main Street hill that led to town.

She stood still looking after them, a handsome figure in a blue, braided dolman holding to modest circumference the voluminous skirts that swept the deck; beside her Moses, white and fluffy, the long hair of his ears braided into blue ribbons, a bright blue bow on his collar; excited by the unusual scene of which he found himself a part but, like the gentleman he was, showing it only in tensed trembling muscles and eager eyes.

Cora watched her friend strolling out of her life, beside the man she was so frantically desirous of annexing, and breathed a prayer for her happiness. Further up the hill her eyes and thoughts projected. Just a short distance, a few blocks, to the office where Ed was even now awaiting her departure, straining his ears for the deep-throated whistle which would announce that the 

Joseph Kinney had shoved off, backed into midstream, and was carrying his menace out of his life forever. A small smile lighted her face, a triumphant and slightly malicious smile, as she touched her breast where, securely fastened under the basque, lay the roll of bills she had demanded, and got.

It had been ridiculously easy. She had gone boldly to

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

his office, had taken advantage of his fear that her tainted presence there should be discovered by his associates; had brazenly demanded the price of her departure, threatening to go at once to Creighton's office and reveal her relationship to Flora. In a panic, ill concealed, compounded of fear at her threats and relief at her promises, he had locked her in, had rushed himself to the bank and had returned with ten $100 bills; had ushered her furtively down the stairs and away forever from his presence.

She had lied glibly to Annie Carter, packed her belongings, all of them, and dispatched them immediately to the wharf. She had taken her place in the parlours and had had a prosperous night. Her dismantled room had stared back at her visitors, as barren and impersonal as the first days of her occupancy. They were old customers, those last visitors; and when they learned she was leaving, had been generous; extra bank notes, and a diamond ring, slightly yellow and flawed, but glittering, and her first. That was from Jo Frank who ran a big gambling house in Lawrence and who had been down regularly each week since their first meeting six months ago. He had bought champagne; so had the others. To drink her health and wish her good luck.

But Cora had not drunk much; she wanted to be at her best when, the next morning, she started her journey.

Mark Creighton had dropped in around twelve and had shown both disappointment and relief when informed of her impending departure. He, too, had ordered wine, and had wished her success, and had drunk much quickly, and grown sentimental and murmured warmly-"If things had been different, Kate—if you weren't what you are-By God, Kate, you are the only

SPORTING LADY 

« 169 » woman I can talk to who understands me. And now you're going. Of course, I know it's best. I might be making a fool of myself if you had stayed. I've had to use all my self control these weeks. But I'm going to miss you, Kate; and if you ever need a friend you can always count on me." 

That's what they all said-"If you ever need a friend-" Well she knew how little they meant it; how quickly they would forget her for others of her kind, once she had vanished.

She had not lingered with Mark for long. Jo Frank had come in, and she had gone off with him with only a casual, "Well, goodbye and good luck, Mark."

Friendly, but indifferent. She had carried it off well. 

The bell tapped, the three-inch hawser was cast off, the Levee fluttered with waving handkerchiefs, raised hats; the Joseph Kinney, voicing deep-throated goodbyes, backed into midstream, turned down the river, riding its breast as if alive, symmetrical and gleaming in its dazzling whiteness. The glass of the pilot house atop the Texas sparkled in the bright sunshine as its tall black smoke-stacks led the way down the turbulent yellow stream. The great side paddles splashed and churned beneath her feet, the gleaming, vigorous town receded further and further until only its high clay bluffs were visible, rising blank and inscrutable above the shambles of shoddy buildings that would one day grow into a great city; a city of wealth and opportunity, carving grandeur and beauty from those very hills that now scarred it. As they rounded a bend and she leaned to catch the last glimpse of familiar landmarks, she muttered: "Goodbye-but not forever. Some day I'm coming back."

SPORTING LADY 

2 

Hours of golden sunshine and spicy warmth of a summer that lingered on, reluctant to depart; lazy hours on deck listening to the brass band playing, watching the Negroes shuffling in shambling dances, snatching at nickels and dimes, heartily guffawing through toothy smiles; passing under overhanging trees that fluttered golden leaves onto the snowy decks-gold and red and brown remnants of their spring bridal attire; stopping briefly for belated crops at Liberty Landing, Missouri City, Napoleon; tying up for cords of cottonwood to feed the fiery furnaces; churning out again, crossing back and forth to follow the tortuous channel of the Big Muddy, now through a deep sluice near the bank, now threading its way carefully between snags and sandbars bared by the low water of autumn. The tapping of the bells, the heaving of the lead-M-a-r-k t-w-a-i-n-q-u-a-r-t-e-r-l-e-s-s t-w-a-i-n. Sometimes skimming a bar, and the sonorous song of the lead heaver-t-h-r-e-e f-e-e-t; and the anxious moment until was heard again—f-i-v-e f-e-e-t—m-a-r-k t-w-a-i-n. 

Ceremonious meals in the long narrow saloon with its glistening white walls, its soft Brussels carpet. Ten to twelve courses-roast venison, squirrel potpie, vegetable and oyster soup, broiled lake trout and baked Buffalo cat; baked beans, snap beans, mashed potatoes, baked sweet potatoes, stewed tomatoes, corn bread, corn pone, hot biscuits; muffins; roast pork, roast beef, veal with dumplings, wild turkey, chicken potpie; apple pie,

SPORTING LADY 

< 171 » mince pie, cocoanut pie, pumpkin pie, custard pudding with chocolate sauce, bread pudding with lemon sauce; fruit cake, plum cake, chocolate cake, cocoanut cake, cookies and doughnuts and ice cream and plum pudding.

Cora rose from the banquets stuffed and surfeited; retired to her virginal white stateroom and dozed; then out again on deck, fearful of missing in slumber too many moments of the journey.

It was nice to pass as one of these passengers; to know that her clear pale face and stately carriage gave no hint of her professional life; to bow and smile and exchange greetings and river gossip with these other well dressed ladies. At first she had felt shy and uncomfortable, but had shown it only in a reserved haughtiness and an unwillingness to make the first move towards acquaintanceship. But after the white-bearded old Colonel Dawley had taken her under his wing, sitting with her by the hour, garrulous with his knowledge of the history of the Missouri River towns to which they glided, tied up, backed off and departed from, the others gathered around until there was quite a circle listening lazily to his chronicles. 

It was in those first hours that the thought came to her. Perhaps it was Irene's reiterated wish which started her mind thus revolving—that, and the golden hours of acceptance by these men and women of another world. 

Whatever it was, sitting there those first days in the reaction from the old life and the relaxation of the new, the thought of turning respectable occurred to her, and she toyed with the idea and the possibilities it presented. 

It was pleasant; and not impossible. She had nearly two thousand dollars. Perhaps she could establish herself as a dressmaker. She liked sewing and designing; had a distinct talent for it. If ever she was going to turn decent,

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

now was the time, when she was cutting all ties, going out among strangers where her past would not intrude. 

Sitting there in that respectable circle, listening to the old man cackling on, accepted without question as one of themselves, the thought was enticing and she projected her mind into the future and planned. 3 At Malta Bend they took on a heavy shipment of wheat. 

Fifty bushels to the acre, Colonel Dawson informed them. Rich hemp land before the War, and before that, when Indians roamed the country, sending up over all these Pteesaw plains wild prairie grass that reached the height of a man on horseback. Old Miami high on its bluffs; home of steamboat captains, shipping point of the richest corn country in the land. Across the stream, following the uneasy devouring channel to its other side where De Witt nestled under the paw-paw and buckeye and hickory growth of Mormon Hill, guardian promontory chosen by the Mormons for their Temple-back in 1832. Had the natives not risen as a man and, reinforced by recruits from Saline, Ray and other counties, five hundred strong, driven them on their way west, the little river town might have become the stronghold of that undefeatable sect, and raised itself to glory through the fame of temples and tabernacles. The old Colonel waxed increasingly eloquent in his historical descriptions. 

And over there, Brunswick, old, staid, German, the tobacco center of the state, nestled trimly under its hills. 

Then came Keytesville Landing, founded by a Meth-

SPORTING LADY 

« 173 » odist minister James Keytes, away back in 1832; from its levee back to the bluffs a score of miles rolled the rich Bowling Green prairie, settled by descendants from the Virginias and Kentucky; the home (eight hundred spreading acres) of the great Sterling Price, raiser of hemp, officer of the Mexican War, noted general of the 

Confederates. And as the white-bearded octogenarian departed from his geography lesson and launched into history as General Price made it, Cora sat dreamily listening, her fingers ruffling Moses' white wool, her eyes fastened steadfastly on the slipping landscape-a lady in a group of ladies and gentlemen, snatching for a short few days the contented atmosphere she had dreamed of when she had planned her honeymoon trip with Wayne.

It was like a dream, she told herself, staring into the mirror above her wash-basin in the white sanctity of her stateroom; staring at the smooth olive skin devoid of make-up, pale like those other ladies; her eyes roving down over the embroidered muslin night-gown concealing her tall strong body, heretofore so sought after, now so placid and undisturbed. Like a dream that flowed smoothly and monotonously, cool and pale, with no warmth to quicken it to life, no sensations to prick it to consciousness. Monotonously, boringly, and, after a few days, drearily.

So this was what it was to be a respectable married lady. This void that caught you up and checked your heart beats and slowed your pulse, and made each hour just a repetition of those hundreds that had preceded it. 

For three days now, three times twenty-four hours, she had not known the ardor of masculine breath whispering in her hair-capped ear; had not felt the first heavy thumping of her pulse, and flashed warm at low-spoken words of praise. For three days she had listened to the

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

droning of a senile old man, whose watery eyes unconsciously sought hers to drive home points in his tedious narratives; she had smirked and smiled and passed banal remarks with phlegmatic, unattractive, prim-mouthed women and their heavily mustached, unresponsive menfolk. For three days she had watched constantly that each laugh should be a gentle ripple, each spoken comment low-voice and quiet; with eyes straight-forward and quickened steps she had passed by the gaming tables at one end of the salon, where men stood tense and unnoticing, fingers clutching colored chips, tossing them on to green baize, rattling dice in leather cups as they called out affectionate admonitions; raking in silently, feverishly, the glittering piles of gold and silver. For three days she had not had a drink. 4 Two and a half miles below Arrow Rock they ran onto a sandbar. All day long the crew and roustabouts worked to set her free. The spar poles were unslung from the bow; the capstans, with great cables wrapped around them, drew taut, slung the big boat around broadside of the stream, but could not budge her from the bar which held her fast. To Cora the day seemed interminable. She had leaned over the rails watching the men at work until her elbows grew numb; had gorged at dinner and slept it off; had shaken the old Colonel and strolled by herself and talked housekeeping and children with Mrs Minters or rather, had listened while Mrs Minters talked. It was dark and cold outside and slate colored

SPORTING LADY 

« 175 » clouds lowered ominously; inside the long salon was crowded and stuffy. At one end of it the gambling went on unceasingly. Men, only men; while she, yawning with the women, yearned to be among them. Where she belonged.

She had not known women could be such bores. Those at Annie Carter's, whatever their other faults, had at least been lively and interesting; quick with slangy repartee; bickering, arguing perhaps, but alive; smutty sometimes, but at least not dull. She went to bed early and, lonely as she had not been for months, took Moses in beside her. But she could not sleep; could only lie remembering the bright lights and mirrors of the house she had left; the laughter and dancing, the champagne bubbling from hollow stemmed glasses, coursing through veins, arousing the blood and pleasantly stimulating the imagination; counted up the dollars that had escaped her. And finally fell asleep after a torrential rain began and lightning flashed into her window and thunder roared across the midnight sky.

All the next day while the rain continued to descend the negroes, soaked to the skin but still good-natured, strove to cast off; and Cora, fractious with inactivity and the stillness of the floor beneath her feet, droned and fretted by turns throughout the hours and tossed in her cabin at night remembering with homesickness the place she had left, cursing under her breath that she had allowed herself to be persuaded. 

But the flooding rain had raised the river and while she was dressing the next morning she felt the boat quiver, heard the turning of the paddles, the tap of the bell, and brightened to realize that they were once more their way. on 

And now, more than ever, was she anxious to reach

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

her destination. As the boat wove its way back and forth with the current she felt that she would scream at the slowness of their progress. It had turned cold and the sky was gray and threatening snow, but she paced the deck through endless hours unable to longer endure the close, forced companionship of the cabin. Then, too, it seemed the only way to elude the unwelcome company of the old Colonel. His rheumatism kept him within, though she often encountered his wistful eyes behind a window. He came aboard at Glasgow. As he walked up the gangplank, a slender, dapper figure with a flashing diamond in his red puffed cravat, a soft wide-brimmed black hat set at a rakish angle on his coal black hair, his eyes looked straight into hers as she leaned listlessly watching the loading of barrels and bales which made up the large cargo they were taking aboard. Eyes, gray like the sea in a storm, inscrutable, but wise. Oh, very wise. It did not need the flicker of a lid to tell her that he recognized her kind. Pleasantly her pulse responded. It had not failed her.

He sat far down the table at supper, but opposite. 

Several times she felt his gaze on her, like a crackling current, but she returned it only once. He did not smile; his face was a mask, but his eyes were contemplative.

She went to her stateroom, rouged her cheeks dis-

SPORTING LADY 

< 177 » creetly but noticeably, gazed at her feverish eyes and impulsively kissed the blooming image. "To Hell with them all!" she quietly informed the dog Moses. "Come and get brushed. You are going to make a new friend. And you must be nice to him, Moses.” 

With the white dog tucked in the crook of her arm she stepped into the passage, swayed, insolent and unseeing, through that end of the saloon where tables and chairs had been removed for dancing. Her erstwhile friends were sitting stodgily about listening to the tuning up of the orchestra, drowsily digesting their suppers. The 

Colonel made a gallant attempt to rise, twinged and sank back, only his plaintive eyes following her as she swung gracefully down the long room and past the gaming table at the other end.

It was as she had surmised. He was standing there at the top of the green covered dice table, inscrutable eyes narrowed, following the rolling bones, reaching forward and placing small stacks expertly on lined squares with long slender nervous fingers. She slackened her walk, paused for a moment behind unconscious masculine backs lining the boards, compelled his gaze, and with a sudden dropping of her lids, swung languidly out the doorway and made her way leisurely to the stern. Depositing Moses upon the bench which circled it, she knelt and leaned her warm cheek against an iron

She heard his soft foot-steps behind her, smelled the aroma of his freshly lighted cigar, and patted Moses' head in admonishment as he uttered a gentle growl of warning. "Lonesome, honey?" "Damnably." She turned her sombre amber eyes into his, level with them; smiled provocatively—"But not now." "That's nice. Say, 'Never no more.' You ought to do well.” "Do you know her?" "Casually. Know her girls better. Grand bunch, finest in the west. Well, what do we do now? It's too cold to stand around out here." "It sure is. Say, can a lady get a drink? I've been thirsty for five days now." "How's that?" "Oh, I've been playing at being respectable and it has nearly killed me. Another day and I would have been in the batty house."

Imagine, I was even thinking of going into business in St Louis; opening a dressmaking shop. But it didn't take long to cure me of those ideas. Well, what about that drink?”

It was a grand evening. Wardell did not return to his interrupted game. The present one promised greater rewards. The little room, cozy with cushioned chairs and card tables, was unoccupied. All the gamblers aboard were engrossed with dice. Several did appear at the open doorway, to eye the occupants and arch brows in surprise and amusement. That dignified Mrs Black, painted and hilarious, drinking wine with a gambler-who would have thought she would turn out that kind? It

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

was something to tell the wife-and old Colonel Dawson. When he ordered the second bottle Wardell closed the door and after that they had privacy. Moses, somnolent on the tufted velvet bench that bordered the room, more than once cocked an inquisitive eye as Cora's laughter rang out. He had not been allowed in the parlours at Annie Carter's; he had never seen his adored mistress in her gayer moments. It disturbed his sense of her. Ho-hum! well, it was long past his bedtime, and the cushion was soft.

Cora could not remember when she had been so happy. Reaction, of course, from the dull, dull days; absence of an admiring male. She was giddy, exhilarated.

Her tongue loosened and she talked. Talked to his steadfast sea-colored eyes with their attractive black circles and wise look; she who so seldom talked, leaving that to the men as she had learned to do. She heard her nimble tongue rambling on and wondered at herself but did not try to restrain it. It was so good to let out all the pent up energy of her five honest days; and he was so understanding, the firm feel of his hands so magnetic. 

Gosh, he was nice. She hadn't thrilled like this since the first days with Wayne. Wayne! Poor Wayne, he was dead and gone.

She told him about Wayne. Not exactly the truth; her mind was keen-not a bit befuddled. She concealed some details, embroidered others. She was kind to herself in the recital. He held her hand and kissed her fingersturned the palms and kissed them, while she was talking about Wayne. That was after she had told him about the relative who had wanted her to leave Kansas City; about the money she had got from him; about the two thousand dollars she had gathered and had for a few

SPORTING LADY 

« 181 » hours thought to invest in a dressmaking parlour in St Louis. How they both laughed about that. And it was then that he had pulled her over into his arms and bent hungrily over her, his eyes devouring, and slowly, expertly, had closed his mouth down over her eager one, in a long, thrilling, burning kiss. She had been shaken as she had never been, not even by Wayne. After that he ordered another bottle. "We haven't christened you yet, beautiful.”

They made up names and laughed at them, and debated and discarded until they finally hit upon one which suited them both. "Julia,” he said, and she quickly supplemented it with: "Wayne." 

He had sprinkled her head and intoned: "I christen you Julia Wayne," and they had drunk solemnly, and he had kissed her again. His kiss had not lost its potency. And then they were both ready to go.

They found the saloon deserted, the tables and chairs in place for breakfast. Two o'clock. Where had the golden hours flown? Quietly, stealthily, with Cora carrying the inert form of Moses and walking a trifle unsteadily, they passed his cabin and reached her own. "Knock twice," she whispered as the door closed on him. 

What a headache! Cora stared with dull eyes fixed on the ceiling of her stateroom, regretting. But only the

overindulgence in wine. "I've only to shake my head and get another jag," she informed Moses. "Good Lord, it's ten o'clock. Poor doggie. Has him's mumsie neglected hims?"

Hearing the mop of the stewardess in the corridor she sat up heavily, searched in her purse, opened the door. "Dorcas-will you please take my dog out? And bring me a cup of black coffee when he's through." 

The maid's eyes glittered at the silver dollar Cora held out to her. "I sho' will, Miss. Ain't yo' well? Now that's too bad. 

Anything else I can get you? Some oatmeal? Ham and eggs?" 

Cora made a wry face. "No. That's all."

The coffee, two headache powders and a sleep enabled her to dress for dinner. But she was down in spirits and rather dreaded the reception she felt would be forthcoming from her former associates. "Gosh, I sure kicked over the traces last night," she murmured to the white face and heavy eyes that greeted her from her mirror. "What a damn fool thing to doget boozed like that. I wonder if I made myself conspicuous? I'll bet I get the cold shoulder now, all right.”

Her premonition was correct. The buzz of conversation ceased as she took her place, eyes stared at her coldly or turned studiously away. She murmured good morning to her near neighbors but received only averted glances and, after a slight nod down the table to Wardell, she assumed an indifference she did not entirely feel, picked at the interminable courses of food, and managed to remain through dessert. Then, wrapping up warmly, for it was cold and cloudy, she escaped with Moses to

SPORTING LADY 

« 183 » the deck and walked briskly for half an hour. She had the outdoors to herself, for a stiff wind was blowing. 

She watched the turgid swirling current through which the great white boat turned and twisted.

The water was very low. Snags, with long tenuous fingers, reached out to clutch at them; treacherous halfsubmerged sandbars sought to deceive and impede them. 

They were riding midway of the stream and far on either side she saw the overhanging bluffs that had been eaten away gradually through the years, feeding the insatiable maw of the dirty river. Stripped willows lined the banks, an occasional farmhouse stared out across the water. It was a desolate scene-lonely, colorless under the gray sky, ugly. But the wind whipped about her, stinging natural color into her face, smarting her eyes, reviving her spirits. "Feeling better?" "A little. Did you have a headache?" "No. Too used to it, I guess. But it certainly was a night, Julia." "Wasn't it?" She laughed and tucked her arm in his, sniffed appreciatively his cigar's aroma. "But I'm paying for it. Absolutely and completely snubbed by my friends." 

He laughed shortly, squeezed her hand. "Why worry about them? It was worth it, wasn't it?" "More than. Only-I don't know what to do with myself. I can't stay out here all day. I'm half frozen now." "Why don't you get in the game? Might as well give them the whole works." "Would they let me? Do they allow women?" "My darling, they allow anyone who has the cash.”

SPORTING LADY CHAPTER FIVE 

1 ORA had played the crap games a few times, both COR in Kansas City and at Jo Frank's ornate gambling house in Lawrence. But never such a game as these river gamblers played. Twenty-five, fifty, a hundred dollars on the throw of the dice.

Wardell had edged her into the packed group around the table, found himself a place at the far end. "You're too disturbing; must keep my mind on the play," he had whispered. "Good luck."

The men had automatically made room for her, seemingly oblivious to her sex, interested only in the rolling white cubes. No words were spoken save the monotonous chant of the "stick man," the plunky rattle of dice against leather, the terse excited calls of players: "Come on, Little Joe!" "Got to buy my baby shoes!" "Ain't no hound dogs left in old Kaintuck!" "Sweet bones, roll 'em for your pappy!" All the picturesque jargon of the dice tables.

She watched for a few turns, recalling the points of the game as taught her by Jo, feeling gradually the rising of blood pressure, the thrill of suspense, the sweet sharp excitement that warms the gambler's heart. Then she bought and edged her chips forward cautiously, trying out her luck. It was with her. She raked in her gains, placed again, gradually raised her stakes. The

SPORTING LADY 

« 185 » even orderly piles before her increased, diminished, grew bigger and bigger as she formulated her routine. Time ceased to register; her cheeks grew flushed, her eyes feverish and crafty, narrowing to shut out all view save the green spread before her. Only a few times did she allow them to wander to where Wardell, wooden faced, slit-eyed, stood tireless as herself, down the length of the table-to notice his meagre hoard of chips, to hear him buying again. Evidently he was losing.

She did not stop for supper. With a dozen others equally obsessed, she scarcely heard the call. She did notice that Wardell's place was vacant. An hour or so later he stood behind her. "Good girl. Keep it up. I've tended to Moses. He's in your room asleep." She had forgotten Moses! For the first time, as she had forgotten everything else. She murmured a grateful "Thanks," abstracted, eyes not turning; slipped him a stack of blue chips-"Here-change your luck." Promptly erased him from her consciousness. 2 Eleven-twelve-one. The game was breaking up, winners cashing in. Cora slipped into her purse a watch, a seal ring, a long gold chain; piled her chips in stacks of fifty, pushed them forward, carefully counted bills and gold and silver. Suddenly she was very, very tired. And hungry. Gosh, how hungry she was. She pushed open her door and saw him sitting there on the wall sofa, before a small table on which rested a plate of sandwiches,

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

glasses, a wine cooler; a partly consumed bottle of whiskey. "Well?" his look enquired. "Gosh, that looks good. You are thoughtful. I'm starved." "You look tuckered out. Here, drink this. Well, how'd you come out?" "Thirty-five hundred dollars!" 

A smile spread over his face- "Good work, honey. I can see where we've got to get together!" 

But after he had taken his stealthy departure she could not sleep, tired as were her frayed nerves and bloodshot eyes. Thirty-five hundred dollars! fifty-five hundred altogether. Gosh, she was rich. A fortune right there in her bag, under her head. Why, she didn't have to go to Kate Clark's. She could travel; see the country; do anything she wanted to do. Excitement like this would be enough to keep her from stagnating; and if 

Worthy were with her, she needn't look for anything, or anyone, else. со She was tired next day; slept through the morning and had her dinner sent in. At two o'clock Wardell knocked at her door. Everyone on the boat is talking about you. You

SPORTING LADY 

< 187 » made a sensation, all right. It might not be so pleasant for you, and I'm in a big poker game. We tie up at St

She followed his advice, drowsily and contentedly lying there, dozing and waking, with the comfortable feel of the money under her pillow and the assurance that the long journey down the river was near an end. 

The poker players had sandwiches sent in to them and did not appear at supper. Around nine o'clock she glanced in at the open door, saw the little room blue with tobacco smoke; whiskey glasses tall and small, dark bottles. She noticed that there was no glass beside 

Wardell. She was glad. He was keeping his head. Most of the chips were before him-chips and some gold jewelry, and a diamond ring. She eyed it covetously. 

Seven men, strained, silent, speaking only in monosyllabic curtness as they announced and raised their bids. 

Wardell lifted his eyes briefly, blankly, to hers, lowered them.

She returned to the saloon and sat down near Colonel Dawson, who awkwardly moved to another chair. 

Women glanced at her coldly, men slyly. Mrs Minters flounced out of the room. Cora amused, unruffled, now that it was so soon over, smiled to herself. And she had seriously contemplated becoming one of these-hens. 

She walked Moses on the deck, looked in again at the players and received a slight frown from Wardell's recognizing eyes. There remained nothing to do but go to bed-take a sleeping powder, be fresh for him when he came to her room. If he did. But it looked like an all-night session.

She roused once at the sound of voices reaching her

"Don't believe all you hear. Am taking a train and will meet you in St Louis, Linden House. Make no connection until you see me. "W" Somehow she got through the interminable supper. Accustomed as she was to the isolation imposed by her profession and hardened as she had become to it, this was somehow different.
