"He'd 'a' had a worse heart attack if the mate hadn't got wise."

These, and other unenlightening scraps only served to augment the worry that had seized her since reading Wardell's hastily scribbled message. She escaped from the hostile company as soon as possible and taking Moses strolled to the stern where she had first spoken to Wardell. It was a clear night, and warmer. Brilliant stars spangled the cup of the sky and were reflected in the turgid current swirling in the wake of the boat. She stood looking morosely down at the churning water, shivering slightly in her warm wraps. Brooding; anxious. 

Just what had occurred? What ill fate was shaking the tower of dreams she had reared in these last days? Well, she would soon know. Tomorrow morning they tied up at St Louis, and she would follow his instructions and go at once to the Linden House. After that—perhaps to 

Kate Clarke's; or, perhaps, adventuring alone. She pressed closer the fat reticule she carried under her arm. 

If he had got into some bad scrape and expected her to help him out-well, she had her own self to look out for; no one else was going to do it for her. She liked him-liked him tremendously. But she wasn't Irene's type. No man was going to hog tie her now that she was financially independent. She shivered again. "Nerves," she muttered. Well, there was no use in worrying; in a few hours she'd see him-perhaps. And then she'd decide on her next move. Of course he would have some glib explanation. She'd like to know the real facts before she saw him. "Don't believe all you hear!" 

Humph! Sounded bad. Put it out of your mind. Pack and go to bed. Tomorrow-well at any rate tomorrow this damned journey would be at an end and she'd be rid of present company.

SPORTING LADY 

« 191 » 

A touch on her arm broke her reverie. Scowling she turned to face the old Colonel. He was muffled in a great coat and puffing at a corn-cob pipe. His faded blue eyes were winking nervously as she stared down coldly into them; his voice was more tremulous than usual. "Well, what do you want?" "I saw you come out, Mrs Black, and I thought maybe you were lonesome. Those people there," with a backward jerk of his head, "haven't been very nice to you since you took up with that stranger. I-I kinda felt sorry for you." "Thanks. You can keep your sympathy to yourself.

I noticed you were not so friendly either when their eyes were on you. Goodnight." She turned her back and stared down into the water. But he was not to be rebuffed, now that he had taken his courage in hand and approached her. His eyes ran over her taut figure appreciatively as he again ventured: "Too bad about your friend. I think myself the Captain was a little hard, but then these fellows must be taught a lesson. Of course, you didn't know what he was. Such a fine upstanding woman as you are must learn to be careful about taking up with strangers." 

Suddenly she faced him, her eyes curious, friendlier. "Just what happened? I had a sick headache last night so didn't see the trouble. Just what did Mr Wardell do that was so terrible?" 

Colonel Dawson refilled his pipe and seated himself beside Moses. "Well, it seems that that old countryman who got on yesterday morning had just sold his farm and was on his way to his son's in St Louis. Had seven thousand in cash on him and went at once and handed it to the

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

Mate to keep for him, all but a few bills. This Wardell was standing near and seen it, and afterwards got friendly with him. The Mate seen that, too, and he knew this Wardell-seems he's had trouble before on the river boats. Professional card shark. But the Mate, being busy, didn't think much about it. Well, it seems Wardell got the old fellow into a game and he won quite a lot at first and then began losing. Guess he must have real gambling blood, even if he was from the country, because he didn't know enough to quit when he'd lost his small bills; and Wardell was very friendly and staked him, and finally the old codger went to the Mate and asked for his roll. "The Mate kind of argued with him but, as he said, he wasn't his brother's keeper and the money was his, so he gave it to him. He was right busy at the time and it was a couple of hours afterwards before he thought of the old man again, and then he got a little troubled and thought he'd better look him up and see what was going on. Well, there he was in the poker game all right and just about distracted, because Wardell had nearly every cent of that seven thousand dollars. The Mate edged around until he stood behind Wardell who didn't seem to notice, for there was a thousand dollar pot on the table and he was playing for blood. He won the pot, and the old man screamed and fell over on the table, and the Mate grabbed the cards. "It turned out those cards were marked and Wardell had substituted them for the ones they were supposed to use-it's an old sleight-of-hand trick that crooked gamblers often employ. They searched Wardell right there and found the other pack on him. Well, he was caught red handed all right. They took him to the Captain and he was furious. Said he'd teach him a lesson,

SPORTING LADY 

« 193 » and ordered the Pilot to stop the boat. It was pouring rain—awful storm. They did allow him to go to his cabin and get his things and then they threw him off miles from nowhere. Didn't even put out the landing plank, for they had run close in to shore. They made him jump for it and he landed in about three feet of water, but I guess he wasn't hurt for I saw him get up and start up the bank. In a woods it was and about five miles from the railroad, so the Mate said. It sure was some excitement for a time." "It must have been. Well, I reckon I'll go in and pack."

The Colonel leered up at her, glanced furtively around, whispered: "Say, Mrs Black-how about a bottle of wine—just you and me—after the boat has grown quiet?-Eh?— 

Shall I bring it?"

Cora gazed steadily at him for a moment, then laughed insolently. "I beg your pardon, Colonel Dawson. You are quite mistaken. Goodnight-and goodbye!"

number 219. I'm sorry, Madam, but we don't allow dogs. The porter will take care of it for you." The dapper clerk bent a conciliatory smile upon her as he motioned to Moses who was eyeing him suspiciously from where he squatted beside Cora's hand-baggage. "Is that so?" Cora rasped. "Then we'll not be staying here. You needn't bother about bringing up my grips," to the hovering bellboy, "and see that the dog remains here until I come down."

The clerk shrugged his shoulders, fingered the carnation in his buttonhole. "I'm sorry, Mrs Wardell. Rules of the house, you know."

She flashed him a withering glance and haughtily followed the bellboy up the broad green carpeted stairs, down a long white corridor, round a turn, and came to a halt before one of many identical doors. "Come in."

He had risen from a deep chair beside the window. 

Natty and trim as when she last saw him, red cravat still sporting its stone. But his face seemed thinner and even more colorless, the lips grimmer, the circles around his eyes blacker. She had entered calm and self-possessed and hard. He should not make a fool of her again. She would listen to what he had to say, unswayed by the emotions with which he had somehow managed to entangle her; she would judge sanely and decide deliberately.

But even on the instant she knew that was impossible. The current that had crackled between them from the first again touched her pulses; her heart missed its beat, then flooded into her throat. In a moment she was in his arms, his kisses on her lips, the curious passion he

SPORTING LADY 

« 195 » seemed able to evoke at will weakening and overpowering her. "Sweetheart! Julia! God, it has been so long. We are never going to be parted again. I'm going to hold you fast, like this—until you are a part of me and I'm a part of you. God, Julia, how I love you!"

There was nothing more to do. She was like wax in his hands. Pliant, acquiescing, her resolutions running from her mind like water in a swift stream, And glad of it deliriously, ecstatically glad. Wherever he went, whatever he did, was all right, so long as he could subdue her like this; cause her heart to palpitate so almost sickeningly, stir her blood so that it pounded its rich red stream against her pulses where, in the white wrist he grasped so masterfully, she could see it fluttering like the heart of a bird held captive. "Oh, my darling, my dear, what did they do to you?”

His face darkened, black brows frowned above the sea-gray, circled eyes. He released her, took up the cigar he had laid aside at her entrance, shrugged his shoulders indifferently, "Well, it's all in the game. Just a bit of bad luck that's past. Let's forget it and talk of the future. Come here, where you belong."

He drew her down upon his lap, untied her bonnet and tossed it to the bed, lifted the little curls at the nape of her neck and pressed his lips against it. "You're going with me, darling. Whether you want to or not. You've met your master and you know it. You're mine, and I'll kill you before I let you go. But you don't want to. I couldn't drive you off, could I? Could 

I, Julia?" 

She kissed him.

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

2 They did not remain in St Louis. Cora supplied the funds that enabled them to join the race-track meet in the south. January found them in New Orleans where they finished out the winter, moving up with the ponies to Memphis, to Louisville.

Luck was with Wardell, and when luck was with him he was very generous with her. They lived grandly at the best hotels, where the fine suite they occupied and their generous tips enabled her to keep Moses with her. 

Wardell knew how to manage those things; Wardell knew everything about fancy living. How to obtain service; how to order an epicurean feast; the vintage of wines; even the colors and styles that most became her. 

Each killing at the track meant a new ornament for her. A diamond sunburst, rings, earrings. He was mad about stones-big flashy ones. Made her wear them all the time. It pleased his vanity to bedeck his big stunning mistress with the gems on which his eyes loved to linger. 

To him they were an emblem of his prosperity, a tangible witness to his luck. They gave him confidence. 

Cora was perfectly happy that first year. And her exuberant happiness improved her. She developed, gained in poise, acquired an imposing manner, albeit seasoned with the careless gaiety and boldness consciously or unconsciously acquired from the sportswomen who were her companions. Wives and sweethearts of racetrack followers, frequenters of gambling houses, confidence men; the upper crust of the underworld. Women

SPORTING LADY 

< 197 » for the most part cut from the same pattern. Sumptuous, feverish for excitement; their laughter a little too loud, their voices a little too shrill, their raiment a little too exaggerated, their conversation interpersed with the argot of the track. No one could mistake them. They travelled around together, making no other acquaintances, clustering close for companionship, averse to even one lonely hour while their men folks were absorbed in play at the stag gambling houses.

This was life as Cora had imagined it, even so far back as the little farm in Clay County; when she had said to Flora, "I'm going to travel and wear beautiful clothes and live among crowds and music."

Wardell had given her all this, but she had no illusions about him. Some that she might once have held had been stripped away by the old Colonel's bland information that last night on the Joseph Kinney; the rest, by their daily association through the subsequent months. She knew he was crooked at cards, at all gambling when opportunity offered; that he had pulled off more than one confidence game; that he was looked upon with suspicion and distrust by his erstwhile companions-friends he had none. But after the first shock, this had ceased to matter. So long as he remained her ardent and generous lover, so long as he crowded from her mind all thoughts of other men and of that other life she had forfeited for him, so long as he played fair with her, nothing else counted. And he had played fair.

From the first he had given her to understand that she could hope for nothing more secure than this relationship into which they had entered so spontaneously. That he was married and had children, in Cincinnati; that he had no intention of freeing himself from the wife he had left there with them-of this she was fully aware.

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

Sometimes he received letters from her, long closely written letters which Cora, miserably jealous at first, had pilfered and read. She found nothing in them to augment her jealousy and she finally came to accept the situation without bitterness; to take what he had to give gratefully, living in the richness of the day at hand, securely confident of the love, or the passion, or whatever it was that motivated his ardor and thoughtfulness and consideration and generosity. For his steady gray eyes never flickered towards another woman; that she knew.

Of course there were some lonely nights despite the women upon whom she could always depend for an evening at the theater, a drive, a card game or drinking party of their own. Sometimes she became utterly bored with them; and Worthy's profession took him from her almost every night. But she had Moses, still adoring, still constant, still enshrining her in his little dog heart, there to remain as long as a single loyal beat was left to it. Moses, who for some strange reason had never liked the new master; who, even now, would sometimes utter a low growl when he heard his returning footsteps in the early morning hours and would droop his tail and slink away and gaze with suspicious eyes at the man who laughed back at him, asserting, "He's jealous of me." SPORTING LADY 

« 203 » It was nice at the tracks. Exhilarating. Sometimes Cora played the ponies herself, but she had little luck with them. And she didn't like dipping into her savings. That was a queer thing about Worthy. He dressed her and ornamented her, but he never gave her any money of her own to spend. Even when she went to the theater with "the girls" she had to pay for her ticket out of her own boodle bag.

Sometimes a crowd of them went out to the tracks in a big tallyho drawn by four horses, and all decked out in their best finery; sometimes she and Worthy took a hack out. She learned about the different stables, their owners, and jockeys; learned to know her way about the paddock and became acquainted with some of the trainers. Occasionally she gave Worthy quite good tips on the different horses; and she kept a little note book in which she entered all their wins and placings. She grew as familiar with other cities as she had been with Kansas City. It was nice to be greeted in such friendly fashion by the hotel clerks and head waiters when the circle of the meets brought them around again. After two years of the life she felt that she belonged-that she was really one of that close fraternity whose existences revolved around the ponies and the tracks.

If Worthy, spoiled by his continued good luck, had not grown ambitious for new worlds to conquer, her whole after-life might have been different. But he took it in his head to go east-to Saratoga and the big eastern

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

meets; this southern route was too small for one of 

Worthy's calibre. Cora blamed herself for not discouraging him. But she was as eager as he for fresh pastures. 

They didn't get beyond Saratoga. With their removal from their accustomed haunts his luck changed. Perhaps it was because he was unfamiliar with eastern ways and horses; perhaps he was only small fry after all. However it was, those eastern sports mulcted him well. Overconfident, he had gone into the game plunging. In a week all he had won in the southern meets (she never knew how much it was) had disappeared. They moved to a smaller hotel, then to a rooming house. He became gloomy and silent and as his supreme confidence waned, morose and inattentive. Night after night she sat alone, save for Moses; day after day watched him leaving her for the track. He no longer wanted her to accompany him.

They took noontime breakfast together in their shabby room, brightened only by the gay negligees she trailed from bed to spread table. A silent breakfast which he gulped untasting, as he pored over and marked the day's racing sheet. A hurried kiss, the slam of the door, and she was left to look longingly after him, to dawdle through the endless hours until he returned, usually as gloomy as when he left. A flavorless meal in one of the cheaper restaurants and he was off again to spend the better part of the night at roulette or cards; and she to walk back to the hotel, get Moses, stroll alone past casinos and cheery hotels, watching enviously as expensive equipages with prancing horses deposited fashionably attired women and men for a night of gaiety; out past great mansions set in rolling lawns, and back through the busy streets to her gloomy rooming house, there to wait and sleep and wake and sleep again. Until

SPORTING LADY 

< 205 » noon. Cora grew loggy from much sleep; she more than caught up with all she had ever lost in those past years. 

How she hated the east that refused to accept them; how she hated these unfriendly easterners who snubbed them.

He was playing on her capital now. Of necessity she had doled it out to him, apparently amiable but inwardly reluctant, grudging. Playing and losing, playing and losing. A hundred dollars a day it went, that money so hard come by, so watched and treasured. Several times he asked for more, when he had a hunch and was sure of a killing; when eagerness shone again in his eyes as of old, and confidence in his luck seemed to return. 

It was so pleasant to have him a little as he was in the old southern days that she acquiesced those few times. 

He always lost it; at least so he reported to her, with excuses and alibis which invariably ended in maledictions and curses at his luck. After those several times she argued with him against increasing his daily stake and for the first time she saw his ugly temper. However, he did not insist and she did not withhold the daily hundred. But it was then that she began to turn and the glamour of her romance dulled. Repressed, tight lipped, she gave but he was striking in her most vulnerable spot. Her money. Her carefully hoarded and gloated over savings. 

And Moses was getting on his rasped nerves. He quarreled about him, swore at him; and Moses, upon hearing his step, growled low in his throat and learned to scramble under the bed, there to lie in the furthermost corner, tail curled under, beady eyes staring, watchful.

She hid one thousand dollars in case, during her sleep, he should become curious and pry. Hid it in a little slit she made in the bottom of her Gladstone. This much, she was resolved, he should never get.

5 He came home that night white and excited, and with bloodshot eyes that were already beginning to show discoloration. He roused her from her first heavy sleep (it was not yet midnight) and told her they were leaving immediately. Silently she went about the business of packing, watching him furtively from the corner of her eye; noting his desperate haste, the nervousness with which he listened at each approaching footstep. She did not question him and she never learned just what had happened, but surmised the occurrence of another such scene as that on the Joseph Kinney.

The house was sleeping as they stole through it with their handbags, the only luggage they had brought east with them, walked to the corner and picked up a hack whose sleepy driver awaited revellers from the casino, and drove to the depot. At his direction she bought tickets for Louisville. There was a train leaving for New

York shortly after midnight, and not until they were safely aboard and he had taken stock of the occupants of the chair car did he relax from his nervous tension. 

There was no meet on at Louisville; only the gambling halls to occupy him. Nor did they return to the Galt

SPORTING LADY 

< 207 » House, but took instead a couple of rooms on the outskirts of town. Two adjoining bedrooms. He was too nervous, he explained, to room with anyone; also, there was no need for her to be disturbed by his comings and goings. She set her lips and restrained the impulse to dissent. Sometimes he did not return during the day or the succeeding night; and when he did arrive, utterly weary and with bloodshot eyes, he slept the clock around. 

The only information he gave was that luck was still rotten; and only that when he asked her for money.

One day, after he had been gone for a week and she was distracted with worry and fear, he reappeared in the middle of the afternoon, just as she was trying to rouse herself enough to take Moses for his walk. He looked slick and neat and, for once, ready to talk. But she did not force him; she waited for him to begin of his own accord, resuming her reading as though unexpectant. He lit a cigar, paced restlessly up and down glancing every now and then to where she sat apparently absorbed in her novel; strolled to the window and drummed on the pane. Then wheeling suddenly, spoke: "Put that damned book down and listen, Jule. We've got to talk things over. I'm at the end of my rope. Oh, of course, it won't last, this hellish luck I've run into. 

But I've got to admit it has me licked right now. I'm sick of losing your money; I guess you haven't much left. And you needn't think I don't appreciate what a good sport you've been all this time. But I've reached the point where I won't take any more from you. You're entitled to what you have left and I'm not going to be a drag on you any longer. We'll have to separate while you still have some, or else—” 

Her heart stopped for a moment, then resumed its

beat; her hand trembled but her voice was quiet and her eyes steady as she asked calmly: "Or else what?"

He came over and sat on the arm of her chair, kissed the back of her neck where the little curls grew, held her close: "Jule, I'm going to quit for awhile. Keep away from the cards, rest up, and give my luck a chance to change. 

But we've got to live, and there's only one way I see to do it." "How's that?" "You let me hock your stones. We ought to be able to get a couple of thousand on them. With it we'll fit up a house for you to run-a small house, only about four girls at first. It'll tide us over until the races begin and maybe I can make a killing. I've always had trouble with cards-they're unlucky for me. It's only at the track that I'll ever make big money. And when I do, girlie, you'll get the stones back again and more with them."

Her voice was hard as she finally broke the pause: "I thought I was through with all that for good. I thought you cared for me too much to want to share me. 

"For Christ's sake, who's talking about 'sharing' you? 

You're out of the business except to manage it, see? Just let me catch you at any of your old tricks and I'll beat you up. You're mine-for all time-if we can swing this. And when I get to winning again we'll sell out or close up. It's only a temporary arrangement. Until I get on my feet again. Well, what say, snooks?" "Where will we get the girls?" CHAPTER SEVEN 

1 

T FIRST Cora hated the idea. She had grown away being accepted as Mrs Wardell. Even though she knew their relationship was sometimes sniffed at suspiciously, she had had a different feeling in her association with their companions. However, one must live; and unless she wanted to go her own way alone again, this seemed the only answer to their problem. The distress she experienced at the mere thought of their separation proved to her that she still cared for Wardell; was unable to let him go even though he should degenerate into a procurer for her house. A common "pimp," most deplorable of men. Stare the facts in the face and decide. She gave him her diamonds.

It was pleasant, though, to fix up the house. To buy glittering new furniture and colorful drapes and rent a piano; to stock the pantry with glasses and wine, the cupboards with dishes, interview and engage help. It was hers, this roomy red brick house with the high ceilings and green shuttered windows. Before long the pride of ownership took possession of her. She walked

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

through the rooms and admired and touched, changed the position of a chair, rearranged a curtain, stood off and approved, and felt almost happy. But it had taken more money than Wardell had been able to procure on the diamonds; had taken nearly the whole of the thousand, brought out from its hiding place. "However," she reassured herself, "I'll always be able to get that much on the things even at auction."

It didn't take long, once they had decided. A week, and she was ready for the girls. Wardell produced them. 

His taste had been good; they were young and attractive. Two were slightly experienced, and the others gave promise. Mazie and Enid, chums, had just rounded seventeen, the age limit for inmates of a house; but they had solicited slyly for a year-ever since their interrupted school days. The other two, Myrtle and Edna, he had picked up in a dry goods store. There had been no coercion about it. They were from villages and had been having a hard time living on their small wages; had supplemented them surreptitiously at times, and for a year or so had considered entering a sporting house but had not known just how to go about it. They were older, twenty-two and twenty; Myrtle a good-looking red head; Edna small, rounded, with bold black eyes and a provoking black bang; vivacious and full blooded. 

Worthy had seen to everything, even a physician's examination. Worthy had also been to his old haunts and rounded up a bunch of men who had agreed to attend their opening party. Best of all, thought Cora as, clad in ruby satin, vividly rouged as in the old days, elaborately coiffured, looking every inch her position, she made the rounds of the rooms for final inspection-best of all Worthy had come out of his bad humors. He

SPORTING LADY 

< 211 » was even gay—and ardent once more-and once more confident that his luck had turned.

Q2 From the first they were successful. They did not make big money, of course; the house was not large enough. 

Seventy-five to a hundred a week, after paying rent and fines. And as Worthy was not gambling and her old wardrobe sufficed, they saved quite a bit of that. But the division of the receipts caused some argument and hard feelings. Cora felt that the larger share should go to her since it was her diamonds and money that had financed them, and she was devoting all of her time to the management of the place. But Worthy, with calm insistence, checked to himself the greater part. For future capital when the track opened, he contended; also, he had to spend at the rendezvous from which he brought her customers. Cora resented this; resented his continuous absences; began to demand explanations. Until this question of money had entered into their relationship they had never had words; now they seemed to be bickering continually.

Nor did things improve when the races opened. By that time she had become known in the town; had slid down the social scale she had occupied as Mrs Wardell, address the Galt House. Heads turned as she passed on the street, bravely attired, insolently staring; leading a beribboned white fuzzy dog who became as familiar in the downtown districts as his mistress herself. She no

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

longer entered the smart restaurants she had once graced, no longer encountered the bowing servitors of her former hotel. But shopping at the big dry goods stores, she felt her new importance; there experienced a form of vanity as she caught the nudgings and whispers of shop girls, the audible comments of customers who made way for her as she sailed haughtily through the aisles. 

It recalled the days in Kansas City, except that then she was "one of Annie Carter's girls," and companioned; now she was "Madam Julia" and always alone. Yes, though she had fallen in the social scale, she had risen in the ranks of her profession. 

In spite of the callousness which served her as she settled once more into the old routine, she had no desire to test it by attending the races. All of the old crowd were there, those former women comrades who had known her as the wife of a successful gambler. She did not wish to risk the awkwardness of encounters and resultant snubs. Of course they learned about her almost as soon as they arrived; but so long as she did not meet them it mattered little. Worthy himself had been the first to propose that she should not attend, and for once she agreed with him, though with a certain amount of resentment. He would not be compelled to forego his former friends because of possible ostracism. No need for them to know that he was being kept by a sporting woman; fed and clothed by money obtained in an illicit profession.

To forestall that, he moved to the Galt House during those weeks the ponies were running, and she saw him seldom and then by stealth. Though common sense told her it was the only thing for him to do, she nursed a bitterness that rankled—and she missed him.

SPORTING LADY 

<< 213 » 3o In spite of these efforts Worthy's luck still refused to perch on his shoulder. Everything he bet on lost. And as the hoodoo continued, he grew jumpy and lost what little self-assurance he had gained. His face grew haggard, the deep circles around his eyes grew blacker, imparting a sinister look to his countenance. His ill fortune became talked about; even his closest comrades grew cool, avoided him, as if mere association with one so marked might influence their own fortunes. And one day in the paddock inspecting the runners, he overheard a loser commenting upon his own "Wardell luck." He had become a byword.

All he had saved up during the months they had been operating soon went. Cora's followed, obtained by cajolery, hot arguments, veiled threats. When the meet closed both were stone broke, save for the income from the house. And even those receipts had fallen off. Hard times had hit Louisville. Some of the gambling houses closed, their habitués scattering to other cities or following the ponies to New Orleans.

Wardell returned to his bed and board at Madam Julia's, and arguments over the nightly receipts grew in violence. Cora was no match for him. And she was tired. God, how tired she was. And sick. There was a nauseous grinding pain in her side that gave her no peace, left her without spirit enough to refuse his demands. She grew lax in her duties, sometimes failing to appear in the parlour for several days and nights at a

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

time; lying in her tumbled bed, listlessly conscious that things were going to rack and ruin; knowing full well the servants and girls were stealing from her; but too apathetic to care much. As patronage fell off, Myrtle and Edna walked out on her and into a big house further downtown. Worthy brought her another girl-a stunning creature with golden hair and slumbering brown eyes. And before long, for the first time since their association, she began to suspect Worthy of infidelity. Once suspicion entered in, her morbid mind magnified every small incident into incriminating evidence. But she never found out if her suspicions were justified-at least, she never caught them red handed. 4 Wardell was playing cards again. As disastrously as before. Unknown to her he had mortgaged the furniture. 

When she had accidently discovered this, there had been a terrible scene; he had been insolent, sneered at her, and disappeared. This new misfortune awakened the old fighting spirit. Once more she roused herself and sought to straighten out her house; once more she and 

Moses, bedecked in finery, paraded the streets, shopped desultorily, that they might be seen and commented upon. Now every night she appeared in the parlour and watched her expenses, paring them down to the point of stinginess; pocketing all of the receipts; counting over her savings like a miser; keeping her eyes on the ominous future looming like a black wall before her.

It was a stormy night, and dull one. Business was

SPORTING LADY 

« 215 » almost at a standstill. A little past midnight she went to her room, eased herself out of her stays, mixed a long highball, and was sipping it and reading a paper-backed novel when she heard his step. Too often she had listened for that step to mistake it now; and if she had, Moses' bared teeth and low growl would have told her. 

As the door opened and he stood before her she saw that he had been drinking; that his mood was ugly. "Give me some money," he said quietly. "Quick, I'm in a hurry."

Before her mind marched the sums he had wheedled from her-thousands of dollars—all her savings; leaving her strapped save for a few hundreds she had managed to retrieve from the wreckage. All she had now to keep herself and Moses when this very furniture about her should be carried from the house. In that moment of anger the scales fell completely from her eyes and she saw him for what he was what she should have seen all along had not her infatuation blinded her. "You've had the last cent you are going to get from me. You've bled me for the last time. To Hell with you and all of your kind, you pimp. I'm through, do you

SPORTING LADY 

understand? Through with you for good. And take your blonde hussy with you when you go!" 

For a moment he stood staring at her unbelieving.

Then hell broke loose in his heart, and with a spring he seized her throat and pressed his long slim fingers tight, tighter a wild pleasure surging in his brain at her stifled gurgles. But only for a moment. With a snarl a small white form sprang at his leg, grabbed it, bit deeper and deeper into the flesh. With a wild curse he released his hold, strove to shake off the ferocious animal; seized a wrought-iron footstool and brought it down with an ominous crack across the dog's back; staggered to the door shouting: "In half an hour I'm coming back. Have that money ready or take the consequences, you dirty whore."

The door slammed and his feet, half running, echoed down the hall. But Cora did not hear them. She was on the floor beside the little white bundle of fur that was Moses. Already his bright eyes were glazing; feebly he tried to lick her hand, but the effort was too great for the little shattered frame, the valorous ebbing spirit. A faint convulsion ran over him and he lay inert in her arms. 5 

How long she lay with her face buried in his fur Cora did not know. It must have been a long time, for the little body had grown cold and stiff in her arms when she finally roused herself; got courage to run her fingers along the little backbone that ended in the fluffy white

SPORTING LADY 

« 217 » plume that never again would wag its welcome. Right in the middle it was, the jagged broken column of his spine. She lifted him up with her, cradled him in her arms, talking to him the baby talk he had understood so well, tasting the salt of the tears that streamed into her mouth at each word; carried him with her to the bureau and held him while she took from Wardell's drawer his pistol, squinted into its loaded barrel, turned the armchair she had occupied until it faced the door; settled the stiff little body more comfortably on her knees as she cocked the revolver and trained it on the doorway. "I'm going to kill him, Moses. He said he was coming back. When he comes, doggie, I'm going to let him have them all. Five barrels, as fast as I can pump them into him. I'm going to kill him for what he did to you, Moses."

Through the long hours of the night she sat with blank eyes, oblivious to the passage of time. But her mind was not blank. Through it raced the story of 

Moses-from the day, ages ago, when, on a flood marooned island he had been plucked from a treetop and entered into her heart; through the spirit-breaking months when she had washed dishes at the restaurant while she awaited the coming of her child-through the bright frivolous days at Annie Carter's-on the Joseph

Kinney on the circling journeys to the race meets. In her sharpened memory the days of his little dog life stood out clearly, each demonstration of love and loyalty lived over once more; each little cunning trick he had hated to learn was performed again on the film that passed before her eyes. The funny little way he "spoke," the craving for chocolates and raw carrots; his aversion

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

to raw meat; the patience with which he stood as she braided into the long white hair on his ears the colored ribbons that had always distinguished him from other dogs; the proud air with which he pranced beside her, lifting his feet high, turning his funny face with its long whiskers up to hers every few steps, as if to make sure it was she and no other who held his ribboned leash. Only for her he had lived-and for her he had died. Had offered up the only thing he had, his little dog life, to save her from harm.

The lamp beside her quivered, smoked acrid fumes, sputtered out. Through one undrawn shade the cold light of dawn began to outline the furniture, touching with gray ghost fingers the white bundle she held on her knees. On the window pane the rain spattered in gusty spurts, and wind rattled the casements in a staccato-like dirge. 

With an ugly laugh she laid aside the pistol and flexed her numb fingers. He had lied again. He had not come. His luck must have changed at last; else by now he too would be cold and stiff.

She heard the far off peal of the door-bell and, minutes afterward, the shuffling steps of her servant. She wondered dully what he could want at this hour-bade him enter. His eyes were popping white balls, his big mouth twisted nervously. "Madam-Mr Wardell had been shot. In a card game. 

He asked for you and the man says you must come quick to the hospital. He's dyin', Madam Julia!" 

She stared at him a moment as if uncomprehending.

Then inside something seemed to break; she burst into hilarious laughter, shrieked out words that the frightened negro scarcely understood: "Tell them-tell them to tell him-that I'm not

Jule's not coming!"

CHAPTER EIGHT 

ΚΑΙ 1 ATE CLARKE'S place was justly celebrated; far grander than Annie Carter's, much more elegant than Cora had believed a sporting house could be. It was very large, accommodating thirty girls. There was a room with mahogany bar presided over by white-coated bartenders; a circular ball room, velvet panelled, and displaying life-sized nude figures which they all considered works of art; a lounge-cardroom furnished in red leather, and a drawing room or "main parlour" with gilt and plush settees and chairs, countless mirrors and whatnots and cabinets. An Aubusson rug covered its inlaid floor and heavy velvet curtains veiled the windows. 

The girls themselves were superior in looks and deportment to those of Annie Carter; girls picked from a waiting list; Kate Clarke had no need of procurers. To be one of Kate Clarke's girls meant to have reached a place in the business that could be topped only by establishing a house of one's own. There was an elegance and refinement about them, a silky, luxurious look that

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

spoke well for Madam Clarke's taste and resources. To 

Cora, schooled in the smaller, freer environment of a house in a town just kicking itself loose from pioneer shacks and mud, the girls seemed snobbish and full of airs. There were no real friendships between any of them. There was too much envy and jealousy, too great a desire to outdo each other in the matter of raiment; too much suspicion and distrust. That was the way it impressed her at first and the impression remained through the years she spent among them. Even when they moved on and fresh recruits took their places it was much the same. The new girls quickly adopted the ways and manners of those remaining behind. Just as 

Cora herself did unconsciously; because she was adaptable; perhaps also because she secretly admired the poise and hauteur and silken elegance of her new companions. 

Kate Clarke herself was a very matriarch of a madam. 

Tall, thin, stern-visaged, with a bold hawk-like nose, shrewd black eyes and gray masses of hair piled high upon an arrogant head, she swept into the parlours every evening at precisely eleven, quite as a queen might appear before her subjects. There was always a little stir, a sort of breathless waiting, just before the wide doors swung apart to admit her. She affected elaborate trained costumes of heavy black silk ornamented with rosepoint or Brussels lace, diamond earrings the size of filberts, a huge sunburst of pure white stones. She was a stern mistress, strict about the conduct of the girls, not inviting confidences and certainly not giving them. 

Perhaps Cora came as near to a glimpse into her shuttered heart as any of them; and that had been only a glimpse, arousing a curiosity which Madam never appeased.

SPORTING LADY 

« 221 » Cora was lucky in gaining admittance without a long wait. After the death of Moses, and Wardell, after the collapse that followed her outburst of hysteria, she found herself in a hospital. There had been an operation -that pain in her side; not a very serious one but sufficiently so to confine her to bed for several weeks; weeks of complete relaxation; calm days, staring at the ceiling, too tired to remember; white nights when she lay drowsy and inert but never wholly asleep. Mild noiseless nurses moving about attending her every wish; calmness, peace. And no disease-they had found no trace of disease. She had been afraid of the pain in her side; afraid perhaps Wardell-but the doctors had said no. That was good; with that assurance she could pluck up heart and carry on when the time came.

It came early in the new year and just before her money gave out. She was amazed at the bills tendered her; counted over her money carefully; grew frightened. But the correction of her disorder, the weeks of rest, had given her new courage. There remained enough to take her to St Louis, and to St Louis she went, the day she was discharged from the hospital. 

It had been difficult to see Kate Clarke. Time and again she went to the huge dull brick house with its dark red shutters, only to be denied access to its mistress. 

Impossible to get past the blank-faced doorman and his reiterated phrase, "Madam's rooms are all taken." 

She moved from her rooming house to one that was less particular and more central, and with her boodle bag emptied by the advance payment of rent, and with the few clothes she had brought in her Gladstone reposing on a pawnbroker's shelves, was reduced to soliciting on the streets. It was a terrible step downward, but

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

what could one do? At any rate it provided food and lodging. This couldn't last forever. It was only temporary. Even then her courage did not desert her. 

One weary day when snow clung in wet chunks to her shoulders, and bedraggled skirts swept up the slush above her shoetops, she returned to her shabby room from an unproductive stroll and sat down to think things out. The result was a letter to Kate Clarke (she wondered why she had not thought of that before); a letter inspired by her distress and ringing with sincerity. 

Wayne Hendrix was its subject; something whispered to her to play him up. He had said Kate Clarke was his friend. She embroidered his brief bits of conversation about Kate and almost herself believed all she wrote. 

By return mail came an answer. She was to call at once. 2 "So Wayne Hendrix talked of me?" Kate Clarke's voice was gentler than Cora had ever expected to hear it. "Tell me about him, my dear. I have often wondered what became of him. I might have known something terrible had happened. He was very dear to me. Of course I was much older-he was more like a son- And Cora told her of their romance and its tragic end. Cora often wondered just what Wayne had been to 

Madam Clarke, but that one little glimpse into a heart that seemed frozen over was the last she was to have until some years later-in 1882, when the screaming headlines of extras announced the dastardly murder of 

Jesse James by Bob Ford. Shot in the back, unarmed, unwarned. That day Kate Clarke had summoned Cora, had gravely laid the paper before her, had talked sadly and proudly of him and his exploits, since the time when, a lad of seventeen, he had joined up with Quantrill. It must have been years since she had mentioned to anyone the name of her dead lover; only a tragedy like this, raising the ghost of buried memory, could call it forth. After that it was again as though they had never shared a confidence. Cora became, as she had been before, merely one of the girls, under the same strict surveillance; a part of her merchandise and means of livelihood.

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

3 Cora was twenty-five when she entered Kate Clarke's house. In any other walk of life that would have been young; in any other profession she would have been at the beginning of her career. But in this business the flame of life burned fiercely, devouringly. Youth and health were their only assets; youth and health and a certain good looks that soon, very very soon, departed. 

From three to fifteen years, at most, were all they could hope for. Some sputtered out pitifully early; others, by the use of common sense, great care and greater luck, managed to hold on much longer. But all were finished, at least for the better houses, at thirty-five. Then began the sinking period, further and further into the depths till they touched the lowest strata of society. Some became procuresses, exploited by men who controlled them; some, like Juanita, became inmates of brothels so low, so degenerate, that even the girls now sailing the high seas of success in what was, after all, the same profession, turned in disgust from the thought of them. But they would come to it, those who did not have the good fortune to marry or to become madams themselves; or to die. That was a possible way out. Disease, of which they were always wary, were always striving to guard against; or suicide, which they might court. Ten percent snuffed out each year, the one way or the other. 

At twenty-five Cora was in the full bloom of maturity; but she was old, and wise, in experience. She had few illusions about the life she was living. She had

SPORTING LADY 

« 225 » talked with too many girls, lived through too many annoyances, was too practical and hard headed, not to know. She had no intention of remaining what she was until she began the slippery toboggan that led to the brothel or the river. Carefully she planned, wisely she saved, during the prosperous years, and never lost sight of her ambition to some day have a place of her own, with others performing the duties she now was forced to fulfill herself. She had had some experience-in a small way, to be sure. She had made mistakes in the 

Louisville house that had taught her more than contemplation and theories could do. This time at Kate 

Clarke's she looked upon as a post-graduate course of which she was determined to take full advantage, against the time when she would use her knowledge for her own benefit and profit.

Shrewdly she observed, pried, questioned. There was little in the running of the house that escaped her. And when her time came she would be well prepared.

HE years stretched out-three, four, five. One by

strom; burning the candle at both ends, flaming with an unquenchable inward fire that in a few short years left them old and worn and undesirable. And still her face remained smooth and firm, her eyes clear and uncircled. She drank but little, smoked scarcely at all; she took excellent care of herself; and she no longer allowed herself the luxury of emotions. Calm, undisturbed, still and poised. As the years passed by and all who had been there when she came had passed on over one route or another, she assumed a position which none of them were able to dispute successfully; second only to Madam 

Clarke. Madam Clarke who now did not appear every evening; who was growing feeble, and needed her strength to combat the dread white disease which, to all apparent, had gripped her. Little by little Cora took over her duties, filching them one by one; until at last, upon those nights when Madam did not descend, she stood in her place, as watchful, as eagle-eyed and obdurate as Kate Clarke herself. The girls did not like her, but they respected her; and after a few controversies in which she was backed up by Madam Clarke, herself, the sought position was duly recognized. 

This slowly managed success did not turn her head, but it did develop initiative. She contrived new styles always just a bit ahead of prevailing fashions. Her taste was quiet but sure; and as her confidence grew she began to experiment with her own person. Blondining had just come into vogue, frowned upon by the conservative and eagerly taken up by the more adventurous of the sporting world. Without hesitation Cora applied the bleach. "I can always dye back," she argued. The result was an entire success. With her clear dusky complexion and amber eyes always artfully outlined, she became a different personality. More stunning and eye-arresting;

SPORTING LADY 

« 227 » and likewise, more undeniably stamped as to her occupation. But for that she did not care. Like the good business woman she was, she believed in advertising her wares. The queer silly complex about being considered "respectable" had vanished with her rise in her own world.

So the next few years marched on while Cora held her youth, a refutation to the general belief that such lives were doomed to be brief, if merry. And those years she had lived out before Kate Clarke's faded into dimness; became as a dream on which she seldom dwelt. 

The memory of Flora and Ed and Mark Creighton seldom intruded. She almost never thought back to 

Kansas City. That had been another life in which Cora 

Simmons had died. Glittering, golden-haired, sumptuous

Julia Wardell had arisen, fully matured, from her poor ashes. 79 2 It had been a sardonic streak in her, to take his name; a satisfying revenge for the epithet he had hurled at her that last night she saw him alive. To make it notorious was just retribution, too, for his disinclination to make it hers legally, when he could have done so with little trouble had he desired. She saw that clearly, after he was dead and she had ceased to care. Now she hoped, and sometimes believed, that somewhere up there in the ether he knew it and writhed at the knowledge. 

For she supposed he was up there in that vague place that passed for heaven. After all, cheating at cards had

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

been his big fault and she did not believe it sufficient to condemn him to everlasting fire.

Her ideas of religion were scanty and confused and seldom brought out for thought. All she knew was that some day she would repose under six feet or more of earth and that the worms would eat her; and this picture was so distasteful that she shudderingly pushed it back when it intruded. After that, after she was buried, she would go to heaven. For had she ever done a wrong thing in her life? She had not. She might have-she might have killed Worthy that night in the violence of her grief and anger. But God had saved her from that sin by letting someone else do it. She had even sacrificed herself and left Ed and Cora to their own lives; that alone would count largely in her favor when she stood at last before the Judgment Seat. She firmly believed this. And she as firmly believed that there was no sin in her mode of life. Her body was her own; had been given to her to do with as she thought wise. If it could make for her a satisfactory and not unpleasant living; that in itself was a sign that she had done no wrong in making use of her body.

The girls among whom she had lived her mature, thinking years had often talked over the subject. They were all of the same mind. Then, too, did she not give generously to charity? Never a week, it seemed, but the churches asked for donations; and she never refused, even when she was not so flush. She had learned that from Annie Carter, and Kate Clarke had also instilled it into her girls. "Don't antagonize the churches," they had both held. "It is better to be friends than enemies. 

They can do us lots of harm.” 

Yes, Cora had no doubt that when her time cameyears and years from now, when she was very old and

SPORTING LADY 

« 229 » had tasted all life had to give and was no longer attractive and in demand-she would find heavenly peace. 

Sometimes Cora thought about Irene and wondered how things were with her. She had not heard from her for a long long time since a year after she had entered her present haven. She had written to Irene giving her new address and long months after had come a letter from Denver. A rather disconcerting letter. Irene's exuberant happiness seemed to have evaporated. Cora herself had always thought it tempting fate to go out to Denver where Charley's people lived. Irene had seemed positive that her past could not follow her so far; but one's past has a way sometimes of cropping up most unexpectedly. So Irene had intimated in her letter. His family had ostracized her, shut her out; and though she did not say so, Cora's natural shrewdness completed the picture: Charley feeling their position, the first ardent days of marriage waning; isolation and tears for Irene; perhaps, eventually, aloneness.

Cora wrote to her, affectionately and encouragingly, but her letter never was answered. Evidently Irene had written that one time only to tell of the adoption of Cora's boy. Irene had been unable to have children of her own and had finally wheedled Charley into saying she could take him. She had been delighted and had written at once to the Orphan Home, only to receive word that he had been adopted by a family whose identity they could not disclose. "Well," thought Cora, "that's fine. Now I shall never have to worry about him again." Not that she ever had. 

The child had never seemed real to her. At least, not after his arrival. He had been real enough that weary summer she had washed dishes at the ten cent restaurant. 

But now everything was all right, and he would have

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

a chance in life that she could never have given him even if Wardell had lived and permitted her to have him with them. And of course, under present conditions, no matter how wealthy she might become, in her professional life the presence of a child would have made things most awkward, for them both. 

Yes, life had turned a smiling face on her at last and the future looked secure and bright, even though she had never had a real lover since Worthy, and was about to pass her thirtieth birthday.

CHAPTER TEN 

IT 1 T WAS in the summer of 1885 that Cora first met "Big Tim" Conners, of Kansas City. That meeting proved a turning point in her life. Of course she had heard of him before-who in Missouri had not? But she was not interested in politics except in local elections whose outcome might threaten their trade; and as their party was well-nigh impregnable, there was seldom real uneasiness over the outcome. "Big Tim" was the "boss" of the state; the man who ruled with iron hand the party that controlled it. Maker of mayors and governors and senators; appointee of the big and little jobs for which the great and small scrambled. Possessed of an immense energy, shrewd, unscrupulous, a grafter in every sense of the word, he had attained

SPORTING LADY 

« 231 » to a position of power that none might challenge. In the aftermath of the Civil War he had gathered up the reins of the struggling young city between the Missouri and the Kaw. A genius at organization, unerring in his estimate of men, hated and feared and reviled by his enemies but capable of inspiring fanatical loyalty among his followers, he had built up the machine of his party and the juggernaut he had created had crushed and maimed the few who stood for decency and honesty and fair government. Arrogant and unshakable, he had demanded tribute and tribute he had received. By 1885 his interests in saloons and breweries, in business property and farms and banks and other less legitimate enterprises rated him in the millionaire class, but the resources of the young city were as yet limited, and it is questionable whether he was really that rich. Yet it was undoubtedly true that of those resources he pilfered what he could. 

The night of Cora's first glimpse of Big Tim, Madam

Clarke had remained in her room after a trying day, and Cora, in her self-assumed position, was in charge of the parlours. Word had reached her that the big boss, on a business trip down from Kansas City, might pay the famous house a visit. She was interested, but not unduly so. Many well-known men had passed through these rooms in the five years she had been here. She had found them much the same as those others who frequented the place. Men, be they great or small, were much alike in the parlours of a sporting house, she had learned.

He came with a party of St Louis politicians, most of whom had been there before. A couple were clients of her own and greeted her with friendly familiarity as if proud to show off before the big gun. Nearly all had

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

been drinking. The girls were excited and mettlesome and after wine had been ordered for the house, ordered by magnum and drunk like soda water, the company grew unduly hilarious. The three man orchestra quite outdid itself, the dancing grew madder and madder. As the huge bottles were emptied and carried off to be replaced by others, several of the girls became slightly drunk, one or two of the men grew rough. Cora barely tasted her glass; she needed all her wits to cope with a situation that might well get out of hand. From group to group she passed, whispering an admonition here, taking some of the girls aside for severer reprimands; pleasantly and tactfully, but firmly, reproving those of the visitors who infringed on the social rules of the house.

She was wearing a black lace dress, curving largely over her bosom, gathered at the back under the pointed tight-fitting basque, into a huge bustle which draped down with flounces ending in a long train. With her unusual height and golden crown of hair she was the most striking woman in the room. There was a dignity and assurance about her that quite dwarfed the others, younger, fresher, though they were. 

She knew that she was performing well; that "Big 

Tim," seated apart from the crowd with the prettiest girl of the house companioning him, drinking whiskey steadily, was watching her with interest, thoughtfully. 

And not possessively. Which was well. Under the conditions she could not leave the rooms tonight for even a little while; it would need only that for the befuddled girls and their guests to get out of hand. Still, it might be tactless to refuse. She was relieved when, returning from the ballroom, she saw that Helen had disappeared with him. Nor did she see him again that night. And

SPORTING LADY 

« 233 » as with many others who were not regular frequenters, she forgot him. 2 It was early in the new year when she received word from him. He wanted to have a talk with her. He would call at three that afternoon if she was disengaged.

She was alone in the main parlour when he arrived and greeted him formally. She surmised that this was to be no amorous adventure; she had known from the first night that he was not attracted to her in that way. 

The occasion called for her grand manner. 

He ordered some beer and settled himself negligently, puffing thoughtfully on a huge black cigar. He did not keep her waiting long, but came directly to the point. "Our town," he opened, "is on the verge of a great boom. In fact, it has already started and it's going to bust the city wide open. How long it will last is problematical, but those who get in at the start are going to make a lot of money. "Now, you know me; and you know that up there in Kansas City what I say goes. I'm aiming to make my pile these next few years, and those who trail along with me are going to share my fortunes. "To get down to brass tacks, we need a fine house in our town. One that will show up favorably with this one. We're way behind St Louie when it comes to sporting houses. Up to now, Annie Carter has done well enough, but we're going to need finer ones these next few years. And anyway, Annie Carter is slipping. She

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

never did have much class and now she's on the down grade. Second rate. I propose to give K.C. something she can be proud of. A real show place. And at the same time I mean to make money out of it; don't ever think anything else. This is not a philanthropic proposition. 

Do you savvy?” "I savvy all right. It sounds interesting." "It is. Now listen. I've got just the place up there and I mean to spend a good-sized roll on it. When I start a thing I start it right. I aim to have as swell a bunch of girls as can be got together; and I mean to treat 'em good. They've got to be high class. No second raters. 

Refined and classy. Savvy?" "Yes." It was hard to repress her enthusiasm. If he would only get on with it-to where she came in. "Now, I was watching you, that time I was here, and 

I've come to the conclusion you've got a career ahead of you. Afterwards I made inquiries about you-oh, I don't go into anything without getting the facts firstand what I heard convinced me that you are just the one I want to run the place. No, let me finish, then you can talk. We've got to understand each other perfectly right from the start, then there's no grouching later. "Now, I'm willing to be extra generous with you. 

Listen to this. This is my proposition. I'll fix up the place and furnish it. If you've got any money you want to put in, that's all right-but not necessary. Twenty rooms besides the reception rooms is about right, I think. 

We can always enlarge if necessary. But that's a nice cozy size to start off with, and big enough to make money. Now. You are to pay me $200 a month rent for the house and $100 for the furnishings. That lets you off easy. We'll split the profits-forty-sixty. Forty to me-and sixty for you. I ain't a hog; I'll be satisfied to

SPORTING LADY 

«235 » clear a thousand a month; we ought to do that easy, and I'm figuring it will be more. With my pull your clientèle is readymade for you. Room rents will be from $20 to $40 a month. That'll take care of your rent, servants and incidentals and leave some over. Fines won't run more than $20 to $25 a month and you can rest assured you won't be molested. Well, what do you say? Sound good to you?" "It sounds grand, Mr Conners. "Then you'll go in with me?" "When can we begin?" her laugh was high-pitched and excited. He looked at her shrewdly, smiled, patted her arm. "Right away. You'd better make plans to leave here by the end of the week. You'll have to supervise the decorations and furnishing and look over the roomers. 

I've got some rounded up, but I've too many irons in the fire to bother about details. Oh, and one thing more, so there'll be no misunderstandings. Now get this-you are a fine looking woman, Julia-and you've got everything I like. But—I never mix business with pleasure.

Ours will be a strictly financial partnership. You won't forget that?" "I won't forget that," and the darkened lids lowered provocatively. "Hell! I hope I don't. Now what say we break a bottle of wine and seal our contract?"

BOOK III CHAPTER ONE 

1 'HE Kansas City "boom" was on. From eastern cities, from villages and from farms people poured into the town on the bluffs. Almost over night real estate prices had sky-rocketed. There were not enough carpenters to run up the shaky frame buildings hastily thrown together to house the new citizens. Farmers, catching the fever, sold out and joined the mad scramble; became builders and ditch diggers; opened small stores or real estate offices. "Real Estate and Loans."

Hundreds of such signs. Farms far on the outskirts being cut up into building lots and selling for ten, fifteen times their value. San Francisco and Chicago had installed cable cars to take the place of horse and mule drawn ones. Very well, Kansas City, the new metropolis, would do likewise. Third in the world to have a cable line. Bridging the great bluff down to the new Union 

Station in the Bottoms with a spindly, perilous incline that was to deal death and destruction in the years to come. Electric lights emerging from the terror of the gas plant explosion. Factories, schools and churches. Mc- 

Gee Addition built up. Rows of ugly cottages and thin brick buildings radiating out from the business center. « 237 »

«238» 

SPORTING LADY 

Stock-yards milling with beasts destined for slaughter; packing houses springing up, to receive their bleeding carcasses. Eastern capital pouring in. Work for everyone at unbelievable wages. Not enough men for the jobs. Trains shooting in and out-to St Louis, to the east, to the desert and the Pacific coast. Bridges spanning the Missouri; "Rapid Transit" connecting Wyandotte, in Kansas. Center of the United States, strategic point of the whole vast country. Give them a few more years and they'd pass Chicago, in railroads, packing houses, stock-yards. Beaming boosters, loyal city fathers. High toned "first families," drawing the lines of social intercourse closer and closer against the invaders. A town grown mad with lust for the gold that lay on those bluffs, overriding business discretion, common sense, all laws of transmutation. 

And a town wide open. Gambling dens; palaces of chance; mecca of confidence men, floaters and criminals and gamblers from the east, from the coast, from the mining towns of Colorado. A red light district twinkling and scintillating and raking in gold where gold was so plentiful. No need to send out scouts to entice visitors. Doors closed and barred by chains that proclaimed the houses "sold out."

Crooked politicians, unwatched, unnoticed; no time now for civic surveillance. Clean house later. Buy, sell, plat, build, while the sun of prosperity shone down so benignly. Shrewd politicians, sharper than their boom mad neighbors, reaching out and taking, strengthening their powers, making solid and sound their stand against the time when the bubble should burst; petty grafters, skulking lawyers, "reasonable" judges; and those crafty few of brains and vision oiling and tuning up the machine which would spew them out more gold than all

SPORTING LADY 

« 239» the cut up farm lands this side of Brush Creek. At their head, holding them all firmly down below his level, the "Big Boss," Tim Conners, towering above them all, issuing his orders, jerking up or kicking out; growing in power and prestige, generating greater hatred, sterner loyalty. Making his pile. 2. 

Quality Hill, overlooking the industrial Bottoms, whose black belching chimneys and creeping tracks of freight cars stretched across the Kaw into Wyandotte; Quality 

Hill, basking under a western sun that ploughed its way through fields of cotton clouds to sink in grandeur upon the plains of Kansas. Here, turreted homes of the elite sat in solid dignity amidst large shady lawns, ornamented by tinkling fountains and stone benches and velvety croquet grounds; their gates guarded by wrought-iron black boys, grinning at the stamping, pawing spans of horses before victorias and landaus which were hitched to their outstretched hands. Conservative cultivated Quality Hill, jealous of her prestige, scanning cautiously and critically all who knocked for admittance at her doors.

In one of the largest of these homes, red brick, cupola crowned, highly ornamented with machine-turned curlicues and staring out through windows bordered with stained glass, dwelt in apparent harmony and assured dignity, the Mark Creightons. Ten years had brought changes to them, aside from the doubling of their family. Flora Creighton, she who had been Flora

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

Simmons, could well look back over the years and smile contentedly at the realization of her dreams come true. 

Richer dreams than she had reached for back on the old farm in Clay County. The nice home with the yard had materialized—and three hired girls, and a coachman.

She attended the receptions to which she had aspiredtoo many. She was an indefatigable worker in the 

Church. That imposing edifice was always written, in her mind, with a capital. Her fashionable clothes were made at Bullene's; she possessed not only a diamond sunburst, but rings and earrings; she had two children, a boy and a girl, and they excelled not only in music lessons but in ballroom dancing and elocution and all the smart little airy graces of quality children of their time. 

Nine and six. Junior and Florence. Only the trip to

Europe had not come to pass; but it would. The children were not yet "out of the way."

To give Flora credit she was a good mother, if a strict one. Watchful. Hour after hour she sat with the children at their practice on violin and piano; she accompanied them each Saturday to their dancing lesson; fashioned little butterfly dresses for Florence and curled her long golden hair around her finger; proudly dressed

Junior in Fauntleroy suits, trimmed his fingernails and corrected his manners. Always, she was correcting his manners. Yes, Flora was bound up in her yellow-haired offspring. Too bound up, Mark had complained at first. "As if," Flora had flung back, righteously indignant, "one could be too bound up!"

Mark had long ago given up complaining about Flora's absorption in the children and her church and social duties, excluding from her interest only himself; or so it had seemed to him at first. But he soon got over that; soon grew quite contented that Flora had found some-

SPORTING LADY 

< 241 » thing to occupy her, leaving him free for his own work and companions. It had taken only a few years to drain 

Flora's mind of all that could interest his. It was a little mind, going its treadmill way, issuing through her delicate lips in routine phrases picked up here and there as they attracted her attention; becoming familiar and threadbare through frequent utterance. But he was not dissatisfied. She made a comfortable home for him, with the assistance of the servants; she was an excellent mother; and he could swear by all he held holy that she was chastity itself; a virgin who by some miracle had become a mother.

Ten years had done things to Mark Creighton, also. 

Added weight had taken away that clean-cut leanness of his face. Gray hairs showed in the crisply waving black curls; dark puffs underlay the handsome eyes; the suggestion of a paunch marred his erect slenderness. All the toll of prosperity; the effort to obtain it, the rich foods and hard liquor it contributed. 

And he had prospered; partly because he was a shrewd lawyer; partly because at the death of his father he had inherited a comfortable fortune which wise investments had augmented. With his future assured and his home life arranged, he had settled down, before his time, into a somewhat stodgy middle age, before middle age should have caught up with him. Cards, male friends, an occasional stag drinking party were his relaxations. His duties, demanded by his wife and passively accepted as a well-trained husband, comprised deaconship in the Church, escort to such social functions as he could not evade, and the lending of his presence to certain social affairs with which Flora repaid her obligations. Duties that irked, but were not unbearable. On the whole, he was contented. Life was smooth, if not exhilarating. If

he sometimes looked back and regretted the passing of the eager, ambitious youth he had been, it was not often or for long. Life had become too comfortable, too well established in a routine that increasing laziness of mind and body did not care to disturb. Flora had all that she desired, his children would be amply provided for. So why worry over what might have been? 3 Out on Independence Avenue, in a white stone house whose driveway wound some distance through treeshaded lawns before ending under an impressive porte cochere, Mr and Mrs Edward Simmons dwelt grandly and luxuriously, as befitted the Mrs Simmons whose father had been one of the founders of the city. Though rich in family history (at least for Jackson County) the Hendersons were financially poor. Poor, but oh so proud.

Both were fully aware that she had exchanged her background for comfort and security; a fair exchange and one that was satisfactory to both. 

Through the years of their marriage they had remained to each other as strangers domiciled by circumstances under the same roof, sharing their meals and their social affairs and their bank account, but never in any instance emerging from their locked up personalities to fuse together by misfortune or grief or great happiness into a fluid moment of oneness.

Perhaps Ed had a shade the advantage in the arrangement; for, though he feared and respected her for her aloofness, her calm haughtiness, she imbued him with a feeling of inferiority, to combat which he threw himself even more energetically than before into his career; doggedly determined to measure up to her through his own efforts, and eradicate his common antecedents by brilliant achievements. 

That they were brilliant none gainsaid. Early he had hitched his wagon to the rising star of the "Big Boss"; and though there were many whispered innuendoes and slurring criticisms as to the legitimacy of some of his transactions, he was covered by the cloak of Tim Conners, and investigations did not harass him. At thirty-six he had turned down offers of political jobs and had concentrated upon his chosen career of criminal lawyer.

At thirty-six he was tall, thin and gaunt of feature; possessed of piercing eyes which bored through the defenses of his opponents, and a vitriolic tongue capable of impassioned harangues which swayed juries from their better judgment. That they had no children seemed no loss to either. 4 This then was the lay which Madam Julia Wardell (she who had been Cora Simmons and Kate Black) found spread before her on the map of Kansas City when, her own ambitions about to be realized, she returned once more to the city on the Kaw; a city that gladdened her heart as her practical mind grasped all the advantages it offered. In the ten years she had been away it had grown unbelievably. Not only in length and breadth.

Its heart was pulsing and throbbing with the effort of emerging from adolescence into maturity. Energy emanated from its very pavements, galvanized, spread out and gripped its crowded thoroughfares. Nervous excitement filled the air. Discretion was thrown to the winds; inflamed minds saw no end to the wonder. The lid was off, the sky the limit. Buy, sell, spend, revel in the unbelievable gold that surfaced the city lotsmore lay beyond the present limits, and tomorrow was another today. 

Big Tim Conners had been right; Big Tim Conners was always right. He had not misled her with his bright predictions. When Madam Julia, after weeks of prepara-

SPORTING LADY 

< 245 » tion, after an orgy of selecting and buying, with a bank account at her disposal that seemed endless, stood at last on that day of her grand opening, her big party, and looked around her and beyond her, from the drawing room through the card room, beyond to the long glittering, mirrored dance hall with its glimpse of mahogany bar at the further end, she drew a deep quivering sigh, as if for the first time, after days of preparation and planning and purchasing and arranging, when she had visualized only one room at a time, she first beheld the place as a whole—a unit, perfect and imposing. Her house and her frame. And with the sigh she had approached close to humility, and she had stood in the center of the room and closed her eyes and whispered, almost aloud-"Thanks, God, for all your goodness to me-at last."

Big Tim, who thought of everything. "I have too many affiliations with Kansas

Hers. A sitting room in gilt and satin and velvet, with chairs that were large and comfortable. Madam Julia was too big, and wise, to surround herself with spindling furniture. Brown, blonde and dark girls; plump and slim, common of face and patrician. Girls for all tastes.

< 247 » Madam Wardell was well aware that she was looking her best in the extremely low-cut black velvet whose long train accentuated her regal height. Her figure, grown increasingly plump in maturity, imparted dignity, majesty; further accentuated by the elaborately dressed and haughtily carried golden head; and by the consciousness that now, indeed, was she mistress of all she surveyed, including the human chattels surrounding her. This, the eve of her triumph, was a far cry from that night of her début in the brick house a block to the north of her. She had indeed travelled a long way from the girl who had sat alone, nervous and selfconscious-until Mark Creighton had stood looking, had ambled towards her; and she had sat drinking beer and listening to his rambling talk until fit to drop with fatigue. Mark, now married to her sister. Why, they were relatives; she hadn't somehow, thought of that before. Let the girls squirm restlessly, while she travelled back and tasted her triumph. This night would never come again. Savour it, by contrasting memories of other days. If Mark would only appear her night would be complete. All but forgotten in the passing of the years, his memory had been resurrected here on her old stamping ground. She had sent him an invitation, as she had sent them to all she had known in the old days; but across his she had written-"Do come and let us renew an old friendship." Perhaps he would be curious. He could have no inkling that Madam Wardell, the fame of whose new house was already abroad in the city, was none other than his old confidante, Kate Black.
