"I have asked you to come in to impress upon you the rules of this house. Now I mean to treat you girls right and to play fair and square with you. I expect you to do the same with me. I have no intention of being too critical, and I'm not going to go snooping and prying around spying on you. As long as you follow the rules I'm about to read and a copy of which will be placed in each room, we'll get along together all right. 

But understand right from the start that I shan't stand for a single infringement of any of them. Try it, and out you go. Here they are:

First-No drunkenness; and no arguing or quarreling in the parlours. 

Second-No smoking except in your own rooms. The men who will come here are high class and they demand refinement in the girls.

Third-Strict honesty about personal effects. No pilfering of jewelry or money if a man happens to be a little drunk or otherwise irresponsible. Anything left behind must be immediately turned over to me, to be returned to the customer. 

Fourth-No vulgar dancing, or vulgarity of any kind in the parlours. What happens in your rooms is your own affair.

Fifth-On the streets and in the stores and public places you must conduct yourselves like ladies. No loud dressing. No oggling. And no very conspicuous makeup.

Sixth-The hours on duty shall be strictly observed and no one is to be excused except for sickness or by special permission.

Seventh-Medical examination twice a month, and no one is excused from it for any reason whatsoever. Also, I may say that if the medical report is unsatisfactory,

CHAPTER TWO 

Now the 1 the crest of the wave for Julia Wardell. On the up and up through these prosperous days while the boom boomed and with it the house on Fourth and Wyandotte. Growing more arrogant and self-satisfied with each week that her reputation spread out through the city. Being seen places. In her open carriage, beplumed and sumptuous, accompanied by one or more pretty girls and loudly dressed sporting men, driving at parade through the residential districts; racing behind a spanking team out to Fifteenth Street on Sundays; occupying a choice box at the Coates House; attending the

Exposition and the Priests of Pallas Parade; present always at the track during the racing season. Sweeping, with head held high but with friendly smiles for the sales-women, through Bullene's and Doggett's and Bernheimers, while the crowds in the aisles opened a way for her, sibilant whispers ran back behind her, shop girls hastened to wait upon her, admiring, envious—ambitious. Was there any chance, Madam? Sorry, no local girls.-Queen of the half-world.

Growing reputation among the men-among the

SPORTING LADY 

«253» mostly married men who made up the house's clientèle. 

Square-honest—no jipping. And close-mouthed. Never a gleam of recognition from Julia Wardell's tawny eyes, out in the city; never a whisper abroad to betray them. 

Whatever went on down there in the North End was sealed within the locked and chained door. Security and secrecy for their philanderings.

Growing reputation among the wives and sweethearts and among just women. Heroine of slanderous and erotic stories spread abroad behind pink palms and an accompaniment of giggles; bearer of a name whispered secretively in groups of women, unmentionable before their attendant males. Curious questionings and forthcoming information proudly given by her modiste and bootmaker, her hairdresser and manicurist; information embroidered by restless neurotic imaginations until it grew into a gaudy garment to bedeck her naked soul. If soul she had. There were those who doubted it; those to whom the name was anathema. To Flora, adding the bitterness of her anger to the bitterness of Ed's. "Why can't you do something?" despair rising into a piercing shriek that threatened to penetrate the heavy mahogany doors of the Creighton library. "You did once. You got rid of her once. Why can't you do it again?" Ed, through helpless irritation, striving to explain. "I've told you. The house belongs to Big Tim Conners. 

"All the more reason you should have a pull. Make him get someone else. Run her out." "That would be hitting his pocketbook. The woman is coining money for him. She's one of his pet projects.” "You could try. She'll ruin you, too. Why don't you try?"

SPORTING LADY 

"It's useless." "You'll be sorry when Mabel hears." "Mabel won't. Cora won't talk. I've been to see her.

All she wants is to be let alone. She's promised if we leave her alone she won't trouble us." "Oh, my God! If Mark ever hears of this!" "Mark! Humph! Say-how is Mark nowadays? I never see him any more. Very busy?" "Too busy ever to have an evening for me. And I can tell you I'm getting sick and tired of that, too. Back to the office every night after dinner and never home until all hours. I might as well be a widow, for all the good he is as an escort." "That so? Too bad. Now I wouldn't worry any more about Cora. We can't do anything about it at present, so we'll just have to grin and bear it. It might be worse. 

Anyway, I'm sure she won't do any talking. We'll have to be satisfied with that." 

And Julia Wardell, while her name was running through the streets, presiding over thronged and outwardly gay parlours, ruling her girls with an inflexible rod, staging her entrance each night at eleven, counting her receipts early each morning. Those receipts, which compensated for the hundred vexatious problems; sordid ugly things that went hand in hand with her business and must be faced and solved. All in the day's work.

The jealous fight between Helen and Lillian, and Helen, with her razor-slashed arms, sent secretly from town with a gold-lined purse and orders from Big Tim to stay away or take the consequences. 

The chained door stormed one Sunday morning by a hulking farmer and two burly country constables,

SPORTING LADY 

« 255 » and Marion, only two weeks an inmate, dragged from her bed, bundled into her clothes, and led kicking and scratching away to her old farm home.

And Sissy, meek and mild as milk, timidly displaying her charms to a new customer; reading with dilated, horror-stricken eyes the name tattooed upon his armthe name of her brother, separated when the home was broken up in childhood. A tragic weeping moment that, with brother and sister hysterical, and Madam investigating the rumpus; Madam quieting them and advising him to take Sissy away with him to his home in Lincoln, to his wife and family. Madam was like that-kind and understanding when it pleased her to be; take Sissy's word for it, Madam was good.

Then there was the story of Beulah. Beulah, who had come out of nowhere, undernourished and ragged. 

Madam had taken pity on her, and fed her and dressed her and taught her manners. Beulah was beautiful, with corn colored hair and deep gray eyes, but from the first she had been something of a problem. Because of her moods. For days she would scintillate with a gaiety and abandon that amounted almost to wildness-alluring and provocative to the men whom she charmed, but requiring a constant surveillance from her ever watchful guardian. Then would follow days of deep melancholy and weeping depression, when she refused to descend to the parlours and barricaded herself in her room, breaking the rule of the house and presenting a bad example to the other girls. On several such occasions 

Madam had seriously considered getting rid of her; but when Beulah herself decided to leave, the case assumed a different aspect. For Beulah on her good behavior was her star boarder and decidedly a financial asset. Madam, brought face to face with this issue, became angry. And

«256» 

SPORTING LADY 

made one of her few false moves. She used restraint. She locked her in.

It seemed to bring the girl to time. Immediately she became docile, returned to the parlours, re-established confidence in her motives. And at the first opportunity disappeared.

Madam accepted her defeat with a shrug of the shoulders and dismissed Beulah from mind as well as from ledger.

Then months later, on one of her busiest nights, a group of religious exhorters established itself before her door. Their leader was Beulah-lovelier than ever in her plain uniform and bonnet-Beulah who, with a fanatical light in her gray eyes, shrilled out a brazen and venomous attack upon the house and its keeper: "Behind those curtained windows, oh Brothers and

Sisters, under gleaming lights and devilishly devised gaiety, lies a nest of Hell itself transplanted to our city. 

There young and innocent girls have been lured into this Satan's Den and there held prisoners, to pander to the horrible appetites of brutes who pass for men. There your sister, and your daughter, may find their way to be defiled and debauched and lost to decency forever. "I do not speak from hearsay. I myself was an inmate of that bawdy house until I saw the light and God in his infinite mercy guided me forth, although restraining hands would hold me prisoner. Led me forth to cry aloud the woes and the wrongs of those poor girls whose desperate plight a deaf and blind city ignores—a city that holds out fat greasy hands for tribute, earned by the defiling of the precious bodies of innocent maidenhood. "Are you going to allow these horrors to continue? 

Are you not your sister's keeper? In the name of our

SPORTING LADY 

< 257 » Father and of the gentle Jesus who died that all might be purified, I call upon you, men of Kansas City, to batter down those doors, break in those windows-storm this citadel of sin and corruption, and release those poor frail creatures, selling their bodies to appease a woman's avarice; to run from our fair city this harlot who is lining her pockets with gold at the expense of our women. Rise, men, and drive her out-her and all of her kind!"

Oh, it was nasty—it was horrible-it was untrue. Of course, the girl's cracked brain was intoxicated with hallucinations passing for religion. Still, it was bad business, and exceedingly uncomfortable. For three nights Beulah harangued before Madam moved to have her restrained. That was easy, of course; but it was not so easy to erase the sordid impression from her mind. 

Then there were the lesser troubles, the pinpricks. 

Quarreling among the girls; their jealousies and envies to be ruled upon, placated, smoothed out. Order brought about, harmony restored. Drunken men to be tactfully excluded or ejected. Secret drinking among the girls; 

Frankie and Selma learning to sniff snow. Away with them. Beatrice taking for a lover a pick-pocket. Money and baubles missing from the rooms. Suspicion among the girls; crafty eyes watching each other, before detectives were called and confidence restored with the arrest of the culprit. 

And always, constantly, girls calling at the doors, insistent. Shop girls, seamstresses, chambermaids, factory girls-all clamoring for a chance at easy money. Girls who had seen Madam Wardell in her spectacular progress through the town; girls whose vanity overrated their allure, who thought they had only to offer to be gladly welcomed, their feet set trippingly on the prim-

«258» 

SPORTING LADY 

rose path they fain would tread. As if Madam Wardell need call for volunteers, she who could pick and choose. 

But she was always kind to these girls, beating at her doors. Always she painted to them an ugly picture of the life they wished to enter; of its ghastly duties, its body-racking, disease-inviting possibilities. Painted it even grimmer and uglier than it was, than it could possibly be, to their attracted eyes. Tried to send them away with its glamour torn from it, its alleged pleasures shown for what they were. And, in some few cases, succeeding. 

No, the life of a Madam, any Madam, was not all grand entrances and popping corks and dancing feet.

Julia, who had known it during those other experiences, had not realized to what extent she would experience it there on Wyandotte. 

So count the gold and silver and greenbacks each night; gloat over the growing length of the savingsaccount; craftily haggle and bargain for each foot of earth, each pile of brick and mortar entered upon deeds in the court house. It has all been hardly earned, no matter what the ledger totals up. Count savings, and grow hard and cold and impregnable to the thrusts this life challenges. Cease to fret and worry at the cares the gray weary days may bring out. Seek solace in velvets and plumes, the carriages and the dashing appearances abroad; soothe vanity with the whispers and nudges and subtle recognitions accorded out in the city; believe the glamour of the parlours, have faith in the enduring potency of champagne bubbles; forget that men are beasts and women little better; remember only that prosperity smiles broadly, that reputation is growing, that youth still pulses beneath that firm white body. 

And that there is always Mark Creighton.

SPORTING LADY 

«259» 2 Two years of prosperity before the bubble burst. Ample time, business being what it was, to salt away enough for the lean years that were bound to follow the orgy of buying and spending; provided, of course, the collapse did not last too long.

But as time went by, as the 'Eighties gave way to the 'Nineties, and a plucked and prostrate city lay dormant among its follies, even Madam Julia began to worry. 

Room after room she closed, for lack of patronage. The string orchestra gave way to a mechanical piano whose dimes were counted and hoarded as were all the other dimes and quarters straying into the house. Eight girls working, and prices down. As low as a dollar. Beer corks popping instead of champagne. Furniture, carpets, drapes, showing wear and tear, needing refurbishing. 

No carriage now, no colored maid to follow through the stores, arms loaded high with purchases. No purchases, even, in those early days of the 'Nineties. Madam 

Julia had grown crafty and stingy. But not seedy. Never that. Enough remained of former raiment which, with her skilful needle, could be turned and twisted into an approximation of the latest styles, still evolved and paraded in the east.

Nor was she the only one of the red light district who suffered. Annie Carter, since Julia's rise her envious and bitter rival, closed up and departed for Denver. Others left the ill-fated city-to pass on, or to disappear.

Madam Julia was one of the few left to uphold the

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

earlier traditions; and she too, might have sunk had not 

Big Tim come to her aid. For most of her profits had gone into real estate which she was unable to sell, even at a tenth of its former value. "Hold on to it," the Boss had said. "The city is coming back and it will all be valuable some time.” Meanwhile he reduced the rent, even skipping over some months at the bottom of the slump. And somehow she weathered it, held on until the upgrade began and slowly, but quite perceptibly, the town staggered to its feet and began to plod up the hill. 

This dreadful era left its mark on her. More than ever she had come to realize the comfort of money; and, when the reaction finally came, hoarded it greedily. 

Rapaciously. Hard lights glittered in her tiger eyes, hard tones gritted in her former rich voice. And as she turned forty the peroxide bottle was needed, not alone to gild her former dark tresses, but also to mask gray hairs. 

The wrinkles of worry were more difficult to conceal, but she managed. When, one gloomy day of self-revelation before her mirror, she discovered what inroads crows feet were making about her eyes, she sought retirement for a week, and went through the painful ordeal of blistering her skin with a solution of corrosive sublimate audaciously demonstrated and sold by a gorgeously beautiful woman as "Youth Rejuvenator.' ("Peeling" was what its users called the new beauty process.) She emerged with a new complexion, rosy and tender, and consequently with new youth in her heart. 

Thereafter, every year until science discovered newer and better beauty secrets, she repeated the process. دو In 1895, when she filled forty years, the city had definitely emerged from the effects of the boom's collapse, and was the center of a steady, sane growth that

SPORTING LADY 

« 261 » was to continue for years to come. Real estate values had found a normal honest level; the house had been refurbished and, glossy and elegant, held its full quota of girls; the orchestra and the maid had been reinstated and a stylish "trap" companioned the carriage; her bank book once more carried a column of figures. And Irene came back. 3 It was Madam Julia's birthday and three o'clock in the afternoon. A snowy, blizzardy day. There were no "strays," as the girls jokingly called the occasional daytime caller, and Madam had invited all of them into the parlour and had cut a big white birthday cake, ceremoniously carried in by the negro cook. She had opened wine, to the pleased surprise of the girls, well aware of Madam's habitual thrift. "In her own honor," they thought and greedily drank it. The party became gay. 

Madam beamed and grew comradely. Sadie turned on the mechanical piano and performed a skirt dance. Sadie had once been on the stage. Madam unfolded an old newspaper clipping and intoned its contents soulfully, pausing when hilarious laughter interrupted her:

"He's dead, Kate. Yes, I'll tell you everything. But first-could I have a drink? I'm about at the end of my rope." "Why of course, you poor dear. Why didn't I think of that? Like a little champagne?" "No. I'd rather have whiskey. Straight. I'm so half frozen, I need it," apologetically.

She poured a half tumbler and gulped down the fiery stuff. Heaved a big sigh. "God, that's a life saver."

Poured another and held the glass greedily in her hands while Julia watched her curiously, a little sadly. "Yes, now I can talk. I'll tell you the whole lousy business. God, Kate, but you're a good friend. The last one left. If you had turned me away it was the river for me. But you didn't, did you? Well, here goes."

Until his relatives found out about me. It only goes to show that a girl who's been in a house oughtn't never to marry. At least not into a respectable family. But I thought our marriage would be different. He was crazy about me at first, he honestly was. But those people of his were poisonous.

I felt it, and I can tell you it made me pretty blue. But

Madam Julia looked at the lined gaunt face, the graying hair, and her heart ached with compassion. So, except for the grace of God, and Irene here before her, might she have looked. Bread cast upon the water stuff, you know." 5 But Irene proved more of a problem than Julia had anticipated that afternoon when, affected by the reunion, and with sympathy accentuated by her unusual overindulgence, she had so readily taken her in. Perhaps had she not felt such a state of well being herself she would not have been so ready to assume a responsibility which soon proved both irritating and expensive. 

It was Irene's drinking, chiefly. Impossible to hide the stuff from her. By hook or crook she managed to filch it, even though it was under lock and key. Seldom

« 269 » did she come completely from under its influence, and when she did, was such a physical wreck and begged for it so frantically that Julia, her resistance worn down, doled it out to her herself. As medicine. She consulted with Mark Creighton. "Send her away to take the Keely Cure," he advised. "She's past helping herself now." "That will be pretty expensive, won't it?”

Only Mark could do that. 

So Irene went away, unaware of her real destination.

And during her sojourn in the sanatorium Julia pondered what to do with her upon her release. Undoubtedly her days of service were over. What should she be doing here among these girls? After all, Madam Julia had certain standards to maintain, friendship or no friendship. Irene would not be convinced that her professional days were at an end. In her ambition to repay 

Julia's generosity, she might become obnoxious in the house. Something must be arranged to get her away without hurting her. 

At last she got an idea and broached it to Big Tim one month end when they were settling their accounts. "I'm thinking of putting Irene in charge of an assignation house, Tim," she said. "Of course she can't remain here." "I'm glad you understand that, Julia. She doesn't fit into this place. I've been meaning to speak to you about that before she finishes her cure." question is, where shall it be? Haven't you a place you could rent us-cheap?"

They'll be seen going into the entrance leading to the hairdressers, and then can just keep on going up stairs. I don't see why I didn't think about using it for that before."

So when Irene came back, cured and subdued, she was duly installed in the new flat; an unpretentious, but cozy and comfortable one which before too long took its place among those other prosperous secret houses which served as rendezvous for philandering married women, precocious youngsters, and their current amorists.

Julia saw to it that Irene was dyed and peeled, provided her with appropriate clothing, stocked her liquor closet, kept as close check as possible on the receipts, appropriated half the profits every week, and felt that at last she had repaid her debt.

SPORTING LADY 

< 271 » 6 It was rather nice having Irene in town. Someone with whom she could be herself. There were no pretensions between them; they had, it seemed, known one another always. And Madam Julia was a little tired of being alwavs on show and on her guard. Never a moment in which to abandon the grand manner and be just Cora Simmons. Or Kate Black. Irene was commonplace and natural with her; and peaceful and contented to be making at last a comfortable living without much trouble. No real meals to serve; just sandwiches and cold delicacies out of cans and bottles from the delicatessen shop. And a good trade in liquor. Scarcely ever, even, having to see her clients. Arrangements made by telephone. Secret comings and goings. Quiet. Orderliness. Lots of time to take care of herself, manicure her nails, read cheap novels. Contentment, and a growing forgetfulness engendered by physical comfort. Best of all, finished with the craving that had undoubtedly been a factor in her troubles.

During that year, Julia came more and more to drop in on Irene for relaxation and a bit of gossip. Just two old friends, somewhat lonely amidst crowds; chumming together and loosening lips tight shut to everyone else. 

It was one such afternoon, over a glass of Chianti, that they got to talking of Julia's boy. Wondering where he was and what he was like. It started with the discussion of a ball which Mrs Mark Creighton had given for her daughter Florence; Florence who was now one

of the popular girls of the city, her name in every exclusive list of the social columns. Julia had seen her often here and there about town; places where even their different stations permitted an encounter. Sometimes she was with Flora, fashionable and haughty, whose glassy blue eyes never strayed to her sister's face. Her sister, who in her heart was laughing at her, knowing what she knew. She often wondered if Flora had an inkling. Mark had gruffly denied it the few times she had brought up the name of the woman who held, at least, his name. 

Mark didn't like to talk of his family to Julia; not as he had before he married. And Julia, true to her code as well as her promise to Ed, had kept secret their relationship. She still hated Flora and Ed, but it did not extend to Mark's children. Funny, she felt an almost proprietary interest in them, and a real satisfaction in Florence's beauty and popularity; was proud that the slim good-looking Junior gave promise of unusual talent as a violinist. 

And more and more, after years of forgetfulness, she had come to wonder about Wayne's son. As the house on Wyandotte, now organized to run smoothly and almost automatically, left her more time to think; as encroaching years slowed her down and she went back oftener, as older people will, to that time of her heyday among the fleshpots and pleasures that somehow now had lost their power to make her enthuse. Which was only natural, when one glanced ahead and saw fifty years staring at one. Fifty years—and then sixty—and beyond that-old age. With a fortune that was already large and daily increasing, thanks to sound investments and securities. And no one to leave it to; no one to profit by all her years of steady application to business; scrupulous saving-for what? Not Junior and

That's what we women have that those other ones never learned; that's how we hold our men, when they can't." "You've got the blues, Julia. CHAPTER THREE 

1 S MADAM JULIA came down the broad stairs the sound of unusual hilarity greeted her ears. Young laughter, male voices raised in some school chorus. "Who are they?" she asked the doorman. "A crowd of law students," she replied. "They blew in half an hour ago from some banquet for the graduates. Some of them are pretty well lit up already." "Humph! A beer crowd." "Yes, Madam; I reckon so."

The noise ceased as he threw open the doors, and with the stately tread long habit had made second nature, she swept into the parlours, smiling graciously, making the rounds of the various groups, stopping to speak with a favored few. The rooms were well filled and the college 1

«» 

SPORTING LADY 

boys apparently unlikely to become obnoxious despite their high spirits. Some sat at tables with the girls, others were on the dance floor. She passed through the cardroom, crossed the ballroom, and paused to speak with the bartender.

It was then that she saw him. She had been right, she would have recognized him anywhere. For it was Wayne 

Hendrix come back to life. The same tall lithe grace, dusky skin and full red lips; the same bold brilliant black eyes looking down half mocking at his dancing partner. Only Wayne's mustache was missing, and in its place were neatly trimmed sideburns that lent a rakish look to his handsome face. 

Madam Julia's heart missed a beat and her face under its painted mask grew pale, her hands trembled. He was so, so like As she leaned heavily against the bar the man behind it eyed her with concern. "What's the matter, Madam? Are you ill?" "Nothing. I'm all right." "Won't you have a glass of sherry? Do you good." "No. I'm quite all right, I said. Who is that young man? The dark one dancing with Jeanette?" "I don't know, Madam. He's never been here before. 

One of those students."

She sat alone and watched him. The ghost of Wayne, young, unchanged; not grown old and gray and wrinkled as he must have, had he lived. Wayne as she had known him in those dead years so long ago. Looking down into a girl's eyes with the same half mocking, half challenging gaze with which Wayne had looked into her own. Youth, unstained by vice, untouched by coarse contacts. Something clean and wholesome emanating from him-from this son of a desperate outlaw and the madam of a sporting house. Not for a moment

SPORTING LADY 

« 277 » did she doubt that he was her son. Even without the astonishing resemblance, she felt she would have known him; because of that queer feeling within her; and the magnetic current that drew her gaze to him. The same sort of magnetism that had vibrated from his father. 

He must have felt it, too, for his eyes sought hers, held them. He whispered to Jeanette, smiling fatuously up into his face. Suddenly Julia wanted to tear him from the girl, from the contaminating touch of her soft, soiled hands.

Afterwards, when they left the floor and came into the drinking-card room in search of a table, she beckoned to Jeanette, inviting them to sit beside her. "You are a stranger here?" "Well, yes and no. I have never been in your place before, if that's what you mean." "I know you haven't been here, or I would have remembered you. But Kansas City? Do you live here?” "Only since attending law school. I was raised on a farm in southern Kansas.' 

"Oh, so your parents are farmers?" "Were. Both are dead now. And they weren't really my parents. You see, I was an adopted child. My own died when I was a baby."

Funny to be talking like this to the notorious Madam Wardell. But there was something nice about her-sympathetic. She seemed genuinely interested; her eyes searching his face, her painted lips smiling kindly. Not a bit like he had imagined her to be from what he had heard about her. Why, she seemed really human; nicer even than the girl, Jeanette, squirming impatiently beside her. This was a real adventure, in a life that held few. A life of farm drudgery, and striving for an edu-

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

cation in the rural schools and years of day and night application to law books. 

He found himself telling all about it, after Madam had got rid of Jeanette. Perhaps it was the wine she had ordered. He had never drunk any before. And then, this big blonde woman seemed to have a way of drawing one out, making one talk. Just a question now and then, a kindly understanding, and he was telling her about his youth, and his studies and ambitions. Nothing in her attitude that suggested her peculiar calling, nothing that put him on his guard. Just like any other middleaged handsome woman; more like a real motherly interest. That was it, she was motherly. Suddenly he told her so. And wondered at such boldness. Of course, a man didn't say such thing to sporting ladies. And he added hastily, a little shyly: "Please don't misunderstand me. I meant it as a compliment." "And I'm taking it as a compliment." Amused. Not angry. "I admire nice clean-cut young men, like you are. So I'm really going to be motherly and tell you something for your own good. Don't come to these places. To mine, or to any other of the kind. You are too nice. Always try to be that—just nice. Keep away from girls like Jeanette-like all these girls. They are not meant for boys like you are. It's the restless, disillusioned married men mostly who are welcome here. 

You can't mix a career and this sort of life. And you are going to make a career, aren't you?" anxiously. "That's what I've been working towards all my life. 

It's the only thing I really care about. You see, I owe it to my foster parents as well as to myself. They worked and saved all their lives just to give me a chance. It's the

SPORTING LADY 

« 279 » little bit of money they hoarded up that has enabled me to go through law school here. And please believe me when I say I've never been in a house before. I only came tonight because the other fellows insisted and you know how it is one doesn't want to seem a prude. I hadn't any intentions except to see what it was like. 

They said they told me"-he was embarrassed-"that your place was something like a club; that one could spend a little on drinks and be entertained and that it needn't go any further. One needn't, I mean. Of course, your place wouldn't make much off of fellows like me; and I wouldn't expect to be here often." "Dear boy, come here as often as you like. I shall always be delighted to see you; provided—you only come to see the old Madam and steer clear of her girls. Just keep thinking of me like you said-motherly. And if you need anything—influence—or find yourself in difficulties promise you'll come to me first. I'm genuinely interested in that career of yours-and if I can help, in any way -

Had she been too soft with him? Maudlin?

Lying in her bed that night, unable to sleep, she had gone over and over the conversation. She had tried while with him to hold herself back, had tried to keep to the role of Madam Julia. But looking there at Wayne's son, her son, she had had to check herself up time and again. Something in her heart had seemed to melt; had

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

made it difficult to be the self she had been all these years; had made her feel again like Cora Simmons. 

What did he think? Or did he? The wine had befuddled him; he had talked to her openly and more directly than any man had ever talked to her before. Please

God that when his mind cleared he would not feel ashamed or sorry; that it would seem all right to him, and that he would come back again and grow to look on her as a friend. And that he would never, never find out who she really was. But then, he never could. No one in the whole world knew that except herself; or ever would. No one should ever hurt him by publishing the truth. And she would help him. Big Tim owed her something for all the money she had made for him. She had never asked any favors of him before, but she was not above asking for this-son. She had been a vile mother. Lying there, she called herself names for the years of neglect. And, lying there, she knew this sudden sentiment was because he was what he was-smart, decent, handsome; one to be proud of—as Mark was proud of Junior and Florence.

Already she was peering into the future, seeing him marching along to achievement, furnishing her with a new interest in life. Something to live and work for; someone who was her secret own, as no other male had ever been; someone to help keep clean and fine as neither she nor Wayne had been; someone to refute the old belief in heredity; and someone, part of her very flesh and bone, to leave behind when she should at last shuffle off. Yes, part of her would go on living. It was an exciting thought. Stimulating. Made life worth going on with. "You're a dotty old woman, Julia," she said aloud in the darkness; and chuckled happily.

SPORTING LADY 

« 281 » 3 "Big Tim" Conners looked at her slyly, a faint ironic smile curling under his drooping white mustache. "What's this, Julia? Getting in your dotage? You know what it means when you women start in on young fellows."

Her yellow eyes flared, but she bit her lip and quelled the anger in her breast. Too much at stake to rail back at him. Just keep her temper and convince him of her sincerity. "I suppose you have a right to say that," she said quietly. "You men have a right to think anything about us. But, Tim, I hope God strikes me dead here before you if I'm not on the level with this. Now listen to me and don't interrupt until I'm through. "We've been partners now for nearly twenty years and in all that time I've been open and aboveboard with you. Twenty years is a long time, Tim, and we've grown to know each other pretty well; and to trust each other. And in all this time I've never asked a real favor of you. How many other friends of yours can say that? You've been passing out your favors to hundreds who do not deserve them as much as I do. But 

I'm not that kind; I pay for what I get. However, this is something that I can't buy. It's something you've got to give me of your own free will-if you decide to. "It's going to mean an awful lot to me―more than I can explain. I'm not telling you anything and you're not asking, understand? I couldn't tell you if you did.

Because I don't know. I only know that his name is

John Marsh; that he is a fine clean youth, a country boy who worked his way through school, whose parents were poor farmers, and who has somehow aroused my interest until I'm willing to go a long ways to help him along. Isn't that enough to know, Tim? Aren't you willing to let it go at that and to grant me the first favor I've ever asked of you?" "You're right, Julia. I was only teasing you. You've got a big heart—one of the biggest. Only-don't let it become too expansive. At least I can give you that word of warning." "Listen, Tim. I can see you're going to do it; and before you tell me so I want you to understand that he is never to know that I've had a hand in it. He wouldn't understand; he's that kind. Perhaps he wouldn't even accept it if he knew I was back of it. I'm only a sporting woman to him-the sort of woman he'd be ashamed to accept favors from. Say, listen, I don't fool myself. I know my place, and I haven't any wish to be anything but what I am. But I know that if it should get noised about that he got in with you through me that he'd be ruined socially. I don't know him, Tim—I never saw him but this once and it isn't likely I'll ever meet him, personally, again. I shan't try to, at any rate. Any satisfaction I get out of the whole thing will be in watching him succeed, and in knowing deep down in me that I had something to do with giving him his chance. I know you think it's all very queer; but just keep your thoughts to yourself, Tim, and don't be too curious." "All right, Julia. Your secret's your own and I'm not going to pry into it. 'Snuff for me that you want me to do it; and if the lad is what you say and makes good, I'll probably be glad on my own account. I can always

«283» use good men. But you can't keep me from my own conjectures, old girl." "Conjecture all you want to—just so you keep them to yourself. So you'll do it? Gosh, Tim, you're a good scout. When? Right away? Tomorrow?" "Perhaps. I'll have him down and have a talk with him. And as soon as I can make a place I'll do it. There now, that's all right. Glad to do it for you, Julia. I only hope your judgment is good." "Isn't it always, when it concerns men? Oh, I know 'em, Tim. I ought to. You can take my word—he won't disappoint you."

But in her most extravagant fancies Julia did not foresee whither Big Tim's patronage was to lead John Marsh.

Her only part had been in starting his footsteps in that direction. That those eager feet would tramp their way to a culmination as astonishing as it was satisfactory to her, never entered into her calculations at that time. Nor could she have pointed the way, or paved it for him, much as she might have delighted in doing so. No, it was Fate and fortune, and his own rampant ambition that was to bring to her late years such satisfaction and pride and, better even than those, a sure revenge, sweet, even though it must be tasted in secret. Something to mull and gloat over in the years when old age crept up on her, bringing little else to hold her interest in the life that was wearing her down. Surprise after surprise came to her about the astonishing John Marsh, through

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

the columns of the newspapers and inside information wheedled from Big Tim. Delightful gifts for her spirits that gave a tickling tenderness to her heart and brought suppressed laughter to her closed lips.

She was herself astonished at this tardily aroused mother love. If that was it. If it wasn't largely composed of pride and vanity. She talked to herself about it, since only to herself could she whisper the truth. "It's almost like Wayne was alive again. Only finer and smarter and honester and much more respectable. 

And Wayne and I must have had some fine qualities which we didn't know about or else, how could we have had such a son? And by some freak of nature John got only the good out of us; all the meannesses and weaknesses and sins we kept for ourselves and gave him only our best."

She asked herself: Had he turned up on the criminal dock, or had been found creeping furtively through night shadows on devious dark missions such as she well knew and could condone in others, would she have flared to this absorbing interest in her son? She would not, she frankly admitted. But why quibble about that? Her son had been born to her full-grown one night in spring, beneath bright lights and to the accompaniment of popping champagne corks. And she would be good to him. Never should he know, however much she might desire him to know. Always would she stand in the shadows behind him, serving him if she could; and never by word or deed allowing hurt or shame to come to him through her. "And now it won't be so horrible growing old, knowing part of me is going to keep on living, yes, and making me proud of him, even after I'm dead and gone."

SPORTING LADY 

« 285 5 

John Marsh had been in Edward Simmons' office barely two years when he was taken into full partnership. Big Tim told her of it when the promotion was only impending. "A bright young man, that," he had said. "He's going far, Julia. We intend to push him politically. Small jobs at first. State's senator, attorney-general; if he makes good, to the House or the Senate."

Congressman-senator! Fine-in time. But just now it was enough that he was in Ed's office; received by the haughty Mrs Simmons; attending the same social functions with Junior and Florence. Perhaps dancing with Florence, gazing down into her eyes with that challenging look he had given to Jeanette. Beautiful popular Florence, daughter of the snippy Mrs Creighton, belle of the younger crowd, in the arms of the bastard son of a train robber and a sporting madam. That was funny. Excruciatingly funny. To be enjoyed with deep secret laughter. And if it went even further? What ifbut no. That would be too good to come true. Too, too rich a gift for the gods to bestow. And yet, she found herself pulling for it, concentrating her thought strenuously, tempestuously upon it; willing it with all her strength even though it seemed too impossible to occur. 

But was it?

She got the first inkling from Mark Creighton- Creighton, who seldom talked of his family. In one of his expansive moods. He knew she admired Florence;

SPORTING LADY 

she had enlarged upon that whenever given an opportunity. And admiration for his favorite child, even coming from Madam Wardell, was nice. At least she had good taste. "I'm afraid I'm going to lose my girl," he had said. "Yes?" she prompted. Not too eagerly; not too curiously; don't scare him from the subject. "Yes. She's been popular with them all and has played no favorites. But now, even father's eyes can see the signs." "Is it someone you like? Would you approve of the match?" "Yes, I guess I would. As much as I could approve of anyone for Florence. He's fine through and through. 

No fortune, but a brilliant future. Yes, I must say that 

I approve of him in every way." 

Quietly now. Don't hurry. Be only mildly interested. "Who is it? Do I know him?" 

Mark threw back his head and laughed, a hearty guffaw. "No, you don't. He's not the kind who comes here. 

That's one of my reasons for approving. Now, old girl, I don't mean that like you take it. But you know fathers are most particular about the men who want to marry their daughters-especially such fathers as I am. No, you've never seen John Marsh-at least, not here."

So! It was true then. The unbelievable had happened. 

Turn away your head, lower your eyes, so that the joy and triumph may not show. Speak quietly, with just sufficient interest. "And her mother? What does she say?" "Well, I think Flora is almost in love with him herself. 

At any rate, knowing her little ways and tricks, I'd say she's been managing the whole affair most capably."

What a joke on Flora! What a joke on Mark! And what a beautiful, gratifying joke on Julia Wardell! 6 It all came to pass smoothly and conventionally. No malicious quirk of fate interrupted or hindered the political and romantic progress of John Marsh. No foggy breath of suspicion even at the last moment dimmed the star of his future. One of those men favored by fortune backed up by ability and personality.

At the next election he had gone to the state legislature, the natural procedure for the man sponsored by Big Tim, the boss who, though aging, still held the reins firmly. And after that, after a few more months had slipped away, the announcement of the engagement and the beginning of the round of pre-nuptial parties; pleasant reading, to be sandwiched between her own steady routine; to creep into bed with in the early morning hours and gloat over.

It was amusing to dramatize scenes in which she confronted Ed and Flora (yes, and Creighton, sometimes irritating in his unconscious superiority) with the truth.

Not that she ever would, really; but it was interesting to pretend, and to imagine the reactions of all of themall of those people so near to her and yet so far removed. 

And she, alone, holding all their secrets-she alone knowing everything about everyone. 

And she alone absent from the wedding. But though they could not know it, the city's most notorious woman was present-in spirit. At the thronged church when

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

the darkly handsome groom and his lovely golden bride, swathed in white satin and tulle, had stood before the lily-laden altar; had intoned their clear responses; had swept triumphant through the swelling peals of the wedding march, down the aisle and away to the flowerbedecked home on the hill; to congratulations and health-drinking and gay chatter and brittle laughter.

She had slipped unseen down the stone steps and into their very carriage; and onto the southward-pointing express; yes, spirit that she was, into the very nuptial chamber itself. And after that the months again moved smoothly, and spring passed, and summer bloomed, and autumn wailed, and winter whitened all the earth. And she visited with

Irene and graced her own parlours, herding and shifting and ruling the girls; and oftener and oftener, as habit and increasing loneliness drew him there, sat with Mark Creighton in her own rooms, relaxed and quiet. 

For Flora was away. In Europe at last. With Junior, studying the violin in Berlin. Flora, dancing at diplomatic balls, attending concerts and musical teas; while

Creighton, by preference, sat in Julia's rooms with her, or awaiting her, in slippered ease; pipe in hand, a highball at his side, newspapers spread on knees; just sitting content, paunchier and quieter from increasing years, and with brain beginning to dull. Lazier. Quieted by the sedative quality of her presence, her noiseless comings

SPORTING LADY 

« 289 » and goings, her low monotonous voice that sometimes lulled him to nodding. Happy and at home as he had never been in the big house on the hill with its disturbing social functions, its mandatory mistress, its unrest and unquiet and scrambling hither and yon to meet the cluttered engagements of the crowded hours. 

It was here that he was the night Florence's son was born; it was here he returned to tell Julia about it. "A dark little rascal, healthy and sound. The image of John."

It was here that he was one night six months later. "Big Tim" was having a party. Local politicians, an attorney-general, the Mayor. Madam Julia was kept late in the parlours. It was a wine-drinking crowd; an important one, for which entertainment must be speeded up. She felt the strain of it; of keeping an eagle eye on the girls, of keeping a tired face laughing at vacuous jokes, of flattering and beaming and trying to appear brilliant and interested. Much nicer to sit under the rose-colored lights across in her rooms with Mark. Still waiting there for her. Waiting and nodding and sipping and reading through the hours she was compelled to liven these parlours and keep things moving for these men who no longer meant entertainment or pleasure for her. "Getting old," she thought grimly. "Arteries are hardening. Mine and Mark's. Have to step down and out soon. Can't stand the pace. Want nothing more than to be left alone; grow old in peace. If we could

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

just go away somewhere together a long ways offaway from all who belong to us, who ever knew us. Forget our looks and get gray and wrinkled, and just be comfortable and quiet. Together."

Instead this. This glare and noise; irritating scraping of ragtime, shuffling of feet. Men growing drunker and more maudlin. No interference with this crowd of Big 

Tim's. The girls knew it; were getting out of bounds. 

Vulgarity, seldom countenanced. Simpering bestial looks on red, vein-streaked faces. Mamie attempting the cancan. This won't do. Big Tim must be warned; get the men upstairs or out. It didn't matter which, just so the crowd thinned and she could leave. 

Two o'clock, and all gone except Big Tim and two or three still talking affairs. She entered her room wearily, tossed her feather fan aside. Mark still sat, in the chair he always used, his head dropped forward. "Poor dear," she said, "why did you wait? I couldn't get away any sooner." He didn't answer and she went to him, ran her hand through his hair the way he liked her to rumple it. The scalp was cold. As cold as her heart stopping painfully. On her knees beside the slumped figure, looking into the face-purplish, with popping, staring eyes. "Oh my God! Mark!" But even as she shook the stiff form she knew that it was useless. 

Here, in this house! If it ever got out! Shame for 

John and Florence. Shame for the grandchild—her

SPORTING LADY 

< 291 » grandchild, too. What to do. Stop shaking. Think. A long drink, from the mouth of the bottle beside him. 

Start her brain working. There, that was better. The ague, the fright, were passing. Big Tim was still in the parlours. Get him. Have it taken out of the house before it was discovered.

Big Tim, his face serious, looking down at the humped figure. "I'll get him away, Julia. Sit down, be quiet, don't worry. I'll have Frank order a carriage. I'll get him home, and say that he was with me, at my house."

Ten minutes alone with him. Ten minutes in which to say goodbye to all the joys they had shared, all the dreams that might have come to pass. Ten short minutes to end the companionship of years. So short a time, and yet she could not stir; could only sit there staring at the grotesque, slumped figure, and the half-concealed purple face. Still sat there while Big Tim and Frank raised it and, half carrying, half dragging it, got it through the door that slammed behind them.

CHAPTER FOUR 

1 T WAS lonely after that.

So many years she had had 

I'Mark. 

"What you need," said Irene, "is to find some young and attractive man who can give you back your youth.

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

Mark was all right, but he sure got you running down hill. Why, these past few years you've been like a settled old grandmother. All you needed was a chimney corner and knitting needles."

Irene who, these last months, was displaying a hectic restlessness that passed for gaiety, and parading a stalwart young prize-fighter tired of the game and looking for softer living; Irene, assuming playful manners and artful coquetries that ill became her age. "What I need," said Julia, "is to renovate the house and take more interest in my business. I'll buy out Big 

Tim and go it alone." 2 Years marching steadily. Security and prosperity and contentment. The house all her own. No partnership now that allowed Big Tim to grunt his disapproval; 

Big Tim grown grouchy and palsied with his eighty years of service to his city; Big Tim, whose trembling fingers were slowly but surely relaxing their hold on the reins he had held so long. No more need of Big Tim's influence to smooth away the obstacles that had been reared to impede her earlier years in the house. She herself a power in her particular field; her own name now one to be reckoned with in the underworld where she reigned. She, Julia Wardell, become an institution which the city accepted as inevitable, and to which it pointed with dubious pride. 

Wealth piling up. Securities. Railroad and mining

SPORTING LADY 

« 293 » and industrial stocks. No real estate now. Just dividends to cash and exchange for other stocks that would bring in more dividends to cash. And always the progress of 

John Marsh to watch with pride and gloating; chance glimpses of grandchildren, of Florence. 

And then Big Tim died. She read the spreading headlines and felt a little sad, but not unconsolable. After all, he was an old, old man. And she no longer needed his help. Neither she, nor John Marsh. 3o But Julia miscalculated the sum of grief accruing to Big Tim's death. For with his passing came Change. An era was about to end. A smug and self-righteous city was being aroused to the sin that dwelt within its borders; and soon, very soon, after his dead hands relaxed their hold, the red light district was to blink out-its mechanical pianos stilled, its habitants scattered far and wide.

A vice commission was named to investigate the segregated section with a view to closing it. The announcement brought together in shared fear those who controlled the sporting houses. Forgotten for the moment their envy of their most prosperous and notorious colleague. One and all they agreed that Madame Wardell was the person to appear before the Commission and present their case.

She did her best. Fear of threatening calamity lent eloquence to her tongue. Pale, genteel, dressed quietly in

< » 

SPORTING LADY 

black, she faced her accusers and pleaded the cause. She told them that houses of the type she conducted were places of last resort for many unfortunate girls, turned from home because of youthful error, bewildered, frenzied, ready to take their lives had they not found sanctuary and kindness and a new will to live in these havens now threatened with being closed to them. She cited cases she had known, girls of her own, who, finding all other doors barred, had found contentment with her and had eventually gone out from her place into homes of their own; married, raised families, lived down their pasts. "Out on your South Side, peaceful and secure now, are many of my own girls who would have met a suicide's death in the river had there not been such havens to welcome them. You respectable people kicked them out and we took them in." She seemed to impress them by her sincerity.

She warned the gravely listening men of danger if the houses were emptied and their inmates scattered to every part of the city; warned of disease that threatened their youth should these women ply their trade (as they undoubtedly would) unguarded and without benefit of medical surveillance. Rising in excitement, her yellow eyes blazing, she exclaimed: "There will always be women of my kind. We have trod this earth since the beginning; we follow the oldest profession in the world and we fill an age-old need. As well try to make a river run upstream as attempt to stamp out this trade which is the demand of and the answer to you men, sitting there in judgment upon us. 

It has always been among you; it always will be. The question is, shall it remain within your control, or shall

SPORTING LADY 

« 295 » it spread out over the city to flourish secretly and malignantly, endangering your son, and yours-and yours. Gentlemen, the answer is up to you; and the consequences be upon your heads." She turned wearily away, a tired woman. 4 Out in the spotlight of public print after all the hidden secret years of her life; her words blazoned forth in headlines, her name on every tongue. Mrs Edward Simmons with sneering condemnations read the account to her husband and demanded that he use his influence with the Commission to have such hussies driven from their glorious city; her husband who, pale and frowning, automatically agreed with her, and attempted to still the fear caused by this raising of a ghost. 

Mrs Flora Creighton out in the Country Club district read it at her breakfast and tore the sheet to ribbons, retired to her boudoir in hysterics, to her bed with smelling salts and a sick headache. 

Mr John Marsh, home from Washington after the adjournment of Congress, mused back over the years to a night in Madam Julia Wardell's house; recalled the woman who had this day made the headlines; a woman whose image was dimmed by the flight of years. "To think I was once in her house," he mused. "I- 

John Marsh. And possibly should have gone again if she hadn't talked to me as she did. As I recall her she was rather nice. Motherly."

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

5 But Madam Wardell's plea was unheeded. The edict went forth. The segregated district was to be closed and a police ban put upon it.

So one cold winter's night the houses of sin shuttered to gloom their gay windows. Stealthy figures emerged from closed portals and scattered out over the city, east, west, south; became furtive neighbors of rich and poor, good and bad; but continued to walk the streets and to open their doors to equally secretive visitors. Continued to ply their trade. 

And when the Negro orchestras and mechanical pianos had blared forth their requiems; when the red lights had winked their last and shut their wary little eyes forever, Madam Julia Wardell turned the key and fastened the chains across the door of the house. "I'm through," she said aloud to empty parlours, to deserted bar, to surprised and uncomprehending lights. "Through and finished at sixty. Well, it was good while it lasted. And it didn't last too long. There's still time to turn over a new leaf-turn respectable. Twenty years of respectability ahead of me. And I owe them to John. 

I'm not sorry. Not really. The mother of a great man should be careful." 

It was the end of an epoch. And the end of "Madam” Wardell.

BOOK IV CHAPTER ONE 

1 'HE lounge of the second-rate hotel was deserted. 

Through open doors beyond the short flight of steps the drenching sun of the Azure Coast blinded with its brilliance, but did not penetrate into the dim cavern of worn rugs, scraped wicker furniture, long tables piled with international newspapers and magazines; did not dispel the mustiness that blended with breakfast coffee and last night's table d'hôte. year of 1930 

The guests who in this still pos- sessed means to follow the summer had scattered out into it to the Promenade, the tennis courts. Or to the Casino.

With the clicking of the door of the ascenseur, the portier came to attention behind his little desk, selected from its cubicle a blue envelope, and approaching the woman who emerged from the dimness, bowed and presented it. "Bon jour, Madam Wardell. A message for Madam.

SPORTING LADY 

2 79 She carried the envelope with her through the door and out into the sunshine. The hand that held it trembled and her knees were shaking so that the trail she made wove from side to side as it led her to the sea. She sank into a chair and fumbled in her purse for a small coin, bestowing with it a mechanical smile upon the fat gray woman who approached. Her kind. But not her class.

A cocotte. Worn out in service, but with old age secure through this chair-renting concession. They did better for these women in France. 

For a time she sat looking out across the blue Mediterranean, waiting for the beating heart, the trembling of the hands to subside. Then she tore the blue envelope and ran her blinking red-rimmed eyes along the black lines.

It had come. That which had threatened these many weeks. Her fortune, her carefully earned, carefully hoarded, secretly gloated over fortune, wiped out in the debacle that had finally descended also upon her own country. The points of her shares-railroad, utilities, mining, oil, reaching the vanishing point. No more dividends to pave a golden way; no second mortgage to be secured on the house. No more money forthcoming for even current expenses. No more money! It could not be possible. She, Julia Wardell, alone in a foreign land, possessed only of the cash she carried on her person! 

She did not need to count it. Well she knew how

SPORTING LADY 

« 299 » much it was, from the many times she had thumbed the notes alone in her room, during these past weeks after those first warnings from America had reached her. 

With strict economy it would take her back home, back to the middle western city on the river where dwelt 

John Marsh and his family. Her grandchildren. At any rate there was a good county farm there.-At the thought a shudder ran over her. The poor-house. Never for Julia Wardell! 3 She sat stunned and numb with the enormity of the catastrophe. A blank wall rearing before her shut out the future, into which she could not peer, for which she seemed unable to plan. Only the past rose up, and memory ran back and picked out its highlights. So many, many years she had wandered; so many years dwelt a stranger among strangers. Why? What had it brought her? Why was she here?

With eyes fixed on the blue horizon of the sea she brooded over the interim that bridged the then and now: 

A life of vagabondage. Passing along the months and years to pile atop the many that had gone before. An ocean rolling between her and the patiently awaiting house; an ocean surging between her and John Marsh. 

From spa to spa, from casino to casino the wanderlust had led her. Filled with a restlessness that could satiate itself only in crowds, excitement, movement. And at the gaming tables. Playing craftily, with grim hard mouth

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

and avaricious eyes. Working out a system. One of many such women living consumingly through the night, sleeping behind darkened windows while the sun rode out the day.

Enforced vacations, when a muddled brain refused to answer to the whip, and queer hallucinations intruded between her weary eyes and the green baize of the tables. 

Rubbing up casual acquaintances as casually dropped when she moved on to fresh adventures. Tarnished women of her own kind, adventurers, gigolos, international gamblers. Never a lack of companionship so long as fine raiment covered her and gold jingled in her pocket.

But now she knew, and had known many years, that never could she pass for the lady she had envisioned when she had closed the house and sailed for foreign parts. Despite the arrogant carriage of her head, the exquisite robing of the still erect old body, the aura of the parlours hovered round her. The trademark of her long life was irrevocably stamped upon her. 

And now even such companionship as her caste permitted was denied her. Since her dividends had ceased and necessity had forced her to a sordid life of strict economy and self-denial. Those gay birds of passage with whom she had fluttered had deserted at the first croakings of disaster. Naturally.

But would it be any different over there in her own country? Who was there to greet her? Not Irene. Even before the ban upon the house Irene had started careening downward. Deserted by the young prize-fighter and caught up by dope. She had tried again to help her. But 

Irene had changed; had grown surly and combative; had refused her admittance when she had called. It had

SPORTING LADY 

< 301 » been a relief when she had vanished. She was doubtless dead by now. Like Ed and Flora. While she was still having the Star mailed to her she had read of their passing during the influenza epidemic. No one left but 

John Marsh, senator these many years. The Paris Herald spoke of him as presidential timber. Her son. President of the United States, perhaps. Yes, it was best that she stay away, for fear-for fear- "My mind is sometimes funny. Sometimes I'm not quite responsible. I might, just from pride, do something frightful."

The old cocotte stood beside her, muttered, held forth her hand. Another hour was beginning. She dropped her coin, rose to her feet, squared her shoulders and turned toward the Casino. "I'll stake it all, win or lose," she muttered. "If the worst comes, there's always the water." 4 A week of fierce, rapacious play, with luck perched on her shoulder, then suddenly deserting. Losing, losing, until fear stalked beside her during the swift unheeded hours of the night and lay her bedfellow through the sundrenched day that penetrated even through the dark hangings at her window.

When despair and consumed vitality seemed about to take their toll, when she swayed in her chair and the hands that grasped the counters trembled as with palsy, her luck turned again and a new thread of vitality spun

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

through her weary flesh. Ghastly under the red spots of rouge, with blinking inflamed eyes and bitter covetous mouth, she raked in the chips with greedy hands, mentally calculating. Through the night and far into the day the play went on; but when, after sweeping the counters into her capacious bag, she staggered from her place to the cashier and exchanged them for bundles of crisp thousand franc notes, almost without warning the agonizing pain struck and she crumpled to the floor an insensate mass of flesh. 5 At the hospital, inert between intervals of pain. Disturbing, impossible insinuations by consulting physicians, only partly grasped through the queer clouding of her brain. And then a "cure." Baths and waters and massage, strict adherence to diet. The flesh departing from the massive frame leaving a gaunt and grotesque skeleton upon which her clothes hung in limp eloquent folds scarcely more sagging than the folds of fleshless skin upon her once plump face.

But she was much, much better; was getting well fast. The pain had diminished until she was scarcely aware of it, so long as she followed her diet. And since the racking pain had ceased-well, one's face could always be lifted. And slenderness was the vogue.

With returning strength came nostalgia. A strange longing for the house on Wyandotte with its shuttered windows and barred door; one ever growing desire to

SPORTING LADY 

« 303 » see lights blaze again from the cut glass chandeliers, to behold once more those rooms thronged by men of the town, her old clientele; to hear the pop of champagne corks and the shuffle of feet over the shining hardwood of the dance floor. To go home, while she still had funds and drive the ghosts from the rooms of which she had been so proud. To meet there a last time with 

John Marsh. And then rest and wait. If what the doctors surmised was true, it would not be too long. But first she must make certain; must consult the specialist in 

Paris whom they had recommended. But not looking like this. Not with these folds of wrinkles draping her cheeks, hindering her vision. He should not be influenced by appearances even before he examined her. CO The great specialist tapped pencil point on pad and under his heavy brows looked curiously at the woman who sat opposite him. An extraordinary, abnormal specimen, unbelievably tall, big-boned, gaunt. Gowned by one of the great couturiers, undoubtedly; hatted by an artist. 

Staring old topaz eyes in the countenance of a young woman. Skin pulled tight over the bony structure of the face, skin pulled up from the eye sockets, giving a malevolent expression to the otherwise expressionless visage the stolid, tight mask of a workmanlike job gone slightly awry. Like the brain it concealed. Yes, undoubtedly the brain, too, was twisted. 

She stared back, under his scrutiny, unblinking, un-

responsive. He sighed, dropped his eyes to the unintelligible signs on the pad before him. Poor devil! "Madam Wardell, how old are you?" 

The topaz eyes blinked. "I told you before. Thirty-five." 

He shook his head impatiently. "That is what you told me, yes. Of course I know better. Face lifting does not fool a doctor. How old are you in years-sixty? Sixty-five?" "Seventy-five." Triumph in the glittering eyes. "And 

I'm good for ten or fifteen years yet. Am I, Doctor? 

That's what I'm here to learn; not to discuss my age. 

What did the tests show-what have you found out?"

The voice was monotonous, expressionless, like the face. 

He hesitated, spoke gently: "It's always difficult to tell a patient what I must tell you. I'm afraid your days are numbered, my dear 

Madam Wardell." "Numbered?" the thin painted lips repeated mechanically. "Yes. You have waited too long. If indeed there was ever a chance, had your case been taken in time." "Do you mean-that I'm going to die?" "I'm afraid that is just what I mean. You may linger a year; on the other hand, the end may come in a month or two. The culmination of this malady cannot be definitely determined. I am pained to have to impart such bad news; and doubly sorry that I can hold out no hope whatever. Perhaps-if you had come long ago-but that is purely speculative." "Thank you, Doctor. I'm glad, anyway, to learn the truth. Gives me time to get back to the States, put my house in order, eh? Adieu. No, don't bother. Lovely day, isn't it? I think I shall walk to my hotel. Goodbye."

Day after precious day, walking the decks with a strange new strength. As if walking would bring her to her journey's end quicker. Now, when time had become such a factor in her life. 

Round and round the decks, swept by sea winds, washed by moonlight. Eyes straight ahead. Never a glance at the berugged occupants of steamer chairs following her course with lifted brows, knowing smiles. "Cracked!" "Quite." "Wonder if she thinks that face fools anyone?" "Looks like a mummy with an alabaster mask." "She's queer fish, all right. Gives me the creeps. 

Unnoticing. Unhurt by averted eyes. Unstung by the cruelly thoughtless tongues. Walk, walk, hurry. Only three days more-two-one-to be tolled off in the endless circuit of the decks.

SPORTING LADY CHAPTER TWO 

1 

HUTTERS off the old house on Wyandotte. Flor ists and caterers coming and going; maid and men servants moving about; bustle, orderly confusion. Mirrors shining, cut glass sparkling, dancing floor waxed and slick. Massed blooms banking mantels, tables, cabinets; corners sprouting tall palms; heavy funereal scents creeping forth, enveloping the rooms.

Frank in maroon uniform, directing, inspecting, criticizing; old Frank, grown gray and stooped, his wrinkled, aged face worried and sad, his forehead raised in pathetic lines, as it had been since that day, a week back, when the taxicab stopped at the front door and he had loosed the chains to the stranger who confronted him. 

He had not known her, had looked askance and started to shut her out. Until she spoke. And she had been pleased at his lack of recognition; had mistaken the horror he had quickly controlled for admiration. Admiration! Poor Madam Julia! Poor old dead mistress. This living skeleton, this masked creature secluded there in the suite, was a stranger to him; an automaton moving about, giving orders, with changeless face and colorless voice. A weird creature who called herself Madam Wardell. But she was not his Madam. His Madam had died, off there across the sea, and had sent this intruder to take her place; to arrange this queer reception. Thank

SPORTING LADY 

« 307 » God, it gave him work to do, work to keep his sad old mind off the presence shut up there in Madam Julia's rooms. The presence that did not step across the threshold, but lay across the bed, or sat writing out cards; searching old files for old addresses many of which were surely discontinued long since. Always, when necessity forced his knock, sitting at her desk writing, or lying on the big mahogany bed staring up at the ceiling; coming to with difficulty; focusing with an effort the burning topaz eyes that had once belonged to his mistress; giving terse monotonous orders and answers. 

In that week of preparation she had not once set foot outside the house; had received no one except the hairdresser, who today had entered, smiling and cheerful, had departed silently, with a perplexed look in his staring eyes. Today she had not risen until noon when she had called for champagne. Twice during the afternoon he had brought bottles in iced buckets, while she sat silently observing him.

Not writing today. The desk was closed. He himself had mailed the dozens of square white envelopes. Her preparations were finished, even to the glittering golden dress spread out upon the bed, awaiting her robing. He had glimpsed it when he carried in the last bucket. But she was not showing the effects of what she had consumed; she was just as she had been this entire week. 

Living, breathing, but, somehow, dead. Yes, her work seemed finished; and he had an uncanny feeling that his was nearly finished also.

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

2 But there had been one square envelope which she had commanded him to deliver in person; one which she would not trust to the mails. It was addressed to Senator 

John Marsh, Commerce Building. And, cognizant of the unblemished reputation of the ranking senator from Missouri, his curiosity had been aroused. It would have been further stimulated could he have read the enclosure, flattened between the formal engraving—a tremblingly scratched plea that the honorable senator would grant this request of an old friend who had known him in his youth and had followed his career with great interest; one who begged to clasp his hand this one time in a last farewell. 3 Frank walked through the rooms on his final inspection. 

Through the parlours with their heavy scented flowers; the ballroom where the orchestra was already tuning up; into the bar, presided over by white-jacketed negroes; across to the dining room whose long buffet tables were spread with every delicacy he could procure. Turkeys and chickens carved to preserve their original appearance; lobsters and shrimps; salad in colorful mounds; sandwiches of caviar and smoked salmon and foreign

SPORTING LADY 

« 309 » cheeses; mounds of crimson strawberries resting on green leaves; everything she had meticulously ordered and he had somehow provided. Everything in order. 

He glanced at the clock. Nearly eleven. The guests would soon be arriving. Madam was to enter the parlours at eleven precisely to receive them. He straightened his tie, pulled down his vest, and stationed himself beside the door.

He was standing there when Madam Wardell opened her own and stepped across the name emblazoned upon the floor. Tall, erect, swathed in gold from the undulated hair of her head to the golden slippers peeping beneath the sweeping skirts; from the tawny gold of her eyes to the huge feather fan held open in her glittering gloves. 

Golden topaz upon neck and arms and cascading from her ears. Even the expressionless mask that was her face seemed golden tinted under the rouge that arched her cheeks. "Madam," the old voice quavered and the dazzled eyes sprung tears as the compliments of years came automatically to his lips, "Madam, you're sho' lookin' gorgeous tonight." "Thank you, Frank. As my friends arrive, announce them. It has been so long. I may have forgotten their faces." Twelve, one, two o'clock, and Madam Wardell still sat upright in her high-backed gilded chair awaiting her guests. A hush was over the parlours where the flowers

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

hung their dying heads. In the dining room waitresses stood about whispering, speculating; at the bar the negroes lounged, bored and idle. But erect, listening, suffering, Frank stood guard at the outer door ready to throw it open at the first shuffle on the worn stone step without. Only the musicians stirred to activity through the monotonous hours, renewing their efforts when at each pause the grim lips of the waiting hostess commanded, "Play!" 

But Kansas City had forgotten. From the narrow turgid river now sullen between the teeth of restraining dikes, to the skyscrapers shooting up from streets whose mud she had trod fifty years before, Kansas City, radiating its hundred miles of boulevards out over the old cow pastures, woods and creeks of bygone days had, in the aftermath of the war, burst off its shackles of old traditions and customs and, like every other city in the nation, had erupted into an inflamed metropolis, dance mad, bridge mad, jazz mad.

Seventeen years since the houses were closed; ample time to grow accustomed to the new order of procedure in amatory conquests; ample time for those men who had thronged her rooms to grow aged, inactive; perhaps senile or chair-held. Or dead. 

But Madam Wardell, waiting straight and taut through the empty hours, did not comprehend. To the old sick woman whose old sick mind hovered in the early days of the century, it spelled the end. Oblivion. 

When the hands of the big bronze clock that had ticked forth so many brilliant hours stood at two, and the servitors drooped like the roses with inaction and boredom, she rose at last and with an effort at steadiness crossed the room and closed its doors behind her for the last time.

SPORTING LADY 

« 311 » "Pay them off and dismiss them," she said to Frank. "I have made a mistake; my invitations have carried the wrong date. Send the food to the Helping Hand. 

Bring me champagne. Then lock up." 65 She did not remove the golden robes and ease herself as had been her wont when the night's work was ended. 

Instead, she sat beneath the old piano lamp through whose tattered silk a rosy light fell softly, as it had fallen upon Mark Creighton that night so long ago. Sat brooding and sipping the wine until the gray of dawn smeared dull patches on the long windows; sat numb and staring until a ray of the rising sun shot into the room, sought out and found the tight drawn face, lighted up the ridges of rouge on cheek bones, the yellow parchment skin. She blinked at the sudden golden light; arose and drew the heavy curtains, swayed to the adjoining bedroom. 6 Lights ablaze now. Clustering on the chandeliers in sitting and bedroom, shining from wall brackets and table lamps, flaring forth from the corridors. Going out in a blaze of light. As she had lived.

She sat before the mirror of the great mahogany bu-

« » 

SPORTING LADY 

reau, gazing long and earnestly at the woman reflected there. Touched the tight cheeks with powder, the high cheek bones with rouge. Smoothed the shining cap of strawlike hair, shadowed carefully the tired topaz eyes, sprayed bosom and hands with perfume. Then, leaning forward, she kissed the reflected image. "Goodbye, my dear. It's time we parted. We have been together too long. But you've been good to me, my dear. You earned me a fortune. And we've enjoyed our life together, haven't we? We would never have had it different, would we? If we had it to live all over again we would choose the same path, wouldn't we? For in no other life, being what we were, could we have crowded so much of living, and done the good that we have. Rescued unfortunates and started them off again; helped the down and outers; given pleasure and gaiety and good, good times to so many. And in no other life could we have made so much money. "But there comes an end to everything-even to our companionship, my dear. We are wise enough and brave enough to cut our bond before corruption cuts it for us. Thank you, my dear, for all you have done for me. 

And perhaps we shall come together again. Who knows? 

Who knows anything? Nobody. But it was good while it lasted, wasn't it, Cora Simmons of Clay County?" 

For a moment she stood looking around the rooms where she had spent so many years. Then with firm hand poured the last glass from the white-swathed bottle and carried it to her bedside table. From the drawer she drew forth the container of white powder and carefully opened it. Carefully, also, arranged her skirts modestly about the long lines of her body, settled snugly into the pillows, caressing them for a moment to find greater

SPORTING LADY 

«313 » comfort; marking the place for her head when it should fall back for the last time. For a brief moment, raising upright, she looked at the bubbles, riotously alive in the hollow-stemmed glass. "And even this is as it should be. Going out on the bubbles that have danced for me through all these years." 

Powder emptying upon greedy tongue, bubbles tingling quickly after, splashing over the satin bedspread as the hand that held the glass relaxed and the golden head dropped to the pillows.
